                     Planar Line-Query Decision Trees:
                 Approximation and Strong NP-Completeness


                                             October 13, 2026


                                                  Abstract
          We identify a hidden planar point using only a supplied separating family of line queries.
      For maximum depth and arbitrary-prior expected depth, respectively, we give deterministic
      polynomial approximation factors below 4.464966 and 5.182976, and quasipolynomial (2 + ε)
      approximations for both objectives. The shared For flat states, Helly’s theorem gives a common
      majority witness, and the convex hull turns each hypothesis’s positive answers into a circular
      interval. The algorithms first discover the interval pattern containing the target, then decode
      its endpoints. Restricting an optimal tree to a pattern class pays for discovery, while count or
      entropy decreases pay for decoding. Exact scheduling and short lookahead combine these two
      credits.
          Both exact objectives are strongly NP-complete, including expected depth under a uniform
      prior. Some restricted results assume a distinguished hypothesis missed by every selected cap;
      its answers form the all-negative route used by those algorithms. These instances admit stronger
      restricted algorithms: exact maximum depth for fixed-size caps, an EPTAS for weighted sin-
      gleton/pair paths and cycles, and a PTAS for uniform fixed-size convex-position caps without
      singleton tests. Constant-factor inapproximability remains open. All constructed queries are
      supplied lines; an on-line third answer admits a factor-two transfer. For the distinct random-
      ized objective maxh ET dT (h), an expected-depth oracle and a finite mixture LP give the same
      polynomial and quasipolynomial factors. Each execution samples one tree from a distribution
      supported on at most n trees.

Figure 1: Seven randomly oriented supplied lines are drawn first, then twenty hypotheses are placed
in distinct cells of their arrangement. The arrows orient the lines: the positive side is to the left
of the arrow. Each internal node queries the correspondingly colored line. At every node the left
branch is the negative answer and the right branch is the positive answer. The leaf numbers match
the point labels. This valid tree has maximum depth five.


1      Introduction
An unknown point is promised to belong to a finite set of planar hypotheses. A query asks on which
side of a specified line the point lies. The line family is part of the input, so each next query must
be one of those supplied lines. Its choice may depend on all previous answers. Such a strategy is
a decision tree; each leaf names the hypothesis identified by the answers on its path. Every query
has unit cost.
    We study two objectives for these trees. The maximum depth is the largest number of queries
used on any hypothesis. The expected depth is the average under a given prior. These objectives
value the same tree differently: maximum depth is governed by its hardest branch, whereas expected
depth can spend more queries on low-probability hypotheses. The expected-depth algorithms accept
every strictly positive rational prior in the finite input model; no lower bound on an individual
hypothesis probability is assumed.
    The supplied-query restriction is what makes the problem interesting. If arbitrary lines were
allowed, a projection in a direction giving distinct projected coordinates would order the points,
and repeated median splits would give height ⌈log2 n⌉. A supplied family may contain no balanced
split. For example, if the only useful tests isolate individual points, the worst-case strategy may
have to test them one at a time and use depth n − 1. The information bound log2 n gives a target;
the challenge is to find a good tree using the separators that are actually available.
    Figure 1 makes the input and output concrete. The left panel has seven supplied lines with varied
orientations and twenty hypotheses placed in distinct cells of their arrangement. The right panel
is one legal decision tree for that instance: successive line-side answers isolate a single hypothesis.
Each internal node uses a line drawn on the left, and its leaves identify the same hypotheses marked
by the numbered points.

1.1     Results and the main ideas
In the restricted results, an all-miss instance has a distinguished hypothesis that every selected cap
misses; its answers form the all-negative route.

    Each row reports the strongest guarantee proved for that setup; the following sections develop
the scheduling and coding procedures it uses. The expected-depth guarantee permits every strictly
                                                                                               −1
positive rational prior. For 0 < ε ≤ 1, the general quasipolynomial algorithms run in (mn)O(ε log n)
time.
    Both exact objectives are strongly NP-complete, including expected depth under a uniform
prior. Constant-factor inapproximability remains open.
    The geometric structure behind the algorithms is easiest to see in a state (the current set of
hypotheses consistent with all answers so far) where no line splits much of the remaining mass.
Every test then has a majority answer. The opposite, minority half-planes are small, and Helly’s
theorem gives a point lying on all majority sides. After translating this common witness to the
origin, each query is represented by a point in a normalized plane. The vertices of their convex hull
(the smallest convex set containing those points) suffice to describe the relevant caps: each original
cap is covered by at most two adjacent vertex caps, and the vertex answers of any one hypothesis
form a circular interval : a consecutive block in the cyclic order in which the hull vertices appear
around the polygon, with wrap-around allowed. This turns the geometric part of the problem into
scheduling and searching over circular intervals.

Maximum depth: schedule discovery against child heights. In a flat state, the algorithm
first builds the recursive codes for the hull-pattern classes. It must then discover which class contains
the target, so it schedules positive hull answers with deadlines set by the child codes’ actual heights.
To analyze this discovery cost, follow an optimal tree along the common witness path. If a class
stays on that path through a long prefix, restricting the tree to the class removes those queries; the
saved depth pays for the class’s first positive hull answer in our schedule.
     Each original query cap is covered by at most two adjacent hull caps, so this comparison ordering
consists of singleton and adjacent-pair blocks, the same structure handled by the interval scheduler.
Enumerating its first adjacent pair fixes a cut and turns the remaining circular demands into ordinary
intervals. The priority fallback then preserves the comparison credit at every deadline. A second
exact scheduler stores subsets of available ranks in a spatial Cartesian tree. Its running time is
polynomial when the total cap is logarithmic; simultaneous recursive bounds for small and large
optima show that this cap suffices for the stronger general factor. Finally, endpoint searches are
charged separately. When the discovery path already provides a negative neighbor, that answer
fixes one endpoint and reduces the charge while preserving the child codes’ true heights.

Expected depth: pay once for learning a pattern. For expected depth, the algorithm again
discovers the complete hull-answer pattern before recursing. The analysis then separates the cost
of finding the pattern from the cost of identifying a hypothesis inside it. Along an optimal witness
path, a class’s constant prefix disappears when the tree is restricted to that class; those saved


                                                    5
queries pay for finding its first positive hull answer. After discovery, the entropy of the pattern
bounds the endpoint information that remains unknown. Exact weighted coverage at each integer
budget, followed by a deterministic choice among shifted geometric batches, sharpens the first-cover
bound used for this discovery cost.
     The endpoint decoder can query one or both endpoints of a positive block. When it queries
the second endpoint, that answer supplies additional information; adaptive block modes credit it
against a coarsened discovery transcript whose cells still retain each cap’s individual bound. A mass
cap on every adjacent support block then forces some targets into later blocks, where the discrete
first-cover bound is sharper. Targets that remain on the all-negative route are accounted for by the
waiting-time bound for the all-zero pattern. Finally, both the saved optimal-depth queries and the
entropy decrease telescope over the recursive partition, so each root-level cost is charged only once.

Strong hardness: encode bin loads by matching choices. Both hardness reductions begin
with strongly bounded 3-Partition: each item of size ai must be assigned to one of p bins, and the
target is load B in every bin. We encode each item’s choice by a matching on its own odd path.
The supplied singleton and adjacent-pair queries let the distinguished hypothesis count first-hit
groups along that path; at the height threshold, the count can attain its lower bound only when the
matching is maximum. Lemma 13.5 then identifies the one unmatched even-position cloud, whose
location records the item’s choice. The path gadgets therefore turn a tree attaining the threshold
into a candidate assignment of all items.
    We then calibrate the objective so that it tests this assignment. For maximum depth, cloud
sizes control the remaining heights of the branches. The resulting tail histogram rules out internal
unmatched positions and requires each bin load to be at most B; since the loads sum to pB, they
must all equal B. For expected depth, the matching cost formula separates a load-independent base
cost from a quadratic penalty in the deviations Lb − B, so the chosen threshold accepts precisely the
balanced assignments. These two calculations make the combinatorial choice visible to the respective
objectives. The integer-parabola construction realizes each singleton, pair, and private test by an
explicit supplied line, completing the geometric instance. Finally, to obtain the uniform-prior result,
we replace a weight w by w uniform hypotheses and give the new points private singleton tests. The
cost identity adds a common internal identification term for every matching; because this term does
not depend on the bin choices, the expected-depth comparison and the reduction are preserved.

Relation to earlier work. Karbasi and Zadimoghaddam’s constrained binary identification prob-
lem is a close information-theoretic precedent [4]. They relate unrestricted identification to source
coding and study how query constraints change the search strategy. In our setting, the common-
witness and convex-hull arguments turn the supplied planar tests into circular-interval demands.
Once the hull-answer pattern is known, entropy measures the endpoint information still needed to
identify the hypothesis, while coverage accounts for the cost of discovering that pattern. We state
and prove the entropy chain rule, log-sum, and prefix-code bounds used in this accounting. Cover
and Thomas [3] gives broader background on these information-theoretic tools.
    The coverage analysis draws on the first-cover viewpoint in min-sum set cover. Feige, Lovász,
and Tetali charge each element by the rank of its first covering set [1]; this is the objective formalized
in Lemma 5.5. Gupta, Nagarajan, and Ravi use a variant of min-sum set cover in their adaptive-TSP
framework for expected-cost decision trees [7]. These works provide a general first-cover accounting
model. Our schedulers specialize that model to weighted circular intervals and exact coverage
budgets, then combine the resulting discovery charge with a separate entropy bound for endpoint
decoding.


                                                    6
    Dasgupta’s analysis of greedy active learning provides a related expected-cost comparison: query
progress is measured by the probability mass it removes and compared with the cost of an opti-
mal identification tree [2]. His analysis leads to a balanced-split rule and uses collision probability.
That mass-removal viewpoint informs our comparison between discovery progress and optimal ex-
pected cost. The planar structure supplies first-cover schedules over circular intervals, while entropy
accounts for endpoint decoding. Adler and Heeringa likewise distinguish covering a group from
identifying an item within it [5], which motivates our separate charges for pattern discovery and
endpoint decoding. Cicalese, Laber, and Saettler study expected and worst-case costs together for
adaptive testing with general query families [6]. We give objective-specific guarantees when each
admissible query is a supplied planar line. For hardness context, Chakaravarthy et al. connect
entity-identification trees with min-sum set cover reductions [8]; our reductions encode matching
choices on odd paths by supplied lines on a parabola.
    There is also a geometric decision-tree precedent. Arkin et al. study point probes for identifying
polygonal models and use a common intersection point to obtain shallow trees [9]. Our model asks
line-side queries about a finite set of point hypotheses. A common witness arises here from Helly’s
theorem after orienting each query by its majority side, and the resulting convex-hull representation
is what leads to the circular-interval structure used by our schedulers.
Proof strategy.
Reading order.
      1. Sections 2–3 explain the model, restriction credits, and the circular-interval geometry.
      2. Sections 4–5 build the deadline and weighted-coverage primitives.
      3. Sections 6, 7, and 8 assemble the best general approximation algorithms.
      4. Sections 9–10 exploit bounded caps. The randomized mixture setup follows, in Section 11, connecting
         expected-depth oracles to mathematical programming.
      5. Sections 12–14 treat boundary answers and strong NP-completeness.
Each long result first states its supporting propositions and gives the overall proof. The next subsection
proves those propositions. Construction parameters are calibrated only after the structural argument.


2      Preliminaries and basic accounting
We collect the model, coding and information inequalities, and geometric terminology used by the
algorithms. Reduction-specific graph and complexity definitions are given in Section 13. For a
positive integer r, write [r] = {1, . . . , r}. The notation |S| denotes set cardinality, while |Q| denotes
the length of a word, vector, or sequence when Q is such an object. The indicator 1[P ] is one if
statement P holds and zero otherwise. We also write 1A (x) = 1[x ∈ A] for the indicator function
of a set A. The floor ⌊x⌋ and ceiling ⌈x⌉ are the greatest integer at most x and the least integer at
least x, respectively. For planar vectors, x · y = x1 y1 + x2 y2 is the dot product.

2.1      Line-query model and objectives
Let H ⊂ R2 be n ≥ 2 distinct points. A finite family L of m supplied lines separates every pair
of hypotheses, meaning that every two distinct points receive different answers to at least one line.
Write a line as the zero set of an affine function fℓ (x) = aℓ · x + bℓ , where aℓ ∈ R2 \ {0} and bℓ ∈ R.
Querying ℓ returns the sign of fℓ (h) for the hidden point h. Every query contributes one to the



                                                        7
depth. Unless explicitly stated otherwise, no hypothesis lies on a supplied line, so the answer is
binary. Section 12 treats a third, on-line outcome.
   After any sequence of queries and answers, the current state is the set of hypotheses whose signs
on all queried lines agree with the answers received. For a state S and a supplied line ℓ, write
                      Sℓ− = {h ∈ S : fℓ (h) < 0},         Sℓ+ = {h ∈ S : fℓ (h) > 0}.
These are the two answer classes; the line splits S when both classes are nonempty.
Definition 2.1 (Line-query decision trees). A binary line-query decision tree is a finite rooted tree
whose internal nodes are labeled by supplied lines and whose two outgoing edges are labeled by
their negative and positive answers. A hypothesis follows the edge matching its answer at each
query. An identifying tree for S has hypothesis labels at its leaves and sends every h ∈ S to a leaf
labeled h. The root has depth zero; a leaf’s depth is the number of queries on its root-to-leaf path,
and the tree’s height is its largest leaf depth.
    A partial decision tree, or stage, may instead stop at leaves labeled by nonempty subsets of S.
Each leaf is labeled by the hypotheses compatible with its path, so these output classes partition
S. Attaching an identifying tree for each class produces a complete identifying tree.
    We omit a query whenever its answer is already determined by the current candidate set; in
particular, a line is never queried twice on one path. Thus every internal node of the resulting tree
has two nonempty children, and we call such a tree proper. Every identifying tree can be put in
this form by deleting one-child queries and reusing the already-known answers, so the convention
preserves the optimum. It also gives a useful size bound: if a proper binary tree on n hypotheses
has I internal nodes, edge counting yields 2I = I + n − 1, hence I = n − 1 and every path has at
most n − 1 queries.
    A transcript records the queries and answers observed along a path. In a fixed binary tree the
previous answers determine the next query, so the transcript can also be represented by its answer
word, encoding negative as 0 and positive as 1. A cached answer is an answer already learned
on that path; reusing it or inferring a constant answer costs no additional query. For nonempty
A ⊆ S, the restriction T |A is obtained by deleting leaves outside A, deleting empty branches, and
contracting queries with only one surviving outcome. It is a valid tree on A, and no surviving
hypothesis’s depth increases.
Definition 2.2 (Membership answers and spines). A membership interpretation designates one
answer class A of a supplied binary query. Its answer is a hit when h ∈ A and a miss otherwise. A
hit may be either affine sign; interpreting the answer changes neither the supplied line nor its cost.
    The spine of a distinguished hypothesis is its root-to-leaf path. Another hypothesis exits that
path at its first different answer; the attached subtree is its local exit code. If this occurs at query
position j, a local depth d contributes total depth j +d. If every designated membership set excludes
the distinguished hypothesis, its spine is an all-miss route and exits occur at first hits. A point p
on no supplied line similarly determines a witness path, even when p ∈    / H.
    Let dT (h) be the number of queries on the path followed by h, stopping at its output leaf; for
an identifying tree that leaf names h. For a tree T identifying a candidate set S ⊆ H, define
                                         cW (T ) = max dT (h).
                                                     h∈S

by
                OPTW (S) =        min      cW (T ),    OPTE (S) =         min      cE (T ).

We set both optima to zero on the empty set and on singletons. Hypothesis weights used later in the
hardness proof specify these probabilities, while query depth still counts each test once. Keeping

weights unnormalized makes the expected costs of disjoint recursive states
   More explicitly, if positive raw weights ωh are supplied, put Ω = h∈H ωh and πh = ωh /Ω.
Then, also on a restricted state,    X
                                          ωh dT (h) = ΩcE (T ).

Thus normalizing weights divides both a weighted cost and its decision threshold by the same
Ω, leaving the optimal tree unchanged. The weights determine hypothesis mass in the expected
objective; each query still costs one.
   The two objectives now differ only in how they combine the child states. If Sℓ− and Sℓ+ are the
answer classes in S, their optimality recurrences on nonsingleton states are

                      OPTW (S) = 1 +         min          max{OPTW (Sℓ− ), OPTW (Sℓ+ )},
                                          ℓ splitting S

                       OPTE (S) = w(S) +            min          {OPTE (Sℓ− ) + OPTE (Sℓ+ )}.
                                                 ℓ splitting S

Algorithm 1 implements these recurrences on the small local states used later, and the same values
serve as benchmarks for the approximation algorithms. Their different combinations follow directly
from the objectives: a query adds one to every path, so the maximum-depth recurrence takes
the harder child; in expected depth the query contributes the state’s mass w(S), after which the
contributions of the two disjoint child sets add.

 Algorithm 1: Exact supplied-query Bellman recursion (small local states)
   Input: State S; objective o ∈ {W, E}; original weights πh .
   Output: An optimal supplied-query tree and its cost.
 1 if |S| ≤ 1 then
 2     return the leaf of cost zero
 3   if the memoized value for (S, o) exists then
 4       return that value and tree
 5 foreach supplied line ℓ splitting S do
 6     (T− , v− ) ← ExactTree(Sℓ− , o); (T+ , v+ ) ← ExactTree(Sℓ+ , o)
 7     vℓ ← 1 + max{v− , v+ } if o = W, and vℓ ← w(S) + v− + v+ otherwise
 8   Memoize the minimum vℓ and the tree joining its stored children
 9   return the memoized cost and tree

   Algorithm 1 takes 2O(|S|) poly(m, L) time with bit-mask states. Its exponential dependence stays
within bounded local sets, while the approximation algorithms handle larger states.
   All logarithms are natural unless a base is displayed, and we use 0 ln 0 = 0, the continuous
extension at zero. Coordinates, line coefficients, and probabilities are rational and explicitly given
by binary-encoded numerators and denominators. Polynomial time is measured in the total binary
encoding length of this input. The decision version of either objective asks, given an instance
and a threshold K, whether a legal identifying tree has cost at most K. For maximum depth the

                                                           9
threshold can be an integer; for expected depth it is rational and the prior is part of the input. An α-
approximation for a minimization objective returns a feasible tree whose cost is at most α times the
optimum on every valid input. For a deterministic algorithm, this bound applies to the particular
tree it returns. Whenever an algorithm permits several choices, fix a deterministic tie-breaking rule,
such as the input order.
  Symbol                   Meaning
  H, L; n, m               Hypothesis points, supplied lines, and their counts.
  S, Sℓ− , Sℓ+             Current state and its negative and positive answer classes for line ℓ.
  dT (h)                   Number of queries on the path for hypothesis h in tree T .
  cW (T ), OPTW (S)        Maximum path depth and its minimum over identifying trees for S.
  πh , w(S)                Root prior probability of h and the original probability mass of state S.
  cE (T ), OPTE (S)        Expected depth using the original probabilities and its minimum over identi-
                           fying trees for S.
  E(S)                     State-mass-weighted entropy of the conditional distribution on S.
  Π, ∆E (S, Π), I(S, Π)    A partition of S, the decrease in optimal expected depth, and the information
                           gained from its class label.
  ωh , Ω                   Optional raw positive weights and their total; πh = ωh /Ω.

Definition 2.3 (Balanced and flat states). Fix 0 < δ < 1/3. A nonsingleton state S is count-
balanced if some supplied line has at least δ|S| hypotheses on each side. Otherwise it is count-flat.
In the flat case, every line has a unique majority answer, on the side containing more than half of
S. We call this its frozen answer and write Cℓ for the open half-plane on the opposite side. Its
induced answer class Cℓ ∩ S is the minority cap, of size less than δ|S|. We also use “cap” for the
corresponding half-plane when discussing its geometry.
    For expected depth, the same split-versus-flat distinction must track probability mass, since
a small number of hypotheses can carry most of the prior. Accordingly, S is mass-balanced if
some line has w(Sℓ− ), w(Sℓ+ ) ≥ δw(S), and mass-flat otherwise. In a mass-flat state, freezing the
mass-majority answer gives w(Cℓ ∩ S) < δw(S).
    To turn each stored object into a legal supplied-query tree, the procedures use the following
conventions. Tables are indexed by realized hypotheses or patterns, and ties follow input order.
During tree assembly, cached answers supply later virtual questions, the origin marker records the
cut position, and infeasible branches are discarded. We then contract every query whose answer
is constant on the current state, leaving tests that distinguish hypotheses. The analysis for each
routine gives its parameter values and proves that they meet the required bounds.

State quantities and calibration choices. The state S, its mass w(S), pattern classes, and
optimal costs record the instance and the algorithm’s progress. Parameters such as δ, β, γ control
the constructions; each algorithm states their admissible conditions before choosing values for its
bound. A coefficient such as Φβ then records how that calibration and the current state determine
a particular charge.
Proof strategy.
The accounting facts below have three roles.
   1. Prefix codes turn query transcripts into information lower bounds.
   2. Restriction deletes queries that a known output class no longer needs.
   3. Potential differences cancel across recursive states, so local charges are paid only once.
The statements are collected first. Their proofs appear together in the next technical subsection, before
the geometric terminology.

Figure 2: The common recursive structure. Arrows show how one stage feeds the next. For
maximum depth, the algorithm constructs class children first and uses their heights as schedule
deadlines. For expected depth, original prior masses determine the schedule scores. The flat stage
has an all-negative leaf when the zero pattern is present, and both scheduling and decoding use
supplied hull lines.


2.2   Binary codes and ordered comparisons
Definition 2.4 (Binary codes and prefix codes). A binary word is a finite sequence of bits from
{0, 1}; {0, 1}∗ includes the empty word ε, of length zero. A binary code on a finite nonempty
alphabet A is an injective map c : A → {0, 1}∗ . The word c(a) is the codeword for a, and ℓa = |c(a)|
is its length. A word u is a prefix of v if v starts with all the bits of u. A code is prefix-free, or a
prefix code, if no codeword is a prefix of another distinct codeword.

          distribution (qa ) on the alphabet, the code’s maximum length is maxa ℓa and its expected
    For a P
length is a qa ℓa . The prefix tree of a code has one node for each prefix of a codeword, with the
empty prefix at the root and edges appending 0 or 1. For a prefix code its codewords are the leaves,
at depths ℓa . Conversely, the root-to-leaf answer words of any binary decision tree form a prefix
code. Unary nodes, which have only one child, can be contracted without increasing any depth. The
empty word is permissible only when the alphabet is a singleton. For example, c(a) = 0, c(b) = 10,
and c(c) = 11 form a prefix code with lengths 1, 2, 2.

Definition 2.5 (Alphabetic codes). For an ordered alphabet y1 < · · · < yr , a prefix code is
alphabetic if its codewords occur in that same lexicographic order, with 0 < 1. An ordered threshold
comparison asks whether the unknown outcome is at most yj for some j.

   In an alphabetic prefix tree, the outcomes below each node form a consecutive range. After unary
nodes are contracted, its two children separate that range at one threshold. Thus an alphabetic code
can be implemented by ordered threshold comparisons whenever those comparisons are available.
The code bits label the two ordered ranges; a line’s raw sign answer is routed to the matching range,
even if its sign encoding uses the opposite bit. Relabeling therefore only changes the names of the
two outcomes. Kraft’s inequality applies to every prefix code, while an executable decision-tree
code must also use comparisons available in the supplied line family. Section 3.4 verifies that the
endpoint-code comparisons meet this condition.


                                                        11
   A word b1 · · · bℓ specifies the half-open dyadic cell

The empty word specifies [0, 1). Such cells are nested or disjoint; one word is a prefix of another if
and only if its cell contains the other’s cell. Half-open endpoints avoid ambiguity at dyadic rationals.
    Query transcripts occupy disjoint leaves of a binary code. The next inequality expresses their
limited capacity; it will turn leaf depths into lower bounds on both decision-tree objectives.
Theorem 2.6 (Kraft’s inequality for binary prefix codes). Every finite binary prefix code has lengths
satisfying                                X
                                              2−ℓa ≤ 1.
                                                a∈A

Conversely, nonnegative integer lengths (ℓa )a∈A are realizable by a binary prefix code if this inequality
holds. The converse specifies codeword lengths; alphabetic order and implementation by a restricted
query family require additional structure.
   The code 0, 10, 11 has Kraft sum 2−1 + 2−2 + 2−2 = 1. More generally, equality holds for a
proper binary tree because its two child cells partition each internal node’s cell. The coding facts
here and the information inequalities below are standard; see Cover and Thomas [3].

2.3   Entropy and information inequalities
Definition 2.7 (Entropy and conditional entropy). A probability vector (qi ) has nonnegative entries
summing to one; its support consists of the indices with qi > 0. For a finite random variable Y with
probabilities qy = Pr[Y = y], its Shannon entropy in natural-log units is

    A stage first reveals a pattern and then identifies the hypothesis within that pattern. Since the
pattern is a function of the hypothesis, the entropy chain rule separates the information spent on
these two tasks. This separation lets the later endpoint decoder pay only for the information left
after pattern discovery.

Lemma 2.9 (Entropy chain rule). For finite random variables,

           H(Y, Z) = H(Z) + H(Y | Z),          H(Y, Z | W ) = H(Y | W ) + H(Z | Y, W ).

If Z is determined by Y , then H(Y ) = H(Z) + H(Y | Z). Also, 0 ≤ H(Y ) ≤ ln | supp(Y )| and
H(Y | Z) ≥ 0; in particular 0 ≤ h(t) ≤ ln 2.

   An information-efficient code must also respect the queries we can ask. For ordered endpoint
outcomes, alphabetic codes keep each branch consecutive and therefore make the coding bound
executable by thresholds.

Lemma 2.10 (Alphabetic threshold codes). Let Y have finite ordered support, and let H(Y ) be
its entropy with natural logarithms. There is an explicit alphabetic threshold code with depth on
outcome y at most log2 (1/ Pr[Y = y]) + 2. In particular its expected depth is at most

                                             H(Y )

This is a dyadic prefix-code construction; see Cover and Thomas [3] for the general coding back-
ground. The short proof here also verifies the extra alphabetic (order-preserving) property needed for
threshold queries. An optimal expected-depth threshold tree can also be computed by a polynomial
interval dynamic program. The explicit code is used when the pointwise depth bound is needed.

2.4     Restriction and recursive accounting
The next accounting step separates two sources of progress. Once a class is known, restriction
contracts queries that no longer help identify its members; meanwhile, the class label itself carries
information measured by entropy. These give complementary lower bounds for the work performed
by a stage.

Definition 2.11 (Partitions and refinement). A partition Π of S is a family of pairwise disjoint
nonempty subsets whose union is S. We say Π refines R, or R is coarser than Π, if every class of
Π is contained in a class of R. The output classes of a partial decision tree form a partition.

    Revealing an output class can only make identification easier: queries whose answers are then
known disappear. This observation compares recursive optima, while the coding inequalities provide
a lower bound on how much work any identifying tree still requires.

Lemma 2.12. For pairwise disjoint subsets S1 , . . . , Sk of a state S,
                 X
                    OPTE (Si ) ≤ OPTE (S),              OPTW (Si ) ≤ OPTW (S).

Proof of Lemma 2.12. Claim 2.4 gives both restriction inequalities. Claims 2.5 and 2.6 supply the
two information lower bounds.

summing them over the recursion charges each root-level potential only once.
Proof strategy.
Sum each potential decrement over the recursive partition tree; every nonroot state then cancels once as
a child against once as a parent. Singleton leaves have zero potential, and the stage-cost bound can be
summed because each query’s original-probability cost is assigned to a unique stage.

has expected cost at most a OPTE (H) + cE(H).

recursive partition tree. Every nonroot internal state occurs once as a child and once as a parent.

Proof of Theorem 2.6. For necessity, prefix-free codewords specify disjoint dyadic cells inside [0, 1),

     For sufficiency, reorder the lengths as ℓ1 ≤ · · · ≤ ℓr and put si = j<i 2−ℓj . Each si is an

integer. The intervals [si , si + 2−ℓi ) are dyadic cells of the prescribed depths. They are disjoint and
lie in [0, 1) by the assumed bound. Their binary descriptions give the required prefix code; assign
them back to the original symbols.

Proof strategy.
For Lemma 2.10, place a small dyadic cell inside each probability interval. Disjoint cells preserve outcome
order; their depths give the coding bound. A separate interval recurrence optimizes the executable threshold
tree.

Claim 2.1 (Ordered disjoint cells give an alphabetic code). Each outcome’s cumulative-probability
interval contains a dyadic cell. These cells are disjoint, occur in outcome order, and form an
alphabetic prefix code.

                                                            16
                                                                                   ′
                                                                  P
Proof. For an ordered outcome y of probability a, let Fy =          y ′ <y Pr[Y = y ]. Its cumulative-
probability interval is [Fy , Fy + a), with midpoint my = Fy + a/2. The midpoint-dyadic code chooses
the dyadic cell containing my at depth ℓy = ⌈log2 (2/a)⌉, in the sense of the cells defined before
Theorem 2.6. If sy is the cell’s left endpoint and wy = 2−ℓy its width, then sy ≤ my < sy + wy and
wy ≤ a/2. Therefore
                        sy > my − wy ≥ Fy ,           sy + wy ≤ my + wy ≤ Fy + a.
The strict first inequality follows from my < sy + wy ; both outer interval bounds then use wy ≤ a/2
and my = Fy + a/2. Consequently no cell reaches outside its own probability interval, even when a
midpoint is dyadic. Thus the entire half-open cell lies in [Fy , Fy + a). These cells are disjoint and
appear in outcome order. Their binary descriptions form a prefix-free alphabetic code.

   The cells have given an abstract ordered code. We now check that its branches are legal threshold
comparisons and that converting the code to a tree preserves the promised depth.
Claim 2.2 (Threshold implementation preserves the depth bound). Ordered threshold queries im-
plement the code with depth at most log2 (1/ Pr[Y = y]) + 2 on outcome y.
Proof. By Claim 2.1, outcome y has a codeword of length ⌈log2 (2/a)⌉, where a = Pr[Y = y]. Since

Indeed, every outcome in the interval pays for the root query; the two disjoint child intervals then

operations. The resulting optimal alphabetic tree satisfies the expected-depth bound of Claim 2.2,
since that claim supplies a feasible comparison tree. Thus the interval program optimizes average
depth, while the explicit midpoint-dyadic construction supplies the pointwise depth bound.

height gives the corresponding lower bound.

Claim 2.6 (Binary capacity lower-bounds height). A binary tree of height d has at most 2d leaves,
so identifying S requires height at least log2 |S|.

Proof. For the maximum-depth bound, prove by induction on d that a binary tree of height d has
at most 2d leaves. The claim is immediate for d = 0; for d > 0, the root has at most two child
subtrees, each of height at most d − 1, so together they have at most 2 · 2d−1 = 2d leaves. Thus
|S| ≤ 2d , or d ≥ log2 |S| = ln |S|/ ln 2.

2.6   Convex geometry and finite set systems
Definition 2.14 (Planar convexity). A set P   is convex if it contains thePsegment joining any two of
its points. A convex combination is a sum j λj uj with λj ≥ 0 and j λj = 1. The convex hull
conv(U ) of a finite nonempty point set U is the set of its convex combinations. A vertex, or extreme
point, cannot be expressed as a convex combination of other points of the hull. The hull is the
convex hull of its vertices; set conv(∅) = ∅ by convention.
    A nonempty planar hull has affine dimension zero, one, or two according as it is a point, a
nontrivial segment, or has nonempty planar interior. In dimension two it is a convex polygon,
whose vertices have a cyclic boundary order; its edges join consecutive vertices. A supporting line
meets a convex set and leaves it entirely on one closed side. It exposes a point if that point is its
unique intersection with the set. A finite point set is in strictly convex position if every point is a
vertex of its hull.

    An open half-plane has the form {x : a · x > b} for a ̸= 0; replacing > by ≥ gives its closed
counterpart. A point is strictly on a chosen side when it lies in that side’s open half-plane. A
common strict witness for several chosen sides lies in all their open half-planes and may be any
point in the plane.
    The geometry will produce witnesses for every three majority sides. The planar Helly theorem
is the step that turns this local consistency into a single witness for all supplied lines.

Theorem 2.15 (Planar Helly theorem). If every subfamily of at most three sets in a finite family
of planar convex sets has nonempty intersection, then the whole family has nonempty intersection.
The sets may be open.

   This is the standard planar form; see Matoušek [10].

Definition 2.16 (Covers and hitting sets). A finite set system consists of a universe U , a candidate
set V , and supplied subsets Fv ⊆ U indexed by v ∈ V . A cover of U is a choice of these sets whose
union contains every element of U . In the equivalent demand formulation, there is a candidate set
V and a subset Dh ⊆ V for each demand h. A hitting set is a subset P ⊆ V with P ∩ Dh ̸= ∅ for
every demand. Choosing v covers the set Fv = {h : v ∈ Dh }. Thus covering demands by candidates
and hitting their subsets are the same incidence problem. A demand with Dh = ∅ is uncoverable.



                                                  19
   With positive demand weights, a choice covers the sum of the weights of the demands it hits,
counting each demand once. An incidence table records the bits 1[h ∈ Fv ], equivalently 1[v ∈ Dh ].
A cardinality budget is a nonnegative integer k permitting at most k selected candidates. We may
cap it at |V |, since there are no more distinct candidates to choose.
Definition 2.17 (Ordered and circular intervals). On a finite linear order v1 , . . . , vM , an ordinary
interval is a consecutive subset {vi , . . . , vj }; we also allow the empty interval. Its nonempty intervals
have first and last elements, called their endpoints. On a cyclic order, a circular interval is a
consecutive block with wrap-around allowed, also permitting the empty and whole-cycle sets. Two
vertices are adjacent when consecutive in this order, including vM , v1 on a cycle. An oriented
adjacent pair specifies which of those two vertices is first. A run of P in a linear candidate order is
a maximal nonempty consecutive block contained in P ; c(P ) counts such runs, with c(∅) = 0.
    Cutting a cyclic order at a removed vertex turns any circular interval avoiding that vertex into
an ordinary interval. When interval demands occur below, candidates are the ordered vertices, and
selecting any vertex in a demand’s interval hits it.

2.7    Query orderings and first-cover costs
Let a universe U have positive element weights πh and supplied sets Fv covering U . We allow
these weights to be unnormalized. A cover ordering Q = (Fv1 , . . . , Fvℓ ) is a sequence of distinct
supplied sets whose union covers U , and |Q| = ℓ is its length. The first-cover time, or latency, of h
is tQ (h) = min{j : h ∈ Fvj }. The weighted min-sum set cover objective is
                                                  X
                                     MSSC(Q) =         πh tQ (h).
                                                           h∈U

Equivalently, MSSC(Q) is the sum of the uncovered mass before each step: a demand first covered
at position tQ (h) contributes its weight to the residuals at positions 0, . . . , tQ (h) − 1. Interchanging
these finite sums gives
                                    |Q|−1          j
                                                         !
                                     X            [                         X
                      MSSC(Q) =           w U\        Fvr ,      w(E) =            πh .
                                      j=0             r=1                         h∈E

The union for j = 0 is empty. Maximum-remaining-weight greedy repeatedly chooses the supplied
set containing the largest total weight of elements not yet covered, counting each element only once.

2.8    Normalized queries and hull patterns
Definition 2.18 (Witness-relative query notation). Enumerate the supplied lines as ℓ1 , . . . , ℓm . For
a state S and a point p on no supplied line, put
                                                aℓi
                    xh = h − p,       ui = −           ,         Ci = {y : ui · (y − p) > 1}.
                                               fℓi (p)
Thus xh is a translated hypothesis vector, ui a normalized query vector, and Ci the cap opposite p
in the original plane. The corresponding dual demand is

                                            Rh = {u : u · xh > 1}.

Write K = conv({0} ∪ {ui : i ∈ [m]}) and let V be its nonzero vertices, in cyclic hull order. Every
v ∈ V equals a supplied query vector; choose one representative line if several coincide, and put

                                                       20
                                                                            P
Cv = {y : v · (y −
                 P p) > 1}.  A support block J i ⊆ V represents u i as ui =  v∈Ji αiv v, with positive
coefficients and v αiv ≤ 1. We choose blocks of at most two vertices, adjacent when there are two;
their existence is proved in Lemma 3.2.

    The origin’s hull bit is always negative because 0 · xh = 0; we use it as a free marker that opens
the cyclic order. If it lies on the hull boundary, it can be inserted at its boundary position as a free
cut marker. The hull K, its vertices, and their cyclic order can be computed in polynomial time by
standard planar convex-hull algorithms [11].
    Indeed, substituting the definitions of ui and xh , and using fℓi (h) = fℓi (p) + aℓi · (h − p), gives

                                                             fℓi (h)
                                           ui · xh − 1 = −           .
                                                             fℓi (p)

Consequently, h ∈ Ci if and only if ui ∈ Rh , and equality ui · xh = 1 means h lies on ℓi . We encode
the cap-side answer as 1 and the witness-side answer as 0. A positive hull answer is this cap-side bit,
regardless of the original affine sign; the relabeling routes the answer of the same supplied query.
If xh = 0, the demand Rh is empty. We write Ci ∩ S for the original-plane hypotheses in the cap,
while xh denotes their translated vectors.

Definition 2.19 (Hull patterns and pattern classes). The hull pattern of h ∈ S is the bit vector
                                                       
                                  σ(h) = 1[v · xh > 1] v∈V .

A pattern class consists of all hypotheses with the same vector, and Π is the partition into these
classes. The positive vertices are the coordinates whose bits are one. Write A0 for the class with
every bit zero, taking A0 = ∅ when no hypothesis has that pattern. When a prior is specified, the
nonzero-pattern mass is b(S) = w(S) − w(A0 ), the mass on which a positive hull answer is possible.

    A discovery schedule lists individual hull tests in their query order along the all-negative route.
Its first-hit time gA for a nonzero pattern class is the index of the first listed vertex positive on that
class.


3    Common-witness geometry and interval tools
Using the notation of Definition 2.18, we prove the structural facts shared by both algorithms.
Algorithm 3 records their common preprocessing. For a flat state, Helly’s theorem gives a common
majority witness. Relative to this witness, each query cap is covered by at most two adjacent hull
caps, so every hypothesis’s positive hull answers form one circular interval. The interval tools below
use this structure to discover patterns and decode their endpoints.
Proof strategy.

    1. Flatness makes every three majority half-planes intersect; Helly’s theorem gives a common strict
       witness.
    2. Radial projection onto the normalized query hull supplies adjacent supports; a half-plane reads one
       circular positive interval.
    3. Interval covers discover a positive vertex, after which a query-free cut turns supplied hull tests into
       endpoint comparisons.




                                                      21
  Symbol                 Meaning
  p, xh , ui             Strict common witness, translated hypothesis vector h − p, and normalized
                         vector for supplied line ℓi .
  Ci , Rh                Original-plane cap opposite p for query i, and the dual set of normalized queries
                         positive on h.
  K, V                   Convex hull of the normalized query vectors together with 0, and its nonzero
                         vertices in cyclic order.
  Ji , αiv               At most two adjacent support vertices for ui and their nonnegative coefficients,
                         whose sum is at most one.
  σ(h), Π                Hull-test bit pattern of h and the partition into equal-pattern classes.
  A0 , b(S)              All-zero pattern class (empty if absent) and, with priors, the mass outside that
                         class.
  gA                     First positive test position for a nonzero pattern class A in a discovery schedule.

3.1    A common majority witness
The majority answers must describe one coherent route before we can compare our discovery sched-
ule with an optimal tree. A common witness provides that route even when no hypothesis has all
majority answers.

Lemma 3.1 (Common witness). In either kind of flat state, a point p strictly inside every majority
half-plane can be computed in polynomial time.

Proof. Flatness forces every three majority sides to intersect. Claim 3.1 and Theorem 2.15 therefore
give a common strict point. Claim 3.2 then finds a rational one by a positive-margin linear program.


3.2    Adjacent supports and circular patterns
The witness lets us express every query relative to the same origin. The next lemma identifies the
two properties that make this useful: each original cap is covered by adjacent hull caps, and each
target sees those hull tests as one interval.

Lemma 3.2 (Hull support and pattern structure). The support blocks of Definition 2.18 can be
chosen with at most two real hull vertices, adjacent when there are two. For every query,
                                                  [
                                      Ci ∩ S ⊆       (Cv ∩ S).                            (1)
                                                    v∈Ji

Negative support bits force the query’s witness-side answer. The positive vertices of every hypothesis
form a circular interval, |A0 | ≤ 1, and every nonzero pattern class lies in a positive hull cap.

Proof. Claim 3.3 projects each query vector radially to a hull edge, and Claim 3.4 shows that its
adjacent endpoints cover that query’s cap. Claim 3.5 then proves the interval structure and uses
separability to bound the all-zero class.

    The routine first finds a point that satisfies every oriented majority inequality, then uses that
common witness to normalize the supplied query normals into a planar hull. The supports Ji retain
the queries represented by each hull direction; grouping hypotheses by their resulting bit vectors
gives the full-pattern classes needed by the recursive algorithms. Lemmas 3.1 and 3.2 justify the
witness and support invariant.
