                                                                            v5
                            0
                  (a) Adjacent supports in the vector hull (b) One circular positive interval

Figure 3: The first panel depicts normalized query vectors: y lies on the edge vw and ui = λy for
0 < λ ≤ 1. Writing y = θv + (1 − θ)w gives ui = αv + βw with α + β = λ ≤ 1. Consequently, if
both support values on a hypothesis are at most one, then ui · xh ≤ 1; equivalently, Ci ⊆ Cv ∪ Cw .
The second panel shows the cyclic order only; the positive hull tests of a fixed hypothesis form one
interval in that order.

 Algorithm 3: HullData: a flat state’s witness, supports, and patterns
   Input: Flat state S and its count or mass measure; supplied lines.
   Output: Strict witness p, full hull K, real vertices V , supports Ji , partition Π.
 1 Orient every majority inequality as ai · p − bi > 0
 2 Maximize t subject to ai · p − bi ≥ t and 0 ≤ t ≤ 1; retain a solution with t > 0
 3 Compute ui = −aℓi /fℓi (p) and K = conv({0} ∪ {ui })
 4 Keep one supplied representative of each real hull vertex in cyclic order

 5 foreach ui do
 6     Extend its ray from 0 to the last point y in K
 7     Express y on its boundary edge and scale the coefficients by ∥ui ∥/∥y∥; omit the origin and zero
        coefficients to form Ji
 8   Compute all bits 1[v · (h − p) > 1] and group equal rows into Π
 9   return (p, K, V, (Ji ), Π), retaining the origin’s boundary position

    When the normalized hull has dimension at most one, its segment endpoints provide the supports
directly.
    Figure 3 separates the two geometric roles: panel (a) shows how adjacent hull vertices support
an original query vector, while panel (b) records only the cyclic order of the hull tests seen positively
by one hypothesis.

3.3     Interval hitting and discovery length
A complete discovery route must hit every nonzero pattern. Once these patterns are intervals,
we can compute its smallest possible test set; this will also provide the final budget in the batch
construction.
Lemma 3.3 (Minimum interval cover). A minimum-cardinality hitting set for ordinary or circu-
lar intervals on a finite candidate order is computable in polynomial time. An empty demand is
infeasible; an empty family needs no candidates.
Proof. On a line, an exchange argument permits choosing the right endpoint of the earliest-ending
demand. On a circle, enumerate one forced hit and cut there; the remaining problem is linear. The
detailed exchange argument is given below.

    Priority and weight considerations can change the order in which tests are chosen. We therefore
also need a length bound for greedy discovery, including the extra saving when the comparison cover

                                                        23
has adjacent runs. Here a demand is a nonempty interval of candidate positions, each demand I has
a fixed weight w(I) > 0, and a candidate’s remaining weight is the sum of w(I) over currently unhit
demands containing it. At each step greedy chooses a candidate of maximum remaining weight;
ties may be resolved arbitrarily. The comparison cover P is any finite set of positions hitting every
initial demand, k = |P |, and c(P ) counts its maximal consecutive blocks in the full candidate order.
After a greedy choice, demands hit by that choice are deleted. The claimed bound holds for every
such cover P ; taking a minimum cover gives the stated special case.

Lemma 3.4 (Greedy cover length with adjacent-run credit). Let a nonempty family of positively
weighted interval demands be covered by a candidate set P of size k. Let c(P ) be the number of runs
of consecutive candidates in P , measured in the entire candidate order. Maximum-remaining-weight
greedy finishes within k + c(P ) − 1 choices, for every tie rule. In particular, a minimum cover of
size k gives the usual bound 2k − 1. For circular intervals the corresponding bound is 2k.

The extra term is the number of runs beyond the first. A scattered cover may force greedy to spend
choices repairing several gaps, whereas a cover arranged in a few contiguous blocks has fewer such
gaps to repair.
Proof strategy.
Use the potential |P |+c(P ) for the still-active comparison cover. An extreme choice can replace an extreme
point of P without changing which positive-weight demands it hits. An interior choice partitions the unhit
demands into left and right interval families. The cover parts on those sides have combined size plus run
count at most the previous potential. Induction pays for the first choice and then for each nonempty side.

Proof. Induct on the comparison cover size, separating extreme choices from interior ones. Suppose
first that the greedy choice v lies left of P , and let u = min P . Every active interval containing v
also contains u: it is covered by some p ∈ P , and the endpoint inequalities give v < u ≤ p inside that
interval. Thus the hit-demand set of v is contained in the hit-demand set of u. Maximum remaining
weight gives W (u) ≤ W (v), while containment and nonnegative weights give W (v) ≤ W (u). Since
each weight is positive, the sets are equal, so the two hit sets coincide. After querying v, every
remaining demand therefore avoids u and is covered by some point of P ′ = P \ {u}. The right
extreme is symmetric. Since u is an extreme point, deleting it cannot split a run, so |P ′ | = k − 1
and c(P ′ ) ≤ c(P ). If demands remain, induction bounds their greedy completion by

                                    |P ′ | + c(P ′ ) − 1 ≤ k + c(P ) − 2.

Including the current query gives at most k + c(P ) − 1 choices. If no demand remains, the single
current query already meets that bound.
    Now suppose the greedy choice v is interior. Every unhit interval lies wholly to one side of v.
Let P− and P+ be the comparison points strictly to its left and right. They cover the corresponding
families. If v ∈/ P , cutting the original order in its gap preserves the total number of runs, so
c(P− ) + c(P+ ) ≤ c(P ) and their sizes sum to k. If v ∈ P , the side sizes sum to k − 1, and deleting
v can split at most one run into two, so c(P− ) + c(P+ ) ≤ c(P ) + 1. In either case,

                               |P− | + c(P− ) + |P+ | + c(P+ ) ≤ k + c(P ).

The induction hypotheses bound the two side schedules by their respective potentials minus one.
Adding the current choice gives at most k + c(P ) − 1. When only one side remains active, the
unused side has at least one comparison point and hence potential at least two; this pays for the
absent induction term. When neither side remains, one choice is within the bound because k ≥ 2

                                                     24
in the interior case. Finally c(P ) ≤ k, since each run contains a distinct point of P ; therefore
k + c(P ) − 1 ≤ 2k − 1. For circular demands, the first greedy choice cuts the circle and leaves
ordinary intervals; the original k points still cover those intervals, giving at most 2k − 1 further
choices and the bound 2k.

3.4     Recovering the interval endpoints
A positive vertex locates a small cap. We recover the full pattern by opening the circular order at
a safe cut and comparing the interval’s endpoints against the supplied queries.
Lemma 3.5 (Query-free endpoint representation). After one positive hull vertex v is known, a
cut computable without queries represents every compatible positive pattern as an ordinary interval
containing v. Supplied hull tests compare its endpoint coordinates. A cached negative neighbor fixes
one endpoint; two negative neighbors fix the entire pattern.

Proof strategy.
Use a cached negative neighbor as the cut when available. Otherwise the origin and an opposite hull edge
give a cut that no compatible positive interval can cross. All later comparisons are actual hull tests, so no
new line or anchor query is introduced.

Proof. A cached negative neighbor supplies a cut and pins one endpoint. Otherwise an opposite hull
edge gives a cut without asking a query. In the resulting linear order, supplied hull tests are endpoint
comparisons. Claims 3.8, 3.9, and 3.10 establish these steps, including degenerate hulls.
   A positive discovery exit is pinned if a neighbor of its discovered vertex has a cached negative
answer. It is singly pinned when exactly one neighbor is known negative, and doubly pinned when
both are. Pinning fixes one or both endpoints, as in Lemma 3.5; it introduces no additional query.
 Algorithm 4: EndpointCode: legal full-pattern recovery
   Input: A positive hull vertex v, compatible patterns, cached answers; code mode; optionally fixed
            continuation heights hA .
   Output: A supplied-query decoder whose leaves are full pattern classes.
 1 if the hull has dimension at most one then
 2     return the already determined nonzero pattern
 3 if v has a cached negative neighbor then
 4     Cut at that neighbor and orient so v is the first positive vertex
 5 else
 6     Cut at a boundary origin, or at the opposite edge met by the ray v0
 7 List the realized left and right endpoint pairs in the cut order
 8 if no continuation heights are supplied then
 9     Search each unfixed endpoint using Algorithm 2; condition the second search on the first answer
10 else
11     foreach nonempty realized endpoint rectangle U , in increasing size do
12         if U has a single realized class A then
13             E(U ) ← hA
14         else
15             E(U ) ← minℓ (1 + max{E(U ∩ ℓ− ), E(U ∩ ℓ+ )}), over proper supplied endpoint
                 thresholds

16      Reconstruct the minimizing rectangle splits, retaining full-pattern leaves and their recorded
         continuation heights
17   return the decoder; skip fixed endpoints and cached thresholds


                                                     25
    The cut turns the cyclic endpoint problem into a linear one, and Lemma 3.5 shows that its
thresholds are supplied queries, so no extra anchor query is needed. If child heights are provided,
the rectangle recurrence compares each legal split by the height of its endpoint subproblems to-
gether with the continuation at the resulting pattern leaf. This is the complete-exit optimization of
Section 8. Its O(M 4 ) rectangles and O(M ) thresholds give polynomial running time. The routine
returns pattern leaves, after which the recursive algorithm attaches each stored child once. The
values E(U ) include the continuation heights when comparing splits, while each child’s query cost
is accounted for inside its attached recursive tree.

3.5   Proofs of the geometric and interval facts
The key step is to convert planar caps into executable endpoint comparisons. Radial projection
gives the one-way cover implication needed by the analysis: every positive original cap is covered
by its support caps. The endpoint cut then follows from the geometry or from cached answers, so
it requires no additional query.

Proof of Lemma 3.1. There are two distinct tasks: obtain a common strict point from flatness, then
find such a point with controlled rational encoding. The following claims separate the geometric
existence argument from its algorithmic realization.
Claim 3.1 (Three majority half-planes have a common strict point). Every subfamily of at most
three majority half-planes intersects.

Proof. Use the measure defining flatness, either cardinality or probability. By Definition 2.3, each
minority cap has measure less than δ times the measure of S. The union bound therefore makes the
union of any three such caps have measure less than 3δ times that of S. Since 3δ < 1, that union
omits a hypothesis. That hypothesis belongs strictly to all three majority half-planes, because no
hypothesis lies on a query line. The same argument covers subfamilies of fewer than three lines.
Thus every subfamily of at most three majority half-planes has nonempty intersection. Theorem 2.15
implies that the whole family has nonempty intersection.

   We now turn this strict intersection into the preprocessing algorithm’s witness. A positive-
margin linear program finds a rational point with polynomial encoding length, so the algorithm can
compute and store it.
Claim 3.2 (A positive margin gives a rational strict witness). The common point can be found with
polynomial encoding length.

Proof. To obtain a strict rational witness, orient each majority inequality as ai · p − bi > 0 and
maximize t subject to ai · p − bi ≥ t for every i and 0 ≤ t ≤ 1. The strict point obtained from
Claim 3.1 and Theorem 2.15 has a positive value in each of the finitely many inequalities. Their
minimum, capped at one, supplies a feasible positive t. This is a linear program: its objective
and constraints are linear in the unknown coordinates of p and the margin t. Rational linear
programming therefore returns a strict witness with polynomial encoding length [12].



Proof of Lemma 3.2. We establish the structure in three steps. First represent each query by neigh-
boring hull vertices; next prove the cap-cover implication; finally identify the interval patterns and
the unique possible all-zero target. Each step is used by a different part of the algorithms.



                                                 26
Claim 3.3 (Radial boundary projection needs only adjacent supports). Each normalized vector has
a nonnegative support expression whose coefficients sum to at most one.

Proof. Extend the ray from 0 through ui to its last point y in K. Such a point exists because the
finite hull is compact and contains ui ̸= 0. Write y = tui with t ≥ 1, so ui = λy for λ = 1/t ∈ (0, 1].
If y lies on an edge with endpoints v, w, write y = θv + (1 − θ)w, with 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1. Then

                        ui = (λθ)v + λ(1 − θ)w,           λθ + λ(1 − θ) = λ ≤ 1.

The coefficients are nonnegative. Delete origin endpoints and zero coefficients to obtain Ji . A
boundary vertex needs only itself. In dimension at most one, the last ray point is a segment
endpoint, so the same conclusion holds directly.

    The support expression is useful because it transfers a negative answer from the hull tests back
to the original query. Only this one-way implication is needed to cover an optimal witness route.
Claim 3.4 (Negative support answers force the original answer). The hull-cap inclusion (1) holds.

Proof. If every support has v · xh ≤ 1, the expression from Claim 3.3 gives
                                                X
                                      ui · xh =     αiv (v · xh )
                                                  v∈Ji
                                                  X
                                              ≤          αiv ≤ 1.
                                                  v∈Ji

The first inequality uses the nonnegative support coefficients; the second uses their sum bound.
Thus a positive original query must have a positive support, proving (1). Equality cannot occur at
a hypothesis because it would put it on the original supplied line. The origin has dot product zero,
so its bit is always negative and it is never queried.

    Having justified cap coverage, we now describe the states produced by hull tests. Their interval
structure enables the scheduling tools; the uniqueness of the zero pattern makes the final negative
route valid.
Claim 3.5 (The hull patterns are intervals with at most one zero point). Every nonzero class
belongs to a supplied positive hull cap.

Proof. The positive vertices are V ∩ Rh . Here is why they are consecutive. Intersect the boundary
line u · xh = 1 with the convex polygon K. Its intersection with K is empty or a segment; in a
genuine crossing its two endpoints cut the polygon boundary into two arcs, one on each side of the
line. The positive vertices lie on the positive arc. A tangency leaves all vertices on one side and also
gives an empty or consecutive block. No hull query vertex lies on the boundary line, since that would
place h on its supplied query. Removing the known-negative origin, if it is a vertex, leaves a circular
interval on V . Degenerate hulls have at most two real vertices and satisfy the same assertion. By
Claim 3.4, an all-zero pattern forces every original query to have the witness’s answer. Two such
hypotheses would therefore have identical answers on all supplied lines, contradicting separability.
Finally, a nonzero class has some positive vertex v, and all its hypotheses belong to Cv ∩ S.




                                                   27
Proof of Lemma 3.3. For ordinary intervals, choose the right endpoint q of an earliest-ending in-
terval I. Any cover has a point u ∈ I. Replacing u by q loses no demand containing u: its right
endpoint is at least q, by the earliest-ending choice of I, and its left endpoint is at most u ≤ q.
Hence it also contains q. Thus an optimal cover can choose q. Delete the intervals hit there and
repeat, proving the endpoint greedy rule is exact.
    For circular intervals, choose a nonempty demand and enumerate a forced candidate v in it.
Delete demands hit by v and cut the cycle there. All remaining demands are ordinary intervals, so
use the preceding rule and add v. Some forced choice belongs to an optimal circular cover; take the
smallest resulting solution.

Proof of Lemma 3.4. Induct on k. When k = 1, its sole candidate hits every demand, and any
greedy maximum does so as well. Let v be the first greedy choice.
    At an extreme, greedy removes the same demands as one comparison-cover point. We can
therefore delete that point from the comparison cover, which gives the induction step that reduces
the cover size.
Claim 3.6 (An extreme greedy choice removes an extreme cover point). The remaining demands
admit the cover P \ {u} with no increase in run count.

Proof. If v lies at or outside an extreme of P , let u be the nearest extreme cover point. For example,
if v < min P = u, every demand containing v must also meet P , and hence must contain u: if p ∈ P
is a point of that demand, then v < u ≤ p and the demand is an interval. Thus the demands hit
by v are a subset of those hit by u, which gives W (v) ≤ W (u) by summing nonnegative weights.
Greedy maximality gives W (u) ≤ W (v), so the scores are equal. Because every weight is strictly
positive, a proper containment would make the second score strictly larger; hence the candidates
hit the same demands. Thus P \ {u} covers the remainder, and removing an extreme leaves its run
count unchanged or smaller. Induction plus the first query gives at most k + c(P ) − 1 choices.

    For an interior choice, the comparison cover may retain all its points. The unhit demands then
split into independent left and right subproblems, whose combined size-plus-run charge supplies the
required progress.
Claim 3.7 (An interior choice creates independent left and right problems). The sum of their
size-plus-run charges is at most k + c(P ).

Proof. Otherwise v lies strictly between the extremes of P . The unhit intervals lie entirely on one
side of v. The parts P− , P+ strictly to its left and right cover these two families, and

                              |P− | + c(P− ) + |P+ | + c(P+ ) ≤ k + c(P ).

     / P , cutting in its gap leaves the total run count unchanged. If v ∈ P , removing it reduces
If v ∈
size by one and can increase total run count by at most one, which proves the displayed inequality
in either case. Runs are measured against the full candidate order, so truncating to the two sides
creates no new gap boundary except at the removed v. Greedy may interleave the two sides, but a
choice on one side covers no interval on the other; hence the choices restricted to either side are still
a greedy sequence for that side. If both families are nonempty, each side has a smaller comparison
cover than P . Applying the induction hypothesis to both sides and paying for the first query gives
                                                               
                    1 + |P− | + c(P− ) − 1 + |P+ | + c(P+ ) − 1 ≤ k + c(P ) − 1.




                                                   28
The last inequality substitutes the preceding bound on their combined size-plus-run charge. If only
one is nonempty, the other cover part still has size plus run count at least two. If Plive covers the
nonempty side, the total is at most
                                                 
                     1 + |Plive | + c(Plive ) − 1 ≤ k + c(P ) − 2 ≤ k + c(P ) − 1.

Here induction bounds the live side, while the unused side’s charge of at least two bounds its
complement in k + c(P ): at least one point remains on that side since v lies strictly between the
extremes, and one nonempty run contributes one to each term. If neither remains, one query suffices,
and k ≥ 2 in this interior case ensures 1 ≤ k + c(P ) − 1.

    Claims 3.6 and 3.7 complete the induction. Finally c(P ) ≤ k, because each run contains a
distinct cover point, giving 2k − 1 without adjacency information.
    For circular intervals, make the first greedy choice and cut the cycle there. Every unhit interval
becomes an ordinary interval. The original k cover candidates still cover these demands, so at most
2k − 1 further choices are needed.

Proof of Lemma 3.5. First exploit a cached negative answer if one exists. Otherwise find a safe
cut geometrically. After either construction, verify that the resulting endpoint searches ask only
supplied hull queries.
Claim 3.8 (A cached negative neighbor pins an endpoint). Only the opposite endpoint remains to
be learned.

Proof. After discovery, one positive hull vertex v is known. First inspect its cached neighbor answers.
If a neighbor is known to be negative, cut at that vertex and orient the order so that v is first.
The positive interval starts at v, so only its other endpoint needs a search. If both neighbors are
negative, the pattern consists of v alone and no endpoint query is needed.

    When the discovery path has no cached negative neighbor, the geometry supplies the cut. The
origin’s known-negative position ensures that a compatible positive interval cannot cross the opposite
edge.
Claim 3.9 (The opposite-edge cut costs no query). Without a cached cut, the normalized hull
supplies a cut that no compatible positive interval can wrap around.

Proof. Otherwise use a geometric cut. If the origin lies on the hull boundary, cut at its free negative
marker. If it is interior, the ray from v through 0 meets an opposite hull edge with consecutive
endpoints a, b. The origin is a convex combination of v, a, b, with positive coefficient on v. Explicitly,
the intersection point is y = −tv for some t > 0 and y = θa + (1 − θ)b, so

                                          t      θ     1−θ
                                    0=       v+     a+     b.
                                         1+t    1+t    1+t
The coefficients are nonnegative and sum to one. If a, b were both positive, then v·xh , a·xh , b·xh > 1,
giving
                                         t               θ               1−θ
                        0 = 0 · xh =        (v · xh ) +      (a · xh ) +     (b · xh )
                                      1+t               1+t              1+t
                                      t + θ + (1 − θ)
                                   >                    = 1,
                                           1+t
a contradiction. The strict step multiplies the positive test values by nonnegative coefficients; the
coefficient of v is strictly positive, so the sum is strictly greater than one even if an opposite-edge

                                                   29
coefficient vanishes. Cut the cyclic order between a and b. A circular positive interval could wrap
around this cut only if both endpoints were positive, which was just excluded. If the ray meets a
vertex y, then y · xh = −t(v · xh ) < 0 < 1 for every compatible hypothesis, so it is a free negative
cut point.

    The cut has removed wrap-around. We can now verify that learning the two endpoints amounts
to asking existing hull tests, which is essential for keeping the construction within the supplied-query
model.
Claim 3.10 (Endpoint comparisons are supplied hull queries). Both endpoint searches, including
their degenerate cases, are legal.

Proof. By Claims 3.8 and 3.9, the positive vertices now form an ordinary interval containing v,
obtained without an anchor query. To its left, querying a vertex asks whether the left endpoint is at
or before that vertex. To its right, querying a vertex asks whether the right endpoint is at or after
it. Thus supplied hull queries give the ordered comparisons needed to locate the two endpoints.
    When neither endpoint is already fixed, learn the left endpoint, then the right endpoint con-
ditioned on the left endpoint and the earlier transcript. For consecutive possible left endpoints,
querying the earlier endpoint separates them; for consecutive possible right endpoints, querying the
later endpoint separates them. These are real hull vertices, so all comparisons used by the alpha-
betic codes of Lemma 2.10 are supplied queries. The conditional endpoint distributions come from
the hypothesis table.
    If K has dimension at most one, it has at most two real vertices. With two real endpoints, one
positive endpoint forces the other negative because 0 lies between them. With one real vertex, its
positive bit is the complete nonzero pattern. Thus no further query is needed in either degenerate
case.




4    Maximum-depth scheduling ingredients
The maximum-depth algorithm of Section 8 constructs its pattern children first and then schedules
their discovery. This section isolates its local tools. The priority scheduler extends the construction
beyond the exact scheduler’s logarithmic rank cap by supplying a passing height trial. All branch
decisions use hypothesis counts and supplied-line geometry.
    Let δ ∈ (0, 1/3) be the count-balance threshold and let γ > 2 be the coefficient of the count
potential Φ(s) = ⌊γ log2 s⌋. Assume
                                            1                    1
                                  γ log2       ≥ 1,     γ log2     ≥ 5.                             (2)
                                           1−δ                   δ
The first inequality pays for a balanced query; the second pays for endpoint recovery at a flat state.
Section 8.3 chooses the constants at the point where these two credits balance.
   For a state S, write CW (S) for the height of the tree returned by the hybrid recursive algorithm.
When a proof refers to a stored child code, its height is the actual height of that code.




                                                  30
4.1      Local credits and the priority fallback

Proof strategy.

      1. Restrict an optimal tree to a pattern class: its common witness prefix disappears and gives that class
         a discovery deadline.
      2. Recover the pattern with supplied endpoint comparisons; the count-potential drop pays for this
         decoder.
      3. Use priorities to meet all deadline prefixes in one ordering. Every accepted ordering is checked
         against actual stored child heights.
   In a count-flat state S of size s, use the common witness and hull patterns of Lemmas 3.1
and 3.2. Every nonzero class A has a = |A| < δs; the zero class A0 is empty or a singleton. Write

                                              zA = ⌊log2 (δs/a)⌋.

All child heights below come from already constructed codes; the optimal tree appears only in the
comparison argument.
    The recursion needs integer query allowances, while its potential uses logarithms. The following
credit converts size reduction into a whole query at a balanced split and enough whole queries for
a flat decoder.

Proposition 4.1 (Integer count credit). A balanced child of size a ≤ (1−δ)s satisfies Φ(s)−Φ(a) ≥
1. A nonzero flat-state class satisfies

                                   Φ(s) − Φ(a) ≥ ⌊γ log2 (s/a)⌋ ≥ zA + 5.                                   (3)

   For a flat state, the optimal tree supplies an analytical comparison schedule. Its initial witness
answers are constant on a class until that class meets a support; restriction turns this delay into
saved height inside the class.

Proposition 4.2 (Supporting-prefix credit). Follow a common witness in a proper optimal tree
of height D = OPTW (S) along a path of length r. Let tA be the first path query whose real hull
supports meet the positive pattern of A; put tA = r + 1 if none does. Then

                                         OPTW (A) ≤ D − (tA − 1).

The supporting singleton or adjacent-pair blocks cover every nonzero class by its index tA , after
appending one positive support for an uncovered terminal nonzero singleton when necessary.

    A first positive answer locates one vertex of the pattern. The endpoint decoder then recovers
the full interval, with a pointwise cost bound so maximum depth controls each class separately.

Proposition 4.3 (Legal endpoint allowances). After a first positive hull answer, the pointwise
alphabetic endpoint codes recover class A with at most
                                                  (4)
                                                 eA = zA + 4

additional supplied queries. If a neighboring hull vertex is already known negative, one endpoint is
                             (2)
fixed and the allowance is eA = zA + 2. A hull of dimension at most one needs no further query
after discovery.


                                                        31
    Scheduling can now use the codes already built for the children. Adding their actual heights to
discovery and endpoint recovery gives a directly checkable route certificate, including the all-negative
route.

Proposition 4.4 (Local route-height certificate). Let hA be a stored class code’s actual height.
Discover a positive vertex in a fixed ordering truncated when all nonzero patterns are covered. If its
                                                                                               (4)
first positive position for A is gA , the whole route through A has height at most gA + hA + eA , or
             (2)
gA + hA + eA on a pinned exit. If every such bound is at most H, the whole tree has height at most
H, including its zero route when that class exists.

    The classes have different deadlines, yet the algorithm must choose one query order. Integer
priorities make later deadlines unable to interfere with greedy progress on any earlier deadline prefix.

Proposition 4.5 (One ordering for all block deadlines). Force an oriented adjacent real pair (v, w)
first. Consider only classes negative on both, with integer block deadlines dA ≥ 2. Suppose a
comparison sequence begins with {v, w} and hits A within dA singleton or adjacent-pair blocks. Sort
the distinct budgets as d(1) < · · · < d(r) , give a deadline-d(j) class weight (s + 1)r−j , and complete by
maximum-remaining-weight greedy. This polynomial-time ordering satisfies gA ≤ 3dA − 2 for every
surviving class simultaneously.

   The endpoint and prefix credits turn the optimal witness path into deadlines, and the priority
ordering meets those deadlines. It therefore provides a passing trial even when the capped exact
scheduler cannot certify the stronger bound.

Proposition 4.6 (Priority fallback height). Suppose stored pattern children satisfy the fallback
bound
                                 hA ≤ 3 OPTW (A) + Φ(|A|).
At a flat state, Algorithm 5 has a passing trial at

                                         H ∗ = 3 OPTW (S) + Φ(s).

A balanced query also fits this height if its two stored children obey the same bound. Consequently
the hybrid recursion can always retain CW (S) ≤ 3 OPTW (S) + Φ(s) as its fallback invariant.
                                                                               (4)
    For a trial H and a forced pair, first-positive exits must pass 1+hA +eA ≤ H, and second-query
                            (2)
exits must pass 2 + hA + eA ≤ H. Classes negative on both receive
                                   (4)
                     uA = hA + eA ,         BA = H − uA ,        dA = ⌊(BA + 2)/3⌋.

Reject any BA < 4 and accept only after checking the resulting actual first-hit positions against
gA + uA ≤ H. These tests describe an executable height certificate; the optimal comparison blocks
are used only to prove that some trial passes.
    For a fixed target height H, each oriented adjacent pair determines which patterns are hit at
its first endpoint, second endpoint, or neither. The first two groups have immediate local height
tests, so a pair that already exceeds H can be discarded. For patterns left on the miss route, the
remaining height becomes a deadline; the priority weights make the greedy order favor the tightest
deadlines. The final check uses the actual hit ranks and child allowances, and accepts the order
only when every miss-route pattern fits.

   Input: Flat state’s hull and classes; child heights hA ; allowances eA , eA ; target H.
   Output: A passing discovery order, or infeasible.
 1 foreach oriented adjacent real pair (v, w) do
 2     Form F, S, N by their first hit on (v, w)
                                      (4)                                       (2)
 3      if some A ∈ F has 1 + hA + eA > H, or some A ∈ S has 2 + hA + eA > H then
 4          continue
 5      foreach A ∈ N do
                       (4)
 6          uA ← hA + eA ; BA ← H − uA ; dA ← ⌊(BA + 2)/3⌋
 7      if some BA < 4 then
 8          continue
 9      Sort distinct budgets as d(1) < · · · < d(r)
10      Give each deadline-d(j) class weight (|S| + 1)r−j

          classes are hit
12      if every A ∈ N has actual gA + uA ≤ H then
13           return this order

14   return infeasible


4.2     Technical proofs of the local ingredients
Proof of Proposition 4.1. For real x, y, write x − y = k + θ with integer k = ⌊x − y⌋ and 0 ≤ θ < 1.
Then ⌊x⌋ = ⌊y + k + θ⌋ ≥ ⌊y⌋ + k, proving the floor-difference inequality. Apply it to the two count
potentials:
                                   Φ(s) − Φ(a) ≥ ⌊γ log2 (s/a)⌋ .
Indeed, the difference of the unfloored logarithms is γ log2 s − γ log2 a = γ log2 (s/a), which is the
argument to the floor-difference inequality. For a balanced child, a ≤ (1−δ)s, hence s/a ≥ 1/(1−δ).
The first condition in (2) therefore gives
                                                              1
                                     γ log2 (s/a) ≥ γ log2       ≥ 1.
                                                             1−δ
Since every real number at least one has floor at least one, the balanced-child claim follows, including
the equality case. For a flat class put x = log2 (s/a) and ℓ = log2 (1/δ). Since a < δs, we have x > ℓ.
The calibration gives γℓ = 5, so, using γ > 1,

                           γx = γℓ + γ(x − ℓ) = 5 + γ(x − ℓ) > 5 + (x − ℓ).

Monotonicity of the floor therefore gives

                           ⌊γx⌋ ≥ ⌊5 + x − ℓ⌋ = 5 + ⌊log2 (δs/a)⌋ = 5 + zA ,

which proves the flat credit including its integer rounding.

Proof of Proposition 4.2. There are two distinct steps. Common answers give a restriction credit;
supporting blocks then cover the positive classes with deadlines measured from the same witness
path. The terminal leaf needs separate attention because the witness may end at a real hypothesis
with a nonzero pattern. Claims 4.1 and 4.2 establish these two steps, respectively.


                                                    33
Claim 4.1 (The common prefix disappears under restriction). Every member of A follows the
witness’s first tA − 1 answers.

Proof. For every index i < tA , no support of the ith query meets the positive pattern of A.
Lemma 3.2 then makes its answer constant on A, with the same value as on the witness. This
includes a query whose support uses the free origin marker. Thus every member of A traverses the
same first tA − 1 nodes of the optimal tree.
    Restrict that tree to A. Since A is nonempty and each of those answers is constant, each
corresponding node contracts to its witness-side child. The remaining tree identifies A and has
height at most D − (tA − 1): each original path had length at most D, and the shared prefix has
been removed from every such path. Minimizing over identifying trees on A proves the inequality.


    The restriction credit must come with a cover that can be compared against a scheduler. Queries
along the path cover every class except, possibly, the hypothesis at its terminal leaf.
Claim 4.2 (The supporting blocks form a comparison cover). Only an uncovered terminal nonzero
singleton needs an appended block.

Proof. Each class with tA ≤ r is hit by that path query’s supports. By Claim 4.1, an unhit class
follows the entire original path and is therefore the single leaf hypothesis reached by the witness.
If its pattern is nonzero, append a positive hull vertex at index r + 1; if it is zero, the witness leaf
already completes the cover. Retaining the first occurrence of a repeated support preserves every
first-hit deadline.



Proof of Proposition 4.3. The query-free cut of Lemma 3.5 makes the positive pattern an ordinary
interval. Let R be the hypotheses compatible with the first positive transcript, so |R| < δs. Use
uniform counts, conditioned on that transcript, as code-design probabilities, while the algorithm
itself follows the deterministic construction. The two conditional endpoint probabilities multiply to
the probability of the full class. Their logarithmic query bounds therefore telescope to one bound
depending on the class size. Claims 4.3 and 4.4 establish the two allowances, according to whether
discovery has already fixed a boundary.
Claim 4.3 (Two endpoint searches telescope their probabilities). If aL compatible hypotheses have
the target’s left endpoint, its full pattern is learned in fewer than log2 (δs/a) + 4 queries.

Proof. Lemma 3.5 makes both searches legal. Applying the pointwise bound of Lemma 2.10 to the
first endpoint and then to the conditional second endpoint gives

                             |R|            aL            |R|            δs
                      log2       + 2 + log2    + 2 = log2     + 4 < log2    + 4.
                             aL              a             a             a

The logarithms add because (|R|/aL )(aL /a) = |R|/a: the second distribution is conditioned on the
learned first endpoint. The endpoint pair uniquely specifies the pattern, while the strict inequality
uses |R| < δs: the first positive transcript confines R to one minority cap. If qA is the integer
number of endpoint queries, the displayed strict bound gives qA < log2 (δs/a) + 4 < zA + 5. Thus
qA ≤ zA + 4, including when the logarithm itself is an integer.




                                                   34
    A negative neighbor supplies more information than ordinary discovery: it fixes an endpoint.
This removes an entire endpoint search and gives the extra saving needed on the second exit of the
initial pair.
Claim 4.4 (A cached negative neighbor removes one search). A pinned positive vertex fixes one
interval endpoint.

Proof. Cut at the negative neighbor and orient the order to place the positive vertex first. Lemma 3.5
fixes that boundary; hence Lemma 2.10 requires only one search to learn the other endpoint in fewer
than log2 (δs/a) + 2 queries. Writing its integer query count as qA , we have qA < log2 (δs/a) + 2 <
zA + 3, hence qA ≤ zA + 2. The negative neighbor is a real cached answer, so no anchor query is
added. In dimension at most one the positive pattern is already determined; inference and cached
answers can only lower cost.



Proof of Proposition 4.4. A route through A makes at most gA discovery queries, then uses the
endpoint allowance of Proposition 4.3, and finally follows a stored code of height hA . Its height is
their sum, bounded by H by hypothesis. The ordering is truncated as soon as all nonzero classes
are covered, namely after at most maxA̸=A0 gA queries. Since hA and both endpoint allowances are
nonnegative, the route tests imply gA ≤ H for each nonzero class. Thus the all-negative route also
has length at most H and ends at the zero singleton. If that class is absent its leaf is omitted. A
nonsingleton state has a nonzero pattern because Lemma 3.2 gives |A0 | ≤ 1.

Proof of Proposition 4.5. The proof separates priorities from geometry. First, the priorities make
the global order greedy for every nested deadline prefix. Then an interval-cover bound limits the
number of queries for each prefix.
Claim 4.5 (Later deadlines cannot change a prefix greedy choice). The global ordering is weighted
greedy for every surviving deadline prefix until that prefix is covered.

Proof. Put U = (s + 1)r−j . A class of earlier or equal deadline has weight U (s + 1)j−i for some
i ≤ j, so every candidate’s prefix score is an integer multiple of U . For j < r, all later classes
together have weight at most
                                                           s
                                      s(s + 1)r−j−1 =         U < U;
                                                         s+1
for j = r their total is zero. Deleting already hit classes can only lower these totals. If one candidate’s
prefix score exceeds another’s, it exceeds it by at least U ; no difference in their later-class scores
can reverse that comparison. Therefore a global score maximizer also maximizes the prefix score.
A nonzero prefix score is at least U and beats every zero prefix score while demands remain. Global
tie-breaking chooses among legitimate prefix greedy ties. This applies to every deadline prefix at
every step of the same order.

    The prefix argument determines which demands each greedy choice serves. To bound the number
of choices, use the forced adjacent pair to cut the line of blocks and remove its first block; the
remaining cover’s size and runs then pay for every later greedy choice.
Claim 4.6 (The first pair leaves at most 3d − 4 later greedy choices). A nonempty deadline-d prefix
finishes by actual position 3d − 2.




                                                    35
Proof. Cut at v and delete its adjacent vertex w. Surviving demands are ordinary intervals. Remove
these two vertices from the union P of the first d comparison blocks. Each of the other d − 1
blocks contributes at most three to |P | + c(P ), where c(P ) counts runs: a pair contributes at
most two vertices and one run, and a singleton at most one vertex and one run. Deleting the
two adjacent cut vertices cannot split any surviving adjacent-pair block. For finite sets U, V on
a line, |U ∪ V | ≤ |U | + |V | and c(U ∪ V ) ≤ c(U ) + c(V ): joining runs cannot create a new run.
Applying these inequalities to the remaining d − 1 blocks gives |P | + c(P ) ≤ 3(d − 1). Every prefix
demand misses the forced pair and was hit by one of these remaining blocks, so P still covers it. By
Claim 4.5, the actual later choices form a greedy sequence for this prefix. Consequently Lemma 3.4
bounds their number by
                                |P | + c(P ) − 1 ≤ 3(d − 1) − 1 = 3d − 4.
The inequality uses the size-plus-run bound just established; the equality simplifies the resulting
integer allowance. Adding the two forced queries gives position at most 3d − 2. An empty prefix
needs no further choice.

   The auxiliary weights have O(s log s) bits, and their incidence scores are computed from the
explicit class-to-vertex table. Thus the ordering and actual first-hit times have polynomial bit
complexity.

Proof of Proposition 4.6. Put D = OPTW (S). For a balanced child A, restriction and count credit,
specifically Lemma 2.12 and Proposition 4.1, give

                               OPTW (A) ≤ D,            Φ(|A|) ≤ Φ(s) − 1.

Thus its complete route satisfies

                       1 + hA ≤ 1 + 3 OPTW (A) + Φ(|A|) ≤ 3D + Φ(s) = H ∗ .

The first inequality is the stored-child invariant. The second substitutes the two preceding bounds,
so the potential drop pays for the new root query. At a flat state, enumerate an initial pair containing
the first optimal-witness query’s supports; pad a singleton support by an adjacent real neighbor.
The first two exits must be checked directly: the deadline scheduler handles only classes that miss
both initial queries. The cached negative answer makes the second positive exit cheaper to decode.
Claim 4.7 (Both initial positive exits fit H ∗ ). The first-query exit has at least one query of discovery
credit; the pinned second-query exit has at least three.

Proof. For either class, Lemma 2.12 gives OPTW (A) ≤ D. Using the stored-child invariant and
then Proposition 4.1,
                                                                               Prop. 4.1
          H ∗ − hA ≥ 3D + Φ(s) − 3 OPTW (A) − Φ(|A|) ≥ Φ(s) − Φ(|A|)              ≥        zA + 5.

The middle inequality drops the nonnegative difference 3(D − OPTW (A)). Thus the first positive
exit satisfies 1 + hA + (zA + 4) ≤ H ∗ . By Proposition 4.3, the second positive exit has a cached
negative neighbor and only the smaller allowance, so 2 + hA + (zA + 2) ≤ H ∗ − 1 ≤ H ∗ . Neither
calculation assumes that the class meets the original support; restriction and flat count credit also
cover classes hit only by the padding vertex.

    For the remaining classes, the supporting-prefix credit becomes an integer block deadline. The
floor below preserves that credit exactly, so the comparison schedule meets the deadlines computed
from actual child heights.

                                                   36
Claim 4.8 (Every later class meets its computed deadline). For a class negative on the initial pair,
dA ≥ tA .

Proof. Here tA ≥ 2, because this class misses the first query’s supports. Proposition 4.2 and the
actual child invariant give hA ≤ 3[D − (tA − 1)] + Φ(|A|). Substituting this into the budget and
then using count credit yields

                    BA = 3D + Φ(s) − hA − (zA + 4)
                        ≥ 3(tA − 1) + Φ(s) − Φ(|A|) − (zA + 4)
                        Prop. 4.1
                           ≥        3(tA − 1) + (zA + 5) − (zA + 4) = 3tA − 2 ≥ 4.

Hence (BA + 2)/3 ≥ tA ; since tA is an integer, dA = ⌊(BA + 2)/3⌋ ≥ tA . The padded first
block and the remaining comparison blocks, including the terminal singleton block if needed, satisfy
Proposition 4.5. Its ordering has

                             gA ≤ 3dA − 2 = 3⌊(BA + 2)/3⌋ − 2 ≤ BA .

The first inequality is Proposition 4.5; the equality substitutes the computed deadline. To justify
the last inequality, write BA + 2 = 3dA + rA with rA ∈ {0, 1, 2}. Then 3dA − 2 = BA − rA ≤ BA ,
                        (4)
so adding uA = hA + eA gives gA + uA ≤ H ∗ .

    The deadline argument bounds every route that uses block scheduling, including the truncated
zero route, by Proposition 4.4 and Claims 4.7–4.8. When the hull has dimension at most one, the
scheduler is unnecessary: Lemma 3.5 shows that two extreme tests determine the pattern and leave
no endpoints to decode. A nonzero class then costs at most 2 + hA . The stored-child, restriction,
and count-credit calculation from Claim 4.7 gives

                                2 + hA ≤ 3D + Φ(s) − zA − 3 ≤ H ∗ .

The second inequality uses zA ≥ 0 and the definition of H ∗ . The zero route also ends after at most
the two extreme tests. Thus the local fallback bound holds in every dimension, and induction gives
the stated invariant for the hybrid tree.


5    Scheduling the first positive answer
The maximum-depth algorithm must coordinate discovery with the heights of its child codes. Ex-
pected depth poses a different scheduling need: caps containing likely targets should be tested early,
which leads to weighted min-sum set cover (MSSC). We use the supplied caps as the covering sets,
with a singleton cap included whenever a supplied test realizes it.
    In the decision-tree application, a positive hull answer exits the all-negative route and reveals
that the target lies in a small cap. The all-zero pattern follows that route through every test; once
the schedule has covered every positive pattern, the all-zero class is the remaining outcome. An
earlier singleton inference shortens its route. Its expected-cost contribution is therefore bounded
by its weight times the schedule length. Accordingly, our ordering bound tracks both the sum of
positive-pattern first-cover times and the length paid on the all-negative route.




                                                    37
  Symbol                  Meaning
  U, πh , p0              Coverable positive-pattern demands, their positive weights, and the weight of
                          a target found only after the schedule finishes.             P
  tQ (h), MSSC(Q)         First-cover position of demand h in ordering Q and the sum h πh tQ (h).
  k0                      Minimum number of candidate tests needed to cover all demands in U .
  β, Φβ , Zβ              Geometric budget factor and the first-cover and total-length accounting func-
                          tions.
  rQ (x), Gu              Comparison-order mass with first-cover time greater than x, and the con-
                          structed schedule for shift u.

    Weighted MSSC has a classical greedy four-approximation [1]. For circular intervals, we obtain
a stronger guarantee by first solving the maximum-coverage problem exactly for each cardinality
budget. Ordering these budget-optimal sets in geometric batches then controls both first-cover cost
and total length.
    This batch method extends minimum cover by optimizing covered weight at every integer budget.
The interval structure makes each optimization tractable: once the previous selected position is
known, the next position determines the newly covered demands.

Lemma 5.1 (Exact budgeted interval coverage). For positively weighted interval or circular-interval
demands on a finite candidate set, a set of at most k candidates hitting maximum demand weight
is computable in polynomial time.

Proof. Algorithm 6 gives both interval oracles. For ordinary intervals, number the candidates
1, . . . , M . If the previous selected candidate is p and the next is q > p, the newly hit demands are
the intervals containing q whose left endpoint is greater than p. An interval containing q and an
earlier selected point must also contain p. Conversely, any interval containing q with left endpoint
greater than p contains no earlier selected candidate. Thus the added weight g(p, q) is determined
by the two positions p and q.
      To maximize covered weight among selections of t candidates ending at q, use the recurrence

                       D(t, q) = max{D(t − 1, p) + g(p, q)},        D(0, 0) = 0.
                                  p<q

Here D(t − 1, p) records the largest weight covered by a selection of t − 1 candidates whose last
position is p. The preceding characterization shows that appending q adds g(p, q), so the recurrence
considers every possible penultimate position and loses no feasible selection. Taking the maximum
over final positions and all t ≤ k therefore gives the best cover using at most k candidates. We
reserve position 0 as a sentinel, an artificial initial marker before every interval and outside the
selectable candidates. It supplies the empty selection as the base case; other nonexistent states
have value −∞, so they cannot enter a maximizing transition. Since there are O(kM ) states and
each maximization checks at most M predecessors, this is a polynomial dynamic program.
    For circular intervals and k ≥ 1, enumerate a selected candidate v. Credit the demands it hits
and delete them. Cutting at v turns the remaining demands into ordinary intervals, on which the
dynamic program selects at most k − 1 more candidates. One enumeration contains a candidate of
an optimal nonempty selection, so taking the best is exact. The k = 0 case is empty.




                                                   38
 Algorithm 6: IntervalCover: exact minimum cover or budgeted coverage
   Input: Ordered interval demands with weights; either minimum or budget k; ordinary or circular
            order.
   Output: A minimum hitting set, or a maximum-weight set of size at most k.
 1 if the demand family is empty then

 2     return ∅
 3   if mode is minimum and an empty demand exists then
 4       return infeasible
 5   if mode is budget coverage and k = 0 then
 6       return ∅
 7 if order is circular then
 8     Enumerate a forced selected candidate v; delete its hit demands and cut there
 9     Solve each residual ordinary instance, with budget k − 1 in budget mode
10     return the best residual solution plus v, also considering the empty budget solution
11 if mode is minimum then
12     P ←∅
13     while a demand remains do
14        Choose the right endpoint q of an earliest-ending remaining demand
15        P ← P ∪ {q}; delete all demands containing q
16     return P
17 else
18     Set D(0, 0) = 0 and all other unreachable states to −∞
19     Precompute g(p, q): weight of intervals containing q with left endpoint > p
20     for t = 1, . . . , min{k, M } do
21         for q = 1, . . . , M do
22             D(t, q) ← maxp<q (D(t − 1, p) + g(p, q)); store a maximizing p

23      return the reconstruction of the best state with t ≤ k, including (0, 0)

    For minimum circular cover the forced candidate need only range over one nonempty demand.
For budget coverage, empty demands contribute zero covered weight. The empty family returns the
empty set in both modes. Lemmas 3.3 and 5.1 establish exactness.
    We now turn these exact budgeted covers into an ordering. Geometrically increasing budgets
let small batches find likely targets early, while the minimum cover at the final budget ensures
that every positive pattern is eventually found. A shift changes when the sequence crosses integer
budgets; averaging over shifts bounds both positive first-cover times and the wait on the all-negative
route. Since only finitely many distinct schedules arise, we can then select one deterministically.

5.1    The batch-ordering guarantee
Fix a nonempty demand family U , its minimum cover size k0 , and the waiting weight p0 . The only
independent scheduling parameter is the growth factor β > 1. The functions below express the
shift-averaged bounds on first-cover cost and schedule length. Once β and the current state are
fixed, both are determined.
    Put
                                              β+1                   1
                          rβ = ⌊β⌋,      λβ =        ,   νβ = 1 +      .
                                              2 ln β              ln β




                                                     39
For integer t, k ≥ 1, define the accounting functions

                                       rβ + 1 ln(rβ !)
                              Φβ (1) =         −          ,
                                          2        2 ln β
                                                        Z t
                                                    1       ⌊x⌋ + ⌊βx⌋

    We first derive a bound for each shifted schedule. Keeping the integer budgets in this calcula-

Each such schedule has length at most (1 + β/(β − 1))k0 .

    A shift that finds likely positive targets early can still leave a long all-negative route. We
therefore average the first-cover cost and the schedule length under one distribution, so a single
shift can balance both contributions.

Proposition 5.3 (One shift distribution bounds both costs). For the log-uniform shift on [1, β),
                                    X
                  E[MSSC(Gu )] ≤        πh Φβ (tQ (h)),  E[|Gu |] ≤ Zβ (k0 ).
                                          h

They also satisfy Φβ (t) ≤ λβ (t − 1) + Φβ (1) and Zβ (k) ≤ νβ k − 1/ ln β.

    The average guarantees a schedule with a suitably small joint cost. Because integer budgets
change only at explicit breakpoints, a finite search over the resulting schedules can find one no more
costly than the average.

Proposition 5.4 (A finite shift search suffices). For fixed β, polynomially many shifts represent
every distinct Gu . Minimizing the joint cost MSSC(Gu ) + p0 |Gu | over these representatives attains
at most its log-uniform average.

Proof strategy.

   1. Exact budget coverage bounds the mass left after each phase.
   2. Greedy order within a phase gives a half-batch saving in first-cover cost.
   3. Average over the same shift for discovery and waiting, then choose the best of finitely many distinct
      schedules.

The shift averaging establishes the bound; the finite search returns a deterministic schedule.

Lemma 5.5 (Geometric-batch ordering). For every fixed β > 1, the following guarantee holds.
Let U be a nonempty family of coverable weighted circular intervals. Let k0 be its minimum cover
size and let p0 ≥ 0 be the weight of a target covered only when the entire schedule finishes. A
deterministic polynomial-time ordering G satisfies, for every comparison cover ordering Q,
                                             X
                        MSSC(G) + p0 |G| ≤       πh Φβ (tQ (h)) + p0 Zβ (k0 ).
                                                    h∈U

The simpler bounds Φβ (t) ≤ λβ (t − 1) + Φβ (1) and Zβ (k) ≤ νβ k − 1/ ln β also hold.
                                                                                      Every candidate
schedule considered by the construction also has length at most 1 + β/(β − 1) k0 .

Proof. Proposition 5.2 bounds each shifted schedule. Proposition 5.3 then bounds their joint av-
erage, and Proposition 5.4 selects a schedule whose joint cost is no larger. Every candidate also
satisfies the length bound, so the selected schedule inherits it.

5.2      Proofs of the batch-ordering propositions

Proof strategy.

      1. Compare each budgeted batch with the first equally sized prefix of an arbitrary cover ordering, and
         bound the total number of scheduled tests by the geometric budget sum.
      2. Use greedy order within each batch to bound its first-cover contribution using the half-batch saving.
      3. Sum these batch costs, then replace batch residuals by the comparison ordering’s residual mass to
         obtain the discrete bound.

Proof of Proposition 5.2. First use Lemma 3.3 to compute a minimum cover Q∗ , and write k0 =
|Q∗ |. For each integer budget k < k0 , Lemma 5.1 supplies a maximum-weight set Qk of at most



                                                       41
k candidates. Fixing these sets deterministically lets each phase use its best coverage set for that
budget; the final phase uses Q∗ and therefore covers every demand.
   Fix a comparison cover ordering Q, and write
                                                   X
                                       rQ (x) =           πh

for the weight whose first-cover index in Q exceeds x. For a shift u ∈ [1, β), use budgets k (j) = ⌊β j u⌋,
j = 0, 1, . . .. In each phase, process Qk(j) when k (j) < k0 and Q∗ otherwise. Order that set greedily
by the weight it covers among the demands still unhit by earlier queries. Omit repeated queries
and those hitting no remaining demand. Stop after the first phase whose budget reaches k0 . Two
global safeguards come first: each phase covers at least as much weight as a comparison prefix with
the same budget, and geometric growth bounds the total number of tests.
Claim 5.1 (Phase coverage and schedule length). After a phase with budget k < k0 , the weight still
unhit is at most rQ (k). The complete shifted schedule has length at most 1 + β/(β − 1) k0 .

Proof. The union of all queries processed through this phase covers every demand covered by Qk :
a query omitted for hitting no remaining demand has its covered demands already hit by an earlier
phase. By Lemma 5.1, the set Qk covers at least as much total weight as the first k queries of Q,
since those queries are one feasible at-most-k candidate set. Hence the schedule has hit at least the
weight hit by that comparison prefix, leaving at most rQ (k). The final Q∗ phase covers all demands.

Proof strategy.

   1. Average the discrete cost bound under the log-uniform shift; compute its initial floor term and
      combine the scale intervals into one integral, which yields Φβ .
   2. Apply ⌊x⌋ ≤ x for the simpler linear bound.
   3. Average the same shift’s earlier batch lengths, evaluate the floor integral by a factorial sum, and use
      the integral lower bound on ln(k!) to obtain the stated bound on Zβ .

                                                            45
Proof of Proposition 5.4. No random shift is used by the algorithm. Let J be the first integer with
β J ≥ k0 . All budget changes occur at the breakpoints u = k/β j for 0 ≤ j ≤ J, 1 ≤ k ≤ k0 , inside
[1, β). Enumerate these breakpoints and one interior point from each consecutive cell, construct the
resulting orderings, and take the one minimizing the rational quantity MSSC(Gu ) + p0 |Gu |. There
are O(k0 ) distinct intervals: for a fixed integer k, a breakpoint k/β j belongs to [1, β) exactly when
β j ≤ k < β j+1 . These half-open ranges partition [1, ∞), so at most one exponent qualifies for each
k. Adding the boundary shifts and one midpoint per gap still leaves O(k0 ) representatives. Also
J = Oβ (1+log k0 ). Every constructed ordering uses distinct candidates and hence has length at most
|V |. Evaluate the joint score MSSC(Gu ) + p0 |Gu | for each representative and return the schedule
with minimum score. The breakpoint and midpoint construction leaves only O(k0 ) schedules to
compare. The budgets, selected sets, and their greedy internal orders are constant on each interval
because their only dependence on u is through the integer budgets, with fixed tie-breaking thereafter.
Hence the finite family represents every schedule occurring in the average of Proposition 5.3: within
each cell, the budget vector, selected sets, and deterministic orders agree with the representative’s.
Thus every schedule in the average has one of the listed joint scores, and the minimum listed score
is at most their weighted average. The waiting-time weight p0 is used only to choose the schedule;
all queries remain supplied candidates.


6     A polynomial approximation for expected depth
We give one algorithm for arbitrary positive rational priors. The prior is fixed throughout the
construction: a query’s expected cost is the mass of hypotheses that reach it. At a state admitting
a balanced supplied line, the algorithm queries that line. At a flat state, it tests hull blocks until
a positive answer is found or a full negative cover identifies the zero-pattern singleton. After a
positive answer it learns the complete hull pattern and recurses within that pattern class. Its
polynomial-time factor is below 5.182976.
    Two costs need to be controlled at a flat state: finding a positive block and recovering the rest
of its pattern. The useful comparison is an entire pattern stage. Queries that become constant after
the pattern class is known provide credit for discovery. Information already learned during discovery
reduces the entropy still needed for endpoint recovery. Charging the stage to these two decreases
lets us sum the bound over the recursive tree. The geometric and interval-coverage primitives were
proved in Sections 3 and 5.

6.1   The algorithm and its supporting propositions
Use fixed calibration parameters β, δ, p∗ , whose values are deferred to Section 6.3. Here 3 < β < 4,
0 < δ < 1/3, and 0 < p∗ < 1/2. A line is mass-balanced if each side has mass at least δw(S).
Otherwise the common-witness construction supplies the normalized hull. Keep its full cyclic order,
including the constant-negative origin marker when it is a hull vertex. Candidate blocks are its
edges: each block uses only its one or two real supplied endpoint tests.
    Write Π for the full-pattern partition and A0 for its all-negative class, which is empty or a
singleton. Throughout this section the prior weights retain their original normalization:

         w = w(S),       p0 = w(A0 ),      b = w − p0 ,     I = I(S, Π),      ∆ = ∆E (S, Π).

The quantity I is the information needed to learn the pattern; ∆ is the optimal cost made unnec-
essary after its class is known. These are comparison quantities used in the analysis; the algorithm
chooses its schedule from observable pattern masses and coverage scores.


                                                  46
Choose the coverage schedule. Small budgets locate common positive patterns quickly; larger
budgets eventually cover every positive pattern. Exact budgeted coverage chooses the blocks, while
greedy ordering places their largest new masses first. We vary the first two budgets because these
early decisions have the greatest effect on expected cost. Compute a maximum-weight block cover
Qk for each budget k < kmin and a minimum full cover Q∗ of size kmin . To justify these DPs,
Lemma 3.2 gives each positive pattern one circular vertex interval. The hull edges incident to that
interval form one circular interval in the edge order, so Lemmas 3.3 and 5.1 apply to the block
demands. For a geometric shift u, set kj = ⌊β j u⌋. Try the following initial budget sequences,
followed by the common geometric tail:

                 q, r, k2 , k3 , . . . , 1 ≤ q ≤ 6,   r ∈ {k1 − 1, k1 , k1 + 1},   q < r < k2 ,
                 q, k2 , k3 , . . . ,    1 ≤ q ≤ 6.

At each budget k < kmin , greedily append productive unused blocks of Qk , ordering them by newly
covered weight. At the first budget reaching kmin , use Q∗ and finish. There are at most twenty-four
variants per shift.
    Refine the usual geometric shift partition by the constant set
                                      k k
                                       ,   ∈ [1, β),          1 ≤ k ≤ ⌈β 3 ⌉.                        (4)
                                      β β2
Represent each open cell by any point in it, and also include each left breakpoint. Thus initial bud-
gets remain represented even when kmin truncates the ordinary geometric partition. In particular,
r = k1 − 1 can first complete the cover at k1 = kmin + 1.

Choose the two-endpoint mode. Finding a positive block leaves a choice: stop at its first
positive endpoint, or ask the other endpoint as well. The extra answer can save later decoding
cost, so comparing only the number of discovery queries would miss its benefit. The following score
includes both the immediate query saving and a guaranteed information credit. On an all-negative
schedule prefix, let x ≤ y be the newly hit masses exclusive to the two endpoints of the next block,
and let z be their shared mass. Put h∗ = 1 − 2p∗ and define the rational entropy credit and saving

                             E∗ (a, z) = min{(h∗ /p∗ )a, h∗ (a + z), (h∗ /p∗ )z},
                                                                                                     (5)
                           D(x, y, z) = max{y, x + y − z + E∗ (y, z)}.

The first choice stops at the first positive answer, asking the smaller exclusive mass first. The second
choice asks both endpoints, asking the larger exclusive mass first. The latter order gives at least as
much credited information because each of the three affine terms defining E∗ (a, z) is nondecreasing
in a, and so is their pointwise minimum. Choose the endpoint with the larger saving. The exact
binary-entropy bound behind E∗ is checked only in the final calibration.
    For a candidate schedule G, let kA be its first block hitting positive class A, and set
                                           X                        X
                             F∗ (G) = 2        w(A)kA + 2p0 |G| −        D.                           (6)
                                             A̸=A0                        blocks

This one score prices positive discovery, the entire zero-pattern waiting route, and the mode savings.
Choose its minimum. At each positive exit, build the legal expected-cost endpoint decoder and
attach the recursive tree for its full pattern class.




                                                         47
 Algorithm 8: Rational choice between stopping and querying both endpoints
   Input: New exclusive masses x, y, shared mass z; calibrated p∗ and h∗ = 1 − 2p∗ .
   Output: Stored endpoint order, mode, and rational saving D.
 1 Label the endpoints so x ≤ y
 2 E ← min{(h∗ /p∗ )y, h∗ (y + z), (h∗ /p∗ )z}

 3 if y ≥ x + y − z + E then
 4     Record stop mode with the x endpoint first; D ← y
 5 else
 6     Record query-both mode with the y endpoint first; D ← x + y − z + E
 7   return the recorded order, mode, and D

    These new masses are computed on the all-negative prefix before the block. In the stored
discovery stage, stop mode ends at the first positive answer; query-both mode learns both bits
before endpoint recovery. Their union and first-cover rank are the same.

 Algorithm 9: ExpectedTree: the polynomial expected-depth algorithm
   Input: State S, original positive rational priors; calibrated β, δ, p∗ .
   Output: A supplied-query identification tree.
 1 if |S| ≤ 1 then
 2     return its leaf
 3   if a mass-balanced supplied line ℓ exists then
 4       return ℓ with children ExpectedTree(Sℓ− ) and ExpectedTree(Sℓ+ )
 5 Compute HullData(S), its pattern classes, and zero weight p0 = w(A0 )
 6 if there are at most two real extreme hull tests then
 7     Build their direct full-pattern stage
 8 else

 9     Use edges of the full hull as blocks, retaining its free origin marker
10     Select the schedule and stored modes by Algorithm 10
11     Build discovery along its all-negative prefix, with those modes on each first hit
12     At each positive exit build expected EndpointCode to recover the full pattern
13 Attach ExpectedTree(A) to each nonzero pattern class A
14 Use a zero-query leaf for the zero singleton; omit it when absent
15 return the tree after contracting constant and cached questions


   The cases in the algorithm have a direct purpose: singleton states already identify their hy-
pothesis, balanced lines split immediately, and flat states use the hull schedule to discover a full
pattern before recursing within its class. Each recursive call reuses the fixed calibration and full
supplied-query family. Since the incidence table and prior weights are known, schedule scores, end-
point modes, and child codes can all be determined before the hidden hypothesis is queried; the
routine therefore returns a concrete tree for it to follow.




                                                    48
 Algorithm 10: Two-budget schedule selection for ExpectedTree
   Input: Positive-pattern block demands, original weights, zero weight p0 ; calibrated β, p∗ .
   Output: A schedule and stored modes minimizing the rational score F∗ .
 1 Compute Q∗ of size kmin and every Qk for k < kmin
 2 Refine the geometric shift cells by (4)

 3 foreach breakpoint or one interior representative u ∈ [1, β) do
 4     k1 ← ⌊βu⌋; k2 ← ⌊β 2 u⌋
 5     for q = 1, . . . , 6 do
 6         V ← {(q, 0)} ∪ {(q, r) : r ∈ {k1 − 1, k1 , k1 + 1}, q < r < k2 }
 7         foreach (q, r) ∈ V do
 8             G ← empty order
 9             Process q, then r if r > 0, then k2 , k3 , . . .
10             At each budget k, greedily append productive unused blocks from Qk if k < kmin , and
                from Q∗ otherwise
11             Stop these phases at the first budget reaching kmin
12             Compute the new masses and stored mode of each block by Algorithm 8
13             Retain (G, modes) with its rational score F∗ (G)

14   return a retained pair of minimum score

Figure 4: The polynomial expected-depth algorithm first computes hull patterns and their block
demands, then uses interval coverage to build candidate schedules and selects one by its rational
score. Solid arrows show this construction-time data flow, using the supplied incidence table and
prior. The chosen stage is then stored as a query tree: dashed arrows trace the unknown hypothesis
through discovery, endpoint decoding, and its already constructed recursive child. The adaptive
mode decides whether discovery stops at the first positive endpoint or queries both endpoints. The
algorithm makes this schedule choice deterministically; the certificate’s mixture is used to prove its
cost bound.
    The analysis follows the same separation between discovery and pattern recovery. First we
extract a benchmark from an optimal tree. Then we bound the cost of a stage with a given schedule,
compare the chosen schedule with that benchmark, and sum stage bounds over the recursion. The

                                                  49
next four propositions state these steps; their proofs follow the theorem. Recall the binary entropy
h from Definition 2.7, and define
                      σ(δ) = −kδ δ ln δ − rδ ln rδ ,     kδ = ⌊1/δ⌋,   rδ = 1 − kδ δ,
with 0 ln 0 = 0. The latter is the minimum entropy of a fully capped mass distribution, including
its possible remainder.
    For analysis, follow the common witness through an optimal tree. Each pattern stays on that
path until a supporting hull test hits it; once the pattern is known, its earlier queries can be omitted.
The first proposition quantifies this restriction credit and translates the witness path into adjacent
hull blocks for comparison with our schedule.
Proposition 6.1 (Witness-path restriction credit). Follow the common witness through an optimal
tree, along a path of length ℓ. For each pattern class A, let tA be the first path query whose hull
supports meet its positive pattern; put tA = ℓ + 1 if none do. Then
                                          X
                                    ∆≥        w(A)(tA − 1) + p0 ℓ.                              (7)
                                          A̸=A0
                                                P
The corresponding positive witness masses mt = A̸=A0 :tA =t w(A) sum to b and satisfy mt ≤ 2δw.
An edge comparison ordering hits every positive class by rank tA . If A0 exists, it covers all positive
classes with at most ℓ blocks, so kmin ≤ ℓ.
    Next we price an actual stage. The rational score accounts for discovery and endpoint-mode
savings, while the information term accounts for the remaining decoding. We deliberately retain
only a coarsened transcript: every positive cell then lies inside one small cap, which gives a uniform
entropy lower bound.
    Write Wpat (S) for the original-prior expected number of queries used by this stage to discover
the full pattern and decode its endpoints. It excludes the recursive child trees and includes the
all-negative route when the zero-pattern class is present.
Proposition 6.2 (Pattern-stage cost and discovery information). For every candidate schedule and
its selected modes, the full-pattern stage uses only supplied queries and satisfies
                                                I − J∗
                          Wpat (S) ≤ F∗ (G) + 3b +     ,     0 ≤ J∗ ≤ I,                      (8)
                                                  ln 2
where J∗ is the information of a coarsened discovery transcript. Every positive coarsened exit is
contained in one hull cap, and hence
                                          J∗ ≥ bσ(δ).                                         (9)
The zero transcript is the zero-pattern singleton when it exists.
    Exact coverage connects the optimal witness ranks to the actual schedule. Proposition 5.3
bounds the average joint score of shifted schedules, and Proposition 5.4 shows that the finite search
returns one whose score is no larger than that average. The next proposition converts this schedule
comparison into a local stage bound; its coefficient B is calibrated after the proof.
Proposition 6.3 (Polynomial schedule and the local bound). With the final calibration, the two-
budget schedule algorithm is polynomial in the input and rational bit length. At every flat state,
including a degenerate hull, its full-pattern stage satisfies
                                                        I − J∗           β+1
                           Wpat (S) ≤ A∆ + Bb +                ,   A=         ,                     (10)
                                                          ln 2           ln β
where B is the positive-mass coefficient certified in Section 6.3.

                                                       50
    The local bound still contains positive mass and residual information. The capped-transcript
entropy absorbs the mass term, leaving a charge to restriction and information decreases alone.
Those decreases telescope over the recursive pattern classes.
Proposition 6.4 (Stage-to-tree conversion). Suppose flat stages satisfy W ≤ A∆+Bb+(I −J)/ ln 2
and 0 ≤ J ≤ I, J ≥ bσ(δ), with nonnegative A, B. A recursion using those stages and mass-balanced
queries has
                cE (T (S)) ≤ A OPTE (S) + cE(S),      c = max{1/h(δ), B/σ(δ)}.
Consequently its ratio is at most A + c ln 2.

Proof strategy.
Expected-depth proof roadmap.
      1. Replace an optimal witness query by its adjacent hull supports. Restriction pays for the resulting
         first-hit ranks.
      2. Choose whether the first positive block stops early or learns both endpoint bits. Its recorded infor-
         mation reduces the endpoint-decoding cost.
      3. Exact coverage transfers witness prefix mass to actual batches. A fixed certificate pays for changed
         initial ranks and for the complete zero waiting route.
      4. Bound every stage by its restriction and entropy decreases, then telescope. Numerical tuning is
         deferred until after these proofs.
   These propositions reduce the overall guarantee to a local schedule bound and a final choice of
constants. The theorem’s proof only combines those ingredients; the geometric, information, and
schedule arguments are established separately below.
Theorem 6.5 (Polynomial expected-depth approximation). For a separating supplied family of
binary unit-cost planar line queries and arbitrary strictly positive rational priors, ExpectedTree
runs in deterministic polynomial time and returns

                      cE (T (S)) ≤ A OPTE (S) + cE(S),           A + c ln 2 < 5.182976.

Here c = max{1/h(δ), B/σ(δ)}. Its running-time exponent is independent of the smallest prior.
Proof. Proposition 6.3 gives the local stage bound; Proposition 6.2 gives its discovery-information
lower bound. Proposition 6.4 sums them over the recursion. The calibration in Section 6.3 gives
the stated factor. Each nonsingleton stage partitions its state into proper disjoint classes, so the
polynomial stage constructions form at most n − 1 recursive stages.

6.2      Proofs of the supporting propositions

    The prefix credit comes from supplied queries on an optimal-tree path. Replace each query by
