Claim 6.2 (Adjacent comparison blocks cover witness prefixes). Replace each witness query by a
full-hull edge containing its one or two real supports. A singleton support may use either incident
edge. This comparison hits every class by its index tA .

Proof. By Lemma 3.2, the one or two real supports fit in one hull edge. Enlarging the support set
to that edge cannot delay a hit. Removing repeated or unproductive edges improves ranks further.
If the terminal singleton has a nonzero pattern and remains unhit, append an edge incident to one
of its positive vertices; its rank is at most ℓ + 1 = tA . If A0 exists, all positive classes were already
hit within the ℓ witness blocks, by Claim 6.1. Each ordinary first-hit mass lies in the union of the
supports of that path query. Writing these caps as Cv , Cv′ , and allowing one to be absent,

                     mt ≤ w((Cv ∪ Cv′ ) ∩ S) ≤ w(Cv ∩ S) + w(Cv′ ∩ S) ≤ 2δw.

The first inequality puts each rank-t class in at least one support cap. The second is the union
bound, and the last uses mass-flatness, since both hull vertices are supplied tests. An appended
      P singleton lies in one such cap, so its mass is at most δw. The classes partition S \ A0 ,
terminal
hence t mt = b. If A0 exists, every positive class leaves its optimal path before the zero leaf; the
at-most-ℓ comparison blocks therefore give a full cover and kmin ≤ ℓ. This proves all remaining
statements of the proposition. The algorithm constructs its own order; we use the witness order
only to compare costs.




                                                      52
Proof of Proposition 6.2. There are three parts to the stage accounting. Count the physical
queries in each endpoint mode, credit the additional information without counting it twice, and
lower-bound the information retained in capped discovery exits. Throughout, first-hit ranks refer
to the actual candidate schedule.

Claim 6.3 (Two endpoint modes have explicit physical costs). A positive class first hit by block
k has nonentropy allowance 2k + 3. Stop mode saves y weighted queries; query-both mode saves
x + y − z before accounting for its additional information.

Proof. At most 2(k − 1) tests precede the hitting block. In stop mode the first-positive route uses
one discovery query and the two legal endpoint codes of Lemmas 3.5 and 2.10, with overhead four.
The second-positive route uses two discovery queries, but its adjacent negative answer pins one
boundary, leaving only overhead two. Relative to 2k + 3, exactly the second endpoint’s exclusive
mass y saves one query. In query-both mode each exclusive route uses two discovery queries and one
endpoint code, for allowance 2k + 2; the shared route uses two queries and two codes, for allowance
2k + 4. Their weighted saving is x + y − z. A free origin endpoint or a cached answer can only
lower these costs. Finish the recorded mode before singleton inference in the analysis, then contract
constant questions in the constructed tree. Here are the allowances before the conditional entropy
term is added; each endpoint code contributes overhead at most two:

                   mode and exit      discovery codes         allowance
                 stop: first positive     1       2   2(k − 1) + 1 + 4 = 2k + 3
                stop: second positive     2       1   2(k − 1) + 2 + 2 = 2k + 2
                   both: exclusive        2       1   2(k − 1) + 2 + 2 = 2k + 2
                    both: shared          2       2   2(k − 1) + 2 + 4 = 2k + 4

For m = x + y + z, the stop allowance is consequently

                           (x + z)(2k + 3) + y(2k + 2) = (2k + 3)m − y,

whereas the query-both allowance is

                      (x + y)(2k + 2) + z(2k + 4) = (2k + 3)m − (x + y − z).

    The second endpoint can reduce the information the decoder must learn, so the stage measure
accounts for both query count and information. Temporarily forgetting that answer creates a
coarsened transcript whose entropy loss measures the benefit exactly and whose positive cells remain
inside individual caps.

Claim 6.4 (Coarsening accounts for the additional information once). In a query-both block, forget

Claim 6.5 (Capped discovery exits have entropy proportional to positive mass). A probability vector
with one unrestricted entry p0 and all remaining entries at most δ has entropy at least (1 − p0 )σ(δ).

      throughout each interval.

Proof of Proposition 6.3. The next claims separate actual coverage and mode savings from
their optimal-witness comparison. Actual first-hit ranks price the constructed schedule; witness
ranks provide restriction credit. Exact coverage is the connection between them.
    The exact mode saving depends on how a block’s mass is split between its endpoints. For a
batch comparison we need bounds depending only on its total newly hit mass and the cap size. Two
affine minorants of the saving provide that simpler form.

Claim 6.6 (Block mode savings have two affine lower bounds). For newly hit mass m = x + y + z,
with endpoint caps bounded by δw, the selected saving satisfies
                                                                             2 − p∗              2
          D ≥ 0,       D ≥ m − δw,           D ≥ c∗ m − d∗ δw,        c∗ =          ,   d∗ =          .

                                                         56
It is immediate if m ≤ 1, because c∗ m − d∗ ≤ c∗ − d∗ = −p∗ /(1 − p∗ ) < 0. The two cap bounds

z = m − x − y. The cap inequalities and the chosen endpoint order become
                           x ≥ m − 1,            y ≥ m − 1,            x ≤ y,    x + y ≤ m.
Specifically, y + z ≤ 1 gives x ≥ m − 1, x + z ≤ 1 gives y ≥ m − 1, and z ≥ 0 gives x + y ≤ m.
Thus the feasible (x, y) triangle has vertices (m − 1, m − 1), (m − 1, 1), and (m/2, m/2). The
concave expression x + y − z + E∗ (x, z) is a lower bound on the selected query-both saving, since
E∗ (y, z) ≥ E∗ (x, z). Concavity follows because, after substituting z, it is a linear term plus the
minimum of three affine functions. The selected saving D dominates it by (5). At the two vertices
with z = 0 it equals m; at the other it is
                     f (m) = 3m − 4 + min{(h∗ /p∗ )(m − 1), h∗ , (h∗ /p∗ )(2 − m)}.

scale δw multiplies both m and the saving by that scale, proving the last affine bound.

Bq,r is nonnegative. Hence this invoice is nondecreasing in t, and comparison edges that hit earlier
than tA , as allowed by Claim 6.2, only improve the bound. Early completion removes nonnegative
continuation terms. For the negative route and k = kmin , the full budget invoices are
                                                                    X
                      Lold (u, k) = k + k0 1{k0 <k} + k1 1{k1 <k} +   kj 1{kj <k} ,
                                                                       j≥2
                                                                X
                       Lq,r (u, k) = k + q1{q<k} + r1{r<k} +           kj 1{kj <k} .
                                                                 j≥2

The final minimum cover contributes the same k; a budget below k contributes at most its full size.
Subtracting now cancels both the final cover and the common tail, so Lq,r − Lold = zq,r (u, k). The
r term is zero for r = 0. If an initial budget already reaches k, all later budgets are larger and their
indicators vanish, so this formula also includes early minimum-cover completion. Neither change is
assumed nonnegative.

                                                      58
    After averaging schedules, each witness rank will have a credit coefficient. Most coefficients
meet a common threshold, and the cap bound limits the mass at any exceptional rank. The next
calculation combines these facts without assuming that all mass is positive: its final term explicitly
retains the zero prior.

here and defer every numerical check to Section 6.3.

    Finally, the averaging argument must describe schedules the algorithm actually tries. Shift cells
give a finite family of representatives, including when a small initial budget completes the cover
early. Hulls with too few tests for the block argument are handled directly and satisfy the same
local bound.

Claim 6.10 (Degenerate hulls and finite shift enumeration preserve the bound). The direct stage
and the finite shift family are polynomial, including all terminal cases.

Proof. With at most two real extreme hull tests, query them directly to learn the full pattern, at
cost at most 2w. In a nonsingleton state with p0 > 0, the witness path has ℓ ≥ 1, so Proposition 6.1
gives ∆ ≥ p0 ; otherwise b = w. The calibrated A, B > 2 imply

                              2w = 2b + 2p0 ≤ Bb + Ap0 ≤ Bb + A∆,

where ∆ ≥ 0 = p0 if the zero class is absent. Here J∗ = I, so the residual-information term is zero.
    For a nondegenerate hull, all coverage calls are polynomial interval DPs by Lemmas 3.3 and 5.1.
Proposition 5.4 gives polynomially many geometric shift cells; (4) adds only a constant number.
More explicitly, for any fixed β > 1, there are O(log kmin ) geometric scales, and for each integer k
at most one exponent places k/β j in [1, β). The initial refinement adds at most 2⌈β 3 ⌉ breakpoints,
a constant for fixed β. The algorithm enumerates each breakpoint and one interior representative
per cell; all such representatives give the same integer budgets within the cell. Within a refined
cell every executed budget, candidate order, mode, and score is fixed. Thus every schedule in the
proof average has an actual enumerated representative. There are at most twenty-four variants per
cell. Prior weights enter only sums and comparisons: for rational input priors, a sum of at most

                                                       61
n input values has a denominator dividing the product of their denominators and therefore has
polynomial encoding length. The fixed rational coefficients in E∗ , D, and F∗ preserve that bound.
No logarithm or proof distribution is computed. Empty positive demands occur only at a terminal
singleton. Early inferences, cached questions, free origin markers, and empty branches can only
reduce the constructed cost.
   This completes Proposition 6.3.

Proof of Proposition 6.4. We use one charge for both kinds of recursive step. A balanced query
already earns enough information to pay for itself. A flat stage earns restriction credit as well, and
its capped discovery information absorbs the positive-mass term. Once both steps have this form,
summing over the recursion requires no further geometric or scheduling argument.
Claim 6.11 (Each stage is paid for by two potential decreases). Every balanced query or flat stage
costs at most A∆ + cI.
Proof. A balanced query has information at least w(S)h(δ), because its normalized side mass lies
in [δ, 1 − δ] and the concave, symmetric binary entropy is minimized there at an endpoint. Thus
                                         I≥w(S)h(δ)    I        Lem. 2.12
                         W = w(S)            ≤             ≤ cI    ≤      A∆ + cI;
                                                      h(δ)
the final step uses A ≥ 0 and the nonnegative restriction credit. Also Lemma 2.9 gives h(δ) ≤ ln 2,
so c ≥ 1/h(δ) ≥ 1/ ln 2. For a flat stage, J ≥ bσ(δ) and c ≥ B/σ(δ) give Bb ≤ cJ. Since I − J ≥ 0,
                                    assumed flat-stage bound     I −J
                                W             ≤                A∆ + Bb +
                                                                  ln 2
                                    ≤ A∆ + cJ + c(I − J) = A∆ + cI.
The second inequality substitutes Bb ≤ cJ and 1/ ln 2 ≤ c; the latter can multiply I − J without
changing the inequality because I − J ≥ 0. The last equality then recombines the information
already learned and the information still needed. Thus the argument uses only this proposition’s
assumptions, and applies to any stages satisfying them.
    Each stage partitions its current state into the classes on which the recursion continues. Thus
the two local credits are decreases of the same potentials before and after that partition. Summing
them cancels all internal-state terms and leaves only the root’s optimal expected cost and entropy.
Claim 6.12 (Restriction and information telescope over pattern classes). The sum of stage costs
is at most A OPTE (S) + cE(S).
Proof. By their definitions,
                                           X                                   X
                    ∆ = OPTE (S) −                OPTE (A),       I = E(S) −         E(A).
                                           A∈Π                                 A∈Π

Let V be the nonsingleton recursive states and L their singleton leaves. As in Proposition 2.13,
every nonroot state occurs once as a child, so the internal terms cancel exactly:
                        X            X                  X X
                            ∆(V ) =       OPTE (V ) −            OPTE (A)
                         V ∈V              V ∈V                 V ∈V A∈ΠV
                                                          X
                                        = OPTE (S) −            OPTE (A),
                                                          A∈L
                          X                          X
                                 I(V ) = E(S) −           E(A).
                          V ∈V                      A∈L


                                                        62
Both leaf sums vanish. Each query belongs to one stage, with its original reach mass, so summing
the previous claim gives
                                       X               Claim 6.11
                        cE (T (S)) ≤          W (V )       ≤        A OPTE (S) + cE(S).
                                       V ∈V

Finally Lemma 2.12, proved by Claim 2.5, gives the binary-tree entropy lower bound E(S) ≤
ln 2 OPTE (S); substituting it yields
                                              Claim 2.5
                               cE (T (S))        ≤        (A + c ln 2) OPTE (S).

This completes the proposition and its multiplicative guarantee.

6.3   Calibration of the expected-depth bound
The preceding proof separates the algorithm from its numerical tuning. The calibration has two
tasks: make every symbolic credit valid, then show that the resulting restriction and entropy coeffi-
cients give the announced approximation factor. The checks below follow that order. The calibration
uses three fixed values:
                               3512863                    393931                  21317
                         β=            ,          δ=              ,       p∗ =          .            (16)
                                 106                      2500000                125000
All other coefficients are derived from these and from the fixed comparison distribution in Section A.
That distribution is used to prove the score bound; the algorithm minimizes its rational score over
the entire candidate family.
    Each parameter has a separate role: the growth rate sets the batch budgets, the balance threshold
bounds flat-state cap masses, and the tent parameter determines the rational credit for asking both
endpoints. The remaining numbers describe their proof analysis.

What must be checked. Claim 6.6 requires the entropy tent H2 (p∗ ) ≥ 1 − 2p∗ . The geometric
comparison in Proposition 6.3 also requires λβ ≥ νβ . The fixed distribution must then pass three
certificate checks. First, every rank coefficient except one must stay above the target threshold.
Next, the mean waiting length of the zero route must remain within its comparison bound. Finally,
the retained credit must cover the zero-route term. Set τ = 2190079/106 . The exact coefficient
construction (14) gives

            1.276476788627 < a1 < 1.276476788629 < τ,                      min at > 2.1900797 > τ.
                                                                          2≤t≤14

All first-batch cutoffs are at most six, k0 ≤ 3, k1 ≤ 12, and r ≤ 13. Thus the signed corrections are
constant for t ≥ 14; at = eβ (t) + κ on that entire tail. Its monotonicity reduces all later coefficients
to the single a14 check. Indeed, when t ≥ 14 all signed-correction indicators equal one and all
first-batch credit indicators equal zero, so

                     dq,r (u, t) = 2(k0 + k1 − q − r),              κ = 2E[k0 + k1 − q − r].

Since ⌊x⌋ ≤ x and ⌊βx⌋ ≤ βx, the integrand defining eβ is nonnegative. Thus at − a14 = eβ (t) −
eβ (14) ≥ 0 for every t ≥ 14: (14) cancels the common tail correction κ, and the integral over [14, t]
has nonnegative integrand.

   The all-negative route adds a separate waiting cost. We check its entire length because its mass

                           D0 (k) = E[q + r − k0 − k1 ] = −κ/2 = D0 (14).

follows. If an expression has enclosure [x, x], sums add endpoints, products take the minimum and

Both entropy denominators and B have strictly positive enclosures. The sign of the subtracted

                  A + max{ln 2/h(δ), B ln 2/σ(δ)} < 5.182975508794 < 5.182976.

The file expected-two-phase-check.py checks these enclosures with exact fractions. Its supple-
mentary tests verify 9,900 signed-phase identities and 1,650 modified-batch comparisons, including
early minimum-cover completion and full negative waiting invoices. They support the implementa-
tion audit; the stated claims and the exact fixed-table certificate establish the theorem.


7    Quasipolynomial approximations approaching two
The same construction works for both objectives. A graded schedule orders adjacent hull blocks
with only a small loss in their ranks, giving a leading factor close to two plus an additive penalty. We
then optimize a short initial part of the tree exactly; this lookahead absorbs the remaining penalty
into the multiplicative factor. The algorithm returns a completed tree, with its graded recursion
supplying each internal continuation.


                                                  66
7.1   Algorithm and the three supporting propositions
Given 0 < ε ≤ 1, use grade spacing λ = 1 + ε/2 and write a = 2λ. The only user-selected parameter
is ε. At the end of the proof, we choose the balance threshold and continuation coefficients to
satisfy the inductive inequalities; these feasible values yield the stated factor. GradeDP stores a
small number of cumulative rank budgets. Rollout compares each legal root query with stopping
lookahead and attaching the graded continuation, using the costs of the constructed trees.
    A nearly exact schedule must satisfy several early-rank requirements at once. The optima for
separate coverage budgets can have incompatible prefixes, so the graded dynamic program retains
their compatibility explicitly.

Proposition 7.1 (Graded interval scheduling). For ordinary interval demands with r nested rank
budgets at most B, GradeDP decides simultaneous feasibility and returns a schedule in (M +
1)O(r) (B + 1)O(r) time, where M is the number of candidates. Circular demands add enumeration
of their first candidate.
    For positive demands of weights w(I) and zero-pattern mass p0 , the same DP, with delay levels
0 and the distinct ⌊λj ⌋, returns an ordering whose joint delay cost
                                        X
                              F (Q) = 2     w(I)(firstQ (I) − 1) + 2p0 |Q|
                                         I

is at most λ times the minimum. It uses r = O(ε−1 log n) grades. For rational input weights,
the data-dependent DP entries are sums of weights times integer ranks, so their bit lengths remain
polynomial. The subtraction of one prices delay before the hitting block; the zero term prices the
complete negative route.

    Scheduling ranks is only part of identification: endpoint recovery and later recursive work still
cost queries. The next proposition combines them with restriction credit, leaving a small state
potential as the only additive penalty.
    Let BW (S) be the height and BE (S) the original-prior expected depth of the corresponding
graded continuation on state S. Both quantities include discovery, endpoint queries, and all recursive
child trees; the continuations return a leaf at singleton states.

Proposition 7.2 (Graded continuation bounds). There are fixed balance parameters for which the
two graded continuations, zero on singletons, satisfy

                                 BW (S) ≤ a OPTW (S) + 3 log2 |S|,                                (18)
                                  BE (S) ≤ a OPTE (S) + 4E(S).                                    (19)
                                                   −1
They use only supplied lines and run in (mn)O(ε log n) time, times a polynomial in the input bit
length. The expected-depth bound has no dependence on the smallest prior.

   To turn the continuation bound into a multiplicative guarantee, we optimize an initial prefix. We
charge those queries at their realized cost, and the resulting saving absorbs the remaining penalty.

Proposition 7.3 (Lookahead removes a bounded continuation penalty). Suppose a legal con-
tinuation on every restricted state S ′ obeys either BW (S ′ ) ≤ a OPTW (S ′ ) + LW , or BE (S ′ ) ≤
a OPTE (S ′ ) + LE w(S ′ ), with a > 1. Exact k-level lookahead is an a-approximation if, respectively,
k ≥ LW /(a − 1) or k ≥ LE /(a − 1). The continuation costs at singletons are zero.



                                                  67
 Algorithm 11: Rollout: exact initial-tree optimization with a baseline
   Input: State S, objective o, lookahead j, and legal baseline routine B.
   Output: The cheapest initial tree of at most j levels, completed by B.
 1 if |S| ≤ 1 then
 2     return its leaf
 3   if (S, o, j) is memoized then
 4       return the stored tree and cost
 5 Compute the actual baseline tree B(S) and retain it as the initial best choice
 6 if j > 0 then
 7     foreach supplied line ℓ splitting S do

10         Store a lower-cost choice and its two child trees

11   Memoize and return the best choice

   For Theorem 7.4, use Algorithm 13 with λ = 1 + ε/2 and k = min{n − 1, ⌈3⌈log2 n⌉/(1 + ε)⌉}.
This choice sets the lookahead cap for both objectives; the baseline completes every search branch
that reaches the cap.

Figure 5: Exact lookahead wrapped around the quasipolynomial baseline. The arrows denote calls
and the returned trees or costs. The baseline completes any branch that reaches its stopping depth.
Thus the logarithmic cap on j bounds the number of search transcripts while each returned tree still
completes identification. The proof compares this finite search with an optimal tree; the algorithm
evaluates only trees it has constructed.

Proof strategy.

     1. Retain several cumulative budgets together. Exact feasibility requires nested prefixes, so the graded
        DP stores their compatibility explicitly.
     2. Map an optimal witness path to adjacent hull blocks. A query already removed by restriction pays
        for its block delay; a count or entropy potential pays for endpoint recovery.
     3. Compare lookahead with the first levels of an optimal tree. Each paid prefix query replaces a query
        previously charged at factor a, which absorbs the continuation penalty.

                                                       68
Theorem 7.4. For every fixed ε > 0, maximum depth and arbitrary-prior expected depth have
supplied-query quasipolynomial (2 + ε) approximations. For 0 < ε ≤ 1, their time is
                                                      −1 log n)
                                            (mn)O(ε

times a polynomial in the input bit length.

Proof. Use the continuations of Proposition 7.2 with a = 2 + ε. Their penalties on any restricted
state are at most 3 log2 n and 4w(S ′ ) ln n, respectively: |S ′ | ≤ n bounds the count potential, and
entropy on |S ′ | hypotheses is at most ln |S ′ | per unit mass. Proposition 7.3 therefore removes both
penalties after O(log n) levels. Concretely, the final calibration permits

                          qn = ⌈log2 n⌉,      k = min{n − 1, ⌈3qn /(1 + ε)⌉}.

When the cap is n − 1, every proper identifying tree is available, so the result is exact. Otherwise
the lookahead guarantee applies. At depth i ≤ k, there are at most 2i answer transcripts, and each
transcript is generated by at most mi choices of supplied queries along its path. Summing these
bounds over i ≤ k gives (2m)O(k) lookahead states. Multiplying by the graded-continuation cost
preserves the stated time. For ε > 1, use the construction with ε = 1.

7.2     Proofs of the supporting propositions
7.2.1    Graded schedules retain compatible prefixes
Each grade prefix has two obligations: stay within its rank budget and hit every interval whose
deadline it represents. Counts enforce the first; the last selected position suffices to check the
second.

Claim 7.1 (Counts and last positions decide every interval deadline). For each grade prefix j, store
its selected count cj and last selected position pj . When a demand [l, q] ends, it has been hit within
that grade exactly when pj ≥ l.

Proof. Scan the candidates in spatial order. Selecting position q at grade i increments cj and
replaces pj by q for every j ≥ i; skipping changes neither. These are exactly the cumulative counts
and last positions. No selected position exceeds the scanned endpoint q, so pj ≥ l is equivalent
to a hit in [l, q]. Reject any count exceeding its budget. Sorting the selected candidates by grade
therefore meets every deadline.
    Conversely, write the cumulative budgets as bj and take any feasible comparison ordering. Drop
candidates whose ranks exceed the last budget; no required deadline hit is lost. Assign each re-
maining candidate v the grade

                                  grade(v) = min{j : rank(v) ≤ bj }.

The candidates with grade at most j are exactly those with original rank at most bj , so their count
is at most bj . Every demand with deadline j has one such candidate in its interval. At its right
endpoint the last selected position of that prefix is therefore at least its left endpoint. Thus all count
and hit checks pass, proving the converse. The recurrence is exact, with at most (M + 1)r (B + 1)r
states and r + 1 transitions per position. For circular demands, force the first candidate, delete
its hit demands and cut there. Its absolute rank consumes one unit of every cumulative capacity,
including the total-count cap.


                                                   69
    Independent prefix coverage would be insufficient. For example, [1, 2] and [2, 3] can both be hit
by rank one, while {1} and {3} can both be hit by rank two. The first requirement forces candidate
2 first, leaving too few ranks for the second. The DP stores exactly this compatibility information.
    Exact deadline feasibility must now become an approximation for delay. Charging the first
qualifying grade bounds each positive delay, while the final selected count also prices the complete
negative route.
Claim 7.2 (Rounded delays bound the joint objective). Charging a demand at its earliest selected
grade gives a λ approximation for F , including zero-pattern waiting.
Proof. Let d0 = 0 and let the positive dj be the distinct ⌊λj ⌋ up to a level covering n. Grade j
has capacity dj + 1. At the right endpoint of demand I, charge 2w(I)dj for the least j whose last
selected position lies in I. Its actual first-cover delay is at most dj . Keep the least accumulated
charge for each state, and add 2p0 clast at completion.
    For a comparison order, rank one uses grade zero. At rank t ≥ 2, the first positive level
at least t − 1 is smaller than λ(t − 1): the preceding unrounded power is less than the integer
t − 1. Duplicate-floor plateaus do not change this argument. These assigned grades respect every
cumulative capacity, so Claim 7.1 makes this graded comparison feasible. For each positive delay, its
rounded charge is at most λ times its original charge; the final selected count, and therefore the zero
waiting charge, is unchanged. Since that waiting charge is nonnegative and λ ≥ 1, the minimum
surrogate is consequently at most λF . Conversely, the actual delay of each returned demand is at
most its charged grade level, so its actual objective is no larger than the minimized surrogate. An
optimum uses at most the number of demands, after unproductive candidates are removed. For a
circular order the forced first candidate has zero delay, all capacities decrease by one, and the final
waiting charge is 2p0 (1 + clast ).

 Algorithm 12: GradeDP: cumulative-grade interval feasibility or weighted delay
   Input: Ordinary intervals; r capacities b0 < · · · < br−1 ; feasibility deadlines, or weights, delay
             levels dj = bj − 1, and zero-pattern mass p0 .
   Output: A feasible graded selection, or a minimum surrogate-delay selection.
 1 Initialize the state (c0 , . . . , cr−1 ; s0 , . . . , sr−1 ) to all zeros, with cost zero; all other states are
    unreachable
 2 for q = 1, . . . , M do
 3     From each reachable state, try skipping q or selecting it at grade i
 4     A grade-i selection increments cj and sets sj = q for all j ≥ i
 5     Reject any transition with cj > bj
 6     foreach interval I = [l, q] ending here do
 7          if mode is feasibility then
 8               Reject if sj < l for its deadline grade j
 9          else
10               Find the least j with sj ≥ l; reject if none
11               Add 2w(I)dj to this transition’s cost

12     Keep one feasible witness, or the minimum cost, for each resulting state
13 In weighted mode add 2p0 cr−1 to each final state’s cost
14 Reconstruct a winning selection and order it by grade
15 return the selection and schedule, or infeasible


    For circular demands enumerate the forced first candidate, remove its demands, cut there, and
reduce every capacity by one. Residual expected costs still use the original delay levels and add
2p0 (1+cr−1 ) at completion. In maximum-depth mode append a final grade with the total-count cap

                                                            70
if it is not already a capacity. Section 7 proves the state invariant and the (M + 1)O(r) (B + 1)O(r)
bound.

7.2.2   Restriction pays for a graded pattern stage
For the expected-depth calculations, use the pattern partition Π and abbreviate ∆ = ∆E (S, Π),
I = I(S, Π), w = w(S), and b = w − w(A0 ). Write J for the state-mass-weighted entropy of the
actual discovery transcript, and W for the expected cost of this stage’s queries, before attaching
the recursive child trees.
     A block rank must be translated back into real queries. The next claim combines the two
possible discovery exits with endpoint recovery; the second exit benefits from the cached negative
first endpoint.
Claim 7.3 (An adjacent block has a uniform endpoint allowance). If a class A first meets block k,
its discovery and endpoint queries use at most
                            2(k − 1) + zA + 5,       zA = ⌊log2 (δ|S|/|A|)⌋                         (20)
on each route. For expected depth, the analogous overhead is at most five times the nonzero-pattern
mass, plus the pattern information remaining after discovery.
Proof. At most 2(k − 1) endpoint queries precede the hitting block. A first endpoint positive costs
one query and has endpoint allowance zA + 4 by Proposition 4.3. A second endpoint positive costs
two queries but has a cached negative neighbor, reducing its allowance to zA + 2. Thus the two
total allowances are respectively
                            2(k − 1) + 1 + (zA + 4) = 2(k − 1) + zA + 5,
                            2(k − 1) + 2 + (zA + 2) = 2(k − 1) + zA + 4.
Their maximum is the stated common allowance. A block containing the free origin has one real
endpoint and obeys the first bound. For expected depth, the two endpoint-code overheads are four
on the first exit and two on the pinned second exit. Together with discovery this gives respectively
five and four queries beyond the block delay and the conditional entropy cost. Summing over positive
exits bounds this overhead by 5b. If P is the pattern label and Ddisc the discovery transcript, the
latter is a function of P : at this fixed state every discovery query is a hull test, and all choices and
stopping rules depend only on earlier hull answers. Hence H(Ddisc | P ) = 0, and the entropy chain
rule of Lemma 2.9 gives
                           wH(P | Ddisc ) = wH(P ) − wH(Ddisc ) = I − J.
Thus the remaining entropy invoice is (I − J)/ ln 2. The second exit’s smaller allowance is precisely
the negative-neighbor saving.
   For maximum depth, build all pattern-child codes first. For a trial height H, set
                                                             
                                       H − BW (A) − zA − 5
                              BA =                              .

Reject negative budgets. Round each budget down to a delay level, use block deadline B  bA + 1, and
cap the total selected blocks at ⌊H/2⌋. Enumerate the first block and apply GradeDP. The trial
range ends at ⌈(a + 3)(|S| − 1)⌉. Low-dimensional hulls use their direct extreme tests.
   The computed deadlines use actual child heights, so their feasibility requires care with integer
rounding. Restriction supplies the needed rank credit before the deadline is rounded down to a
grade.

                      H ∗ = ⌊aD + γ log2 s⌋ ≥ 2D + 2,       ⌊H ∗ /2⌋ ≥ D + 1.

Here the final step uses γuδ ≥ 5, so the count-potential drop pays for both extreme tests. The zero

   For expected depth, the same witness comparison is summed with the original hypothesis
weights. Discovery information then pays for the endpoint allowance, giving a local bound that
cancels through recursion.

   The maximum-depth trial range is 1 ≤ H ≤ ⌈(2λ + 3)(|S| − 1)⌉. The baseline selects its trial
from these explicit tests, while the optimum appears in the comparison argument.
   There are O(ε−1 log n) grades, with polynomially many counts and spatial positions. To com-
pute zA , compare 2z |A| with δ|S| and choose the largest admissible integer z; this locates the
grade directly, without evaluating a logarithm. All objectives are rational sums of actual costs.
Proper disjoint-child recursion has at most n − 1 stages. This proves the time and legality parts of
Proposition 7.2.

7.2.3   Paid prefix queries absorb continuation penalties
To analyze lookahead, compare with a prefix of an optimal tree. On every unfinished maximum-
depth route, the prefix has already consumed exactly the queries that create the saving against
factor a.

Claim 7.6 (The maximum-depth penalty is paid on every frontier path). An optimal height-D tree,
truncated at depth k, has continuation height at most D − k at each surviving frontier state.

Proof. If D < k, the whole optimal tree is a lookahead candidate. Otherwise each surviving frontier
state has optimal height at most D − k: the original optimal subtree is a feasible tree of that height.
The assumed continuation bound therefore completes its path at height at most

                         k + a(D − k) + LW = aD − (a − 1)k + LW ≤ aD.

The equality isolates the saving of a−1 per paid prefix query, and the inequality uses (a−1)k ≥ LW .
Earlier leaves are already within height D. Exact lookahead can only improve this candidate.

   The expected-depth comparison needs no common leaf depth. Only the mass still unfinished
matters: each such hypothesis has paid all prefix queries, so their total payment absorbs its contin-
uation penalty.

                                                   74
Claim 7.7 (Expected prefix cost pays for every surviving frontier mass). Let P be an optimal tree’s
expected cost through depth k, and Mk the mass still requiring continuation there. Then P ≥ kMk .

Proof. Every surviving hypothesis has paid for all k prefix queries. If R is the sum of the optimal
continuation costs, then P + R = OPTE (S) by partitioning the optimal tree’s queries at depth k.
At each frontier state its optimal restricted cost is no larger than that of the original subtree, by
Lemma 2.12. Thus completing the frontier with the baseline costs at most
                                                            P ≥kMk
    P + aR + LE Mk = a OPTE (S) − (a − 1)P + LE Mk            ≤      a OPTE (S) + [LE − (a − 1)k]Mk .

This is at most a OPTE (S) under the stated condition. It assumes neither a bound on the opti-
mal tree’s height nor a lower bound on its prior masses. These two claims prove the lookahead
proposition.

7.3    A simple calibration; no optimization is needed

Parameter calibration.
Fix the balance threshold at δ = 1/4, the count coefficient at γ = 3, and the entropy coefficient at c = 4.
These values affect only the continuation penalty and lookahead length, which disappear from the final
ratio. Thus no table of optimized constants is needed for this scheme.

    Indeed, (4/3)3 > 2 and 3 log2 4 = 6 > 5 verify (21). Here σ(1/4) = ln 4 and h(1/4) ≥ 12 ln 2.
The elementary bounds 2/3 ≤ ln 2 < 3/4 give 4h(1/4) > 1 and 4σ(1/4) > 5. The lower bound
           R 2the first term of the logarithm series in (17); the upper bound is the strict trapezoidal
follows from
bound for 1 dx/x. Finally 4 ln n < 3 log2 n, so the single rational lookahead cap in the theorem
covers both objectives. For singleton inputs, all procedures return the zero-query leaf.


8     Polynomial maximum depth with rank-subset scheduling
The algorithm combines exact scheduling inside a logarithmic rank cap with the priority fallback
of Section 4. It constructs one code for each pattern child and compares scheduling alternatives
against those codes’ actual heights. In the proof, an optimal tree certifies that at least one trial is
feasible.

8.1    The algorithm and its guarantee
Use the count threshold δ and potential coefficient γ of (2). For the original input size n, fix once
                                                       
                                                   γq
                         q = ⌈log2 n⌉,    R=              ,    L = R + 1.                         (22)
                                                  γ−1

The cap L is fixed from the original size n and inherited by every recursive child, so the rank-state
bound stays uniform throughout the tree. A singleton is terminal because its hypothesis is already
identified. At each larger state, we first ask a count-balanced supplied query when one exists and
recurse on its two answer classes. A flat state has no such query, so it proceeds to the scheduling
step below.
    At a flat state S, construct every full hull-pattern child once and record its actual height hA =
CW (A). Then try increasing integer heights H through ⌊(3 + γ)(|S| − 1)⌋; because the children
stay fixed, each trial reuses their codes and the first passing trial gives the smallest certified height.


                                                    75
Compare the priority fallback with exact adjacent-block scheduling. For the latter, candidates are
edges of the full normalized hull, with the origin kept as a free negative marker. Each positive
vertex arc therefore becomes a circular interval of edge blocks. Write
                                                                          
                                                           H − hA − zA − 5
                      zA = ⌊log2 (δ|S|/|A|)⌋,     BA =                       .
                                                                  2
Proposition 4.3 bounds the endpoint continuation by zA + 4 queries after the first positive answer;
the shared allowance zA + 5 includes the first positive query and also covers a pinned exit. Thus,
after reserving the child height and endpoint allowance, at most BA full blocks can precede the hit
block, since each uses at most two queries. This gives deadline BA + 1. The total cap ⌊H/2⌋ follows
for the same reason: every block costs at most two queries. Reject negative budgets; otherwise
assign block deadline BA + 1 and use the total cap

                                           min{L, ⌊H/2⌋}.                                         (23)

The scheduler receives these integer deadlines directly, so its feasibility checks match the route-
height bounds. An optional exact whole-exit decoder, proved safe below, may be included in the
same trial. Return a passing alternative at the least height, attach its endpoint decoders and stored
child codes, and contract constant queries. If the hull has dimension at most one, its two real
extreme tests already determine every pattern, so query them directly.

 Algorithm 14: HybridTree: general polynomial maximum depth
   Input: State S, original size n, fixed global cap L = R + 1, where q = ⌈log2 n⌉ and
            R = ⌊γq/(γ − 1)⌋, and the count parameters calibrated in Section 8.3.
   Output: A tree satisfying Theorem 8.1.
 1 Stop at a singleton; at a count-balanced state query a balanced line and recurse
 2 Compute HullData(S) and build each child’s hybrid code once
 3 Handle a low-dimensional hull directly; otherwise record child heights hA

 4 for H = 1, . . . , ⌊(3 + γ)(|S| − 1)⌋ do
 5     Try PriorityTrial with these stored children
 6     Set zA = ⌊log2 (δ|S|/|A|)⌋ and BA = ⌊(H − hA − zA − 5)/2⌋
 7     if all BA ≥ 0 then
 8         Try circular SlotDP on full-hull-edge demands with deadlines BA + 1 and slot cap
             min{L, ⌊H/2⌋}
 9     Optionally try whole-exit SlotDP using actual endpoint-continuation heights and the same
         total cap; test feasibility with these heights
10     if any alternative passes then
11          Attach its endpoint decoders and the stored child trees
12          Terminate the negative cover at A0 when present; omit that leaf otherwise
13          return a passing tree, contracting constant queries


   The global cap is inherited unchanged by every child. The priority fallback proves that a trial
always passes; the algorithm never tests the unknown condition OPTW (S) ≤ R.

Figure 6: At a flat state, the algorithm first computes the hull and children, then tests increasing
target heights using three schedule checks. The arrows carry the geometry, child heights, and
candidate schedules needed by those checks. The logarithmic cap L is fixed at the original root.
The priority fallback guarantees a passing trial by the stated height bound; the exact and whole-exit
checks can certify an earlier trial, and the algorithm selects the first trial accepted by its explicit
checks.




                                                   77
Proof strategy.

   1. Solve interval deadlines exactly by partitioning a small set of global query ranks (the positions
      assigned in the full schedule) between spatial left and right regions.
   2. Prove two recursive bounds simultaneously: the priority fallback works for every optimum; exact
      scheduling improves it when the optimum fits the global cap.
   3. At the root, a small optimum uses the stronger bound. A large optimum absorbs the fallback’s count
      potential. Calibrate the two count-credit inequalities only after these structural arguments.
   Spatial left and right regions may be queried in an interleaved order. The exact scheduler assigns
each region a subset of global query ranks; these positions preserve its deadlines while allowing the
two searches to interleave.

Proposition 8.2 (Exact rank-subset scheduler). For N interval demands on M ordered candidates,
with integer first-hit deadlines and at most L distinct selected candidates, feasibility and a schedule
can be found in O(M 3 3L + N M 2 ) time with O(M 2 2L ) states. Circular intervals add a factor M by
enumerating the first candidate.

    The exact scheduler’s short global cap makes its stronger bound available on small-optimum
states, while the priority fallback covers the remaining states. Proving both bounds together lets
the recursion use the strongest child estimate whose threshold survives restriction.

Proposition 8.3 (Two simultaneous recursive guarantees). At every recursive state S, with s = |S|
and D = OPTW (S),

                                                 CW (S) ≤ 3D + Φ(s),                                (24)
                               D≤R        =⇒     CW (S) ≤ 2D + Φ(s).                                (25)

The threshold R is global, so restriction preserves the second invariant’s inductive hypothesis in
every child.

   A feasible abstract schedule must still account for actual endpoint queries, stored child contin-
uations, and the negative route. The next statement connects those certificates to a finite legal
construction.

Proposition 8.4 (Executable trials and polynomial reconstruction). Every accepted trial has com-
plete height at most H, including its zero route when present. A passing trial is reached within
the stated range; geometry, scheduling, endpoint decoding, and reconstruction have polynomial bit
complexity. The optional whole-exit alternative preserves both guarantees in Proposition 8.3.

Proof of Theorem 8.1. At the root, if D ≤ R, (25) gives CW ≤ 2D+Φ(n) ≤ 2D+γ log2 n ≤ (2+γ)D.
The middle step removes the floor from Φ(n), while Claim 2.6 gives D ≥ log2 n; together these facts
bound the logarithmic potential by γD. If D > R, integrality gives D ≥ R + 1 > γq/(γ − 1). Since
log2 n ≤ q, this implies γ log2 n/D < γ − 1. Substituting this bound into (24) therefore gives

                             CW     γ log2 n
                                ≤3+          < 3 + (γ − 1) = 2 + γ.
                              D        D
Proposition 8.4 supplies the legal polynomial construction, and the calibration below gives its nu-
merical factor. Trial acceptance is determined by the constructed code; the proof uses D to show
that one of the checked trials meets the bound.


                                                  78
8.2   Technical proofs of the propositions
Proof of Proposition 8.2. The scheduler’s state separates spatial information from temporal infor-
mation. A spatial split removes the intervals it hits; absolute ranks preserve every deadline while
allowing the left and right searches to interleave in any order.
    Candidates are 1, . . . , M . A demand [a, b] of deadline d must contain a selected candidate
assigned a rank at most d. For region [l, r], let I(l, r) contain the original demands wholly inside it.
Define F (l, r, T ) for an available subset T ⊆ [L] of absolute ranks; unused ranks are allowed. The
key sufficiency question is which demands can still matter inside a region. Interval geometry makes
ancestor hits recognizable from the region alone, while the rank subset records its remaining time
slots.
Claim 8.1 (Contained intervals are exactly the unhit demands). The spatial region and the absolute
rank subset are sufficient state.

Proof. Each region is a connected component of the candidate line after its ancestor separator
points have been deleted. An original interval not contained in one such component must contain
a deleted separator: intervals contain every candidate between their endpoints. It was therefore
hit at an ancestor. Conversely, an interval contained in a remaining region contains none of those
separators and is unhit. Queries in other remaining regions cannot hit it either. Thus exactly I(l, r)
remains relevant in [l, r]. The subset T records when its future candidates may be queried in the
global order; two regions with the same number of queries can have different available ranks and
hence different deadline feasibility.

   Once that state is sufficient, its earliest available rank can be used for the next separator.
Partitioning the later ranks permits every interleaving of the left and right searches.
Claim 8.2 (The earliest available rank gives an exact recurrence). For nonempty I(l, r) and T ,
put t = min T . Reject if a contained deadline is less than t; otherwise accept precisely when some
p ∈ [l, r] and partition T \ {t} = TL ∪˙ TR satisfy

                                F (l, p − 1, TL )   and   F (p + 1, r, TR ).

Proof. By Claim 8.1, it suffices to check the contained demands. For sufficiency, place p at rank
t. Every contained demand containing p is hit at rank t, no later than its deadline because the
preliminary test checked every contained deadline. An interval not containing p is wholly in [l, p − 1]
or [p + 1, r]. The two accepted subproblems give distinct candidates with disjoint absolute rank
sets, so merging their selections by rank preserves all their deadlines and completes the state.
    For necessity, let a feasible state have first selected point p at rank t′ ∈ T , and put t = min T .
If t < t′ , then t is unused: every other selected point has rank greater than t′ . Assign p to t and
keep all other ranks fixed. If t = t′ , leave the schedule unchanged. Thus selected ranks remain
distinct, and advancing the first point cannot delay any first hit. No contained deadline can be
smaller than t, since no available rank would then meet it. Partition the other selected ranks by
whether their candidate lies left or right of p, and distribute unused ranks arbitrarily between the
two parts. Each unhit interval lies wholly on one side, so the corresponding subproblem is feasible.
This gives a partition of all T \ {t} as required. When the demand family is empty, the empty
selection is feasible; a nonempty family with T = ∅ is infeasible.

   The DP may leave rank gaps, which only postpone hits. Compressing those gaps produces an
executable linear schedule; choosing its first point similarly turns a circular instance into a linear
one.

                                                    79
Claim 8.3 (Reconstruction and circular cuts preserve feasibility). The recurrence has the stated
time and state bounds.

Proof. Sort selected points by their assigned ranks and remove unused slots; all hits accelerate.
Precompute existence and minimum deadline of contained demands in O(N M 2 ) time. There are
O(M 2 2L ) states, and for a mask of size k there are at most 2k−1 partitions after its minimum is
removed. The coarser total bound is
                                                   L  
                                 X                 X  L
                                         2|T | =              2k = (1 + 2)L = 3L .
                                                         k
                                T ⊆[L]             k=0

The first equality groups masks by cardinality, and the second is the binomial theorem. Hence all
masks contribute a total partition factor 3L . With O(M 2 ) spatial regions and at most M candidate
roots per region, the transitions take O(M 3 3L ) time, in addition to the O(N M 2 ) preprocessing.
Removing unused ranks sends every assigned rank to an earlier or equal schedule position, so no
deadline is missed.
     An empty circular demand family accepts immediately. Otherwise enumerate its first selected
point at rank one. Delete the intervals hit there and cut the candidate circle at that point; every
survivor becomes an ordinary interval. A surviving original deadline at most one is impossible. For
all other survivors subtract one from their deadline and use L − 1 remaining slots. Appending the
first point to any accepted linear schedule restores the original deadlines, and every feasible circular
schedule has one of the enumerated first points. The enumeration adds a factor M .



    Within an interval state, the earliest available rank must be assigned to one candidate p. Choos-
ing p divides the remaining candidates into left and right intervals, and the unused ranks must split
between those two subproblems. The recursion enumerates these partitions; memoization reuses
each interval-and-rank state.
 Algorithm 15: SlotDP: exact interval deadlines with a rank subset
   Input: Ordinary interval demands and deadlines; region [l, r]; available absolute ranks T ⊆ [L].
   Output: A feasible rank assignment, or infeasible.
 1 if no original demand is wholly contained in [l, r] then
 2     return the empty assignment
 3   if T = ∅ then
 4       return infeasible
 5   if this state is memoized then
 6       return its stored result
 7 t ← min T
 8 if a wholly contained demand has deadline < t then
 9     return infeasible
10 foreach p ∈ [l, r] and TL ⊆ T \ {t} do
11     TR ← T \ (TL ∪ {t})
12     Recurse on ([l, p − 1], TL ) and ([p + 1, r], TR )
13     if both assignments are feasible then
14         Memoize their union with (p, t) and return it

15   Memoize and return infeasible




                                                             80
    After the recursion succeeds, sort the selected candidates by rank and suppress unused ranks to
obtain the schedule. For circular demands, enumerate the first candidate and cut the circle there;
this converts the remaining demands to intervals, with one subtracted from their deadlines and the
slot cap to account for the forced first rank. In the optional complete-exit variant, first compute
both oriented decoder costs using Algorithm 4. Those costs replace the ordinary deadline precheck
by the local height tests of Section 8; the same rank partitions then handle the remaining scheduling
choices.

Proof of Proposition 8.3. Induct on s, with zero-cost singleton states, proving both bounds simul-
taneously. A balanced child A has OPTW (A) ≤ D and Φ(|A|) ≤ Φ(s) − 1, by restriction and
Proposition 4.1. For either coefficient c ∈ {2, 3}, its inductive route bound gives

                         1 + hA ≤ 1 + c OPTW (A) + Φ(|A|) ≤ cD + Φ(s).

The first inequality is the appropriate inductive hypothesis; in the second, restriction bounds the
optimal child height by D and the unit potential drop pays for the root query. When c = 2, D ≤ R
also gives OPTW (A) ≤ R, so the stronger child hypothesis is available. Thus a balanced query
preserves either bound. The priority alternative preserves the universal bound by Proposition 4.6;
smaller actual children only improve its budgets.
    For the stronger flat-state bound, assume D ≤ R and put H ∗ = 2D + Φ(s). All children meet
the same global threshold, so their stronger inductive bound is available. The stronger flat-state
proof has three parts: convert child-height credit into deadlines, fit a comparison cover inside both
caps, and translate its blocks into physical routes. The optimal witness is used only to establish
existence of a passing trial.
Claim 8.4 (Actual child heights give feasible comparison deadlines). Every nonzero class satisfies
BA + 1 ≥ tA at H ∗ .

Proof. Proposition 4.2 gives OPTW (A) ≤ D − (tA − 1). The actual stored height therefore satisfies
hA ≤ 2[D − (tA − 1)] + Φ(|A|). Substitution gives

                      H ∗ − hA − zA − 5 ≥ 2(tA − 1) + Φ(s) − Φ(|A|) − zA − 5
                                         ≥ 2(tA − 1),

The first inequality substitutes the stronger inductive child bound; the second uses the nonzero-class
count credit of Proposition 4.1, namely Φ(s) − Φ(|A|) ≥ zA + 5. Thus

                                   H ∗ − hA − zA − 5 ≥ 2(tA − 1).                                (26)

Dividing by two gives a lower bound by the integer tA − 1; taking a floor consequently preserves
it, so BA ≥ tA − 1 ≥ 0. Thus this comparison trial passes the nonnegative-budget check and has
deadline BA + 1 ≥ tA . This links an unknown optimal witness to deadlines computed from actual
stored codes.

    The comparison must satisfy each block deadline, the global rank cap, and the cap on the
negative route. A terminal nonzero singleton may require one appended block, which accounts for
the extra slot in the global cap.
Claim 8.5 (The comparison fits both total caps). Every class meets its deadline in a cover of at
most D + 1 edge blocks.



                                                 81
Proof. Map each witness-path query to a full-hull edge containing its supports. Enlarging a singleton
support only accelerates hits; delete repeated and unproductive blocks. If the path ends at an
uncovered nonzero singleton, append an incident edge at its supporting index r + 1, with r ≤ D. If
the zero class exists it is the terminal witness leaf and no append is needed. The comparison has
at most D blocks with a zero class and at most D + 1 otherwise, and it hits A by rank tA . Since
D ≤ R, this fits L = R + 1. Also s ≥ 2 gives log2 s ≥ 1, and γ > 2 gives Φ(s) = ⌊γ log2 s⌋ ≥ 2. As
D is an integer,             ∗                              
                              H            Φ(s)             Φ(s)
                                   = D+            =D+             ≥ D + 1.
                               2             2               2
Pulling the integer D through the floor gives the second equality, and Φ(s) ≥ 2 gives the final
inequality. Thus the comparison fits both the L-block rank limit and the cap 2⌊H ∗ /2⌋ on the
all-negative route, even when a terminal singleton block has been appended.

   An abstract edge block becomes at most two supplied queries. The second first-positive exit has
a cached negative neighbor and a cheaper decoder; accounting for this asymmetry yields one safe
block allowance.
Claim 8.6 (A feasible block trial bounds every physical route). Class A first hit at block k has
complete route height at most
                                   2(k − 1) + zA + 5 + hA .                                 (27)

Proof. The preceding k − 1 blocks use at most 2(k − 1) supplied queries. If the first endpoint of
block k is positive, discovery uses at most 2k − 1 queries. Proposition 4.3 then adds its zA + 4
endpoint allowance and the stored height hA , so the complete route is bounded by

                         (2k − 1) + (zA + 4) + hA = 2(k − 1) + zA + 5 + hA .

If the second endpoint gives the first positive answer, the first endpoint is a cached negative neighbor.
The pinned allowance in Proposition 4.3 therefore gives

                            2k + (zA + 2) + hA = 2(k − 1) + zA + 4 + hA ,

which is one query smaller than the stated common allowance. A block with one real endpoint obeys
the first bound; cached answers and the origin marker cost nothing. Put X = H ∗ − hA − zA − 5.
Since BA = ⌊X/2⌋ and k − 1 ≤ BA ,

            2(k − 1) + zA + 5 + hA ≤ 2⌊X/2⌋ + zA + 5 + hA ≤ X + zA + 5 + hA = H ∗ .

The last inequality is 2⌊X/2⌋ ≤ X. Thus either positive route fits the trial. The all-negative route
has at most 2⌊H ∗ /2⌋ ≤ H ∗ queries and identifies A0 if present; an absent zero leaf is omitted.

    Claims 8.4 and 8.5 provide a feasible comparison schedule. Hence Proposition 8.2 finds a passing
trial at H ∗ , and Claim 8.6 converts it into the required route-height bound. If the hull has dimension
at most one, its at most two extreme tests learn the pattern directly. For a nonzero class the stronger
child bound and Proposition 4.1 give 2 + hA ≤ 2D + Φ(s) − zA − 3 ≤ H ∗ , since zA ≥ 0. The all-
negative route also uses at most two tests, and H ∗ ≥ 2. The universal calculation is identical with
coefficient three. Both invariants follow by induction.




                                                   82
Proof of Proposition 8.4. The local route certificate and (27) prove the accepted-height tests. The
universal invariant supplies a passing integer trial. Indeed D ≤ s−1 follows from a proper identifying
tree, and log2 s ≤ s − 1 for integer s ≥ 1, so

                       3D + Φ(s) ≤ 3(s − 1) + ⌊γ(s − 1)⌋ = ⌊(3 + γ)(s − 1)⌋,

where the equality uses the integrality of 3(s − 1). The remaining obligations are running time and
reconstruction. The rank search is exponential only in the cap fixed at the root, while all recursive
stages retain explicit supplied queries and child codes.
Claim 8.7 (The global cap makes exact scheduling polynomial). All arithmetic and the complete
reconstructed tree have polynomial size.

Proof. For fixed γ, put α = γ/(γ − 1). For n ≥ 2, q = ⌈log2 n⌉ < log2 n + 1 and L = ⌊αq⌋ + 1 <
α log2 n + α + 1. For either b = 2 or b = 3, exponentiating gives

                                   bL < bα+1 bα log2 n = bα+1 nα log2 b .

The inequality uses monotonicity of bx for b > 1, and the equality follows from blog2 n = nlog2 b . The
constants are fixed, so both rank-mask costs are polynomial. The singleton input returns immedi-
ately. There are polynomially many trial heights and first blocks, and at most n − 1 nonterminal
recursive stages with disjoint child sets. To see this count, the recursive states form a rooted parti-
tion tree whose leaves are individual hypotheses. Each nonterminal state has at least two nonempty
children: balanced queries split properly, and a flat nonsingleton state cannot be one class because
every nonzero class has size less than δs and the zero class has size at most one. In a rooted tree
with n leaves and at least two children at each internal vertex, counting edges gives at most n − 1
internal vertices. The witness LP, hull incidence, and endpoint codes have polynomial bit complex-
ity. Every recursive child is smaller: balanced queries split properly, nonzero patterns have size
below δs, and A0 is a singleton. Compute q and zA from their defining power-of-two thresholds,
then set R = ⌊γq/(γ − 1)⌋. Priority weights have O(n log n) bits. Contracting constant queries
gives a proper tree with at most n leaves and n − 1 internal nodes.

    Fixed endpoint allowances make the deadline instance simple, but may overestimate a particular
exit. An exact rectangle decoder can inspect the realized patterns and their stored continuations.
It uses the same legal endpoint comparisons and therefore can only improve a trial.
Claim 8.8 (Exact whole-exit decoding cannot weaken a trial). The optional alternative is polyno-
mial under the same cap and accepts every fixed-allowance schedule.

Proof strategy.

   1. Use dynamic programming on endpoint-coordinate rectangles, and prove by induction on the realized
      classes that the recurrence gives the minimum continuation height.
   2. Show that unhit interval demands are exactly those contained in the current region. This makes
      each rectangle decoder independent of queries already resolved outside it.
   3. Compare the minimizing decoder with the explicit endpoint code used by a fixed-allowance schedule;
      its route bounds imply that the same schedule passes the more precise height test.
   4. Count the rectangles and threshold transitions, then include the rank-subset factor 3L .




                                                    83
Proof. After the legal cut, each class has one realized endpoint pair; let M be the number of hull
candidate positions. For a coordinate rectangle U , give a one-class leaf value hA and compute

                                                      1 + max{E(U ∩ ℓ− ), E(U ∩ ℓ+ )} ,
                                                                                      
            E(U ) =                min
                      proper supplied coordinate thresholds ℓ

ignoring empty outcomes. For an empty rectangle no decoder is needed. A rectangle containing only
class A has value hA ; otherwise two distinct endpoint pairs differ in some coordinate, and a legal
hull comparison between those coordinate values gives a proper threshold. Each proper threshold
sends its realized pairs to strictly smaller nonempty sets. Induction on the number of realized pairs
shows exactness: every permissible endpoint tree has such a first threshold and costs one plus the
larger continuation height, while attaching the two minimizing continuations attains that value. A
rectangle is specified by two boundaries in each coordinate, giving O(M 4 ) rectangles, with O(M )
thresholds to try in each. The leaf values include the stored class continuations. A pinned endpoint
is the same recurrence in one coordinate.
    At state ([l, r], T ), Claim 8.1 identifies the unhit demands as precisely the original intervals
contained in [l, r]. For candidate block p in either orientation, compute the whole decoder heights
E1 (l, r, p) and E2 (l, r, p) for its first-positive and second-first-positive cells. These cells depend only
on the region and block: an outside query cannot hit a contained interval, and a crossing interval
was already hit by an ancestor. At k = min T , replace the fixed-deadline check by

                       2(k − 1) + 1 + E1 (l, r, p) ≤ H,         2k + E2 (l, r, p) ≤ H,

omitting empty cells. Keep the rank partitions, circular first-block enumeration, and negative-route
cap. These prefixes conservatively include cached endpoints and the origin. For a cell containing
classes A, the explicit endpoint code with their stored continuations is a feasible rectangle decoder
                                         (4)                                                 (2)
tree. Its whole height is at most maxA (eA +hA ) on a first-positive cell and at most maxA (eA +hA )
on a pinned cell. Therefore the minimizing values E1 , E2 are no larger than these bounds. If each
fixed-allowance class route passes at block rank k, then respectively 2(k − 1) + 1 + E1 ≤ H and
2k + E2 ≤ H. Keeping the same candidate schedule and negative-route cap thus passes the whole-
exit trial as well. Precomputation and the rank-subset transitions are polynomial times 3L .
