9     Stronger algorithms for small caps
Consider instances with a distinguished hypothesis p and orient each supplied query so that its cap
is the answer side missing p. Let P be the other N hypotheses. These assumptions concern the
supplied queries themselves. At a recursive flat state, the common external witness serves as an
analysis aid, while p remains the distinguished hypothesis. The restricted algorithms below clarify
which small-cap constructions can support approximation hardness.




                                                         85
9.1      Exact maximum depth with bounded caps
Theorem 9.1. If every cap has size at most a fixed integer b, maximum depth is exactly solvable in
                                                  2
polynomial time. A running-time bound is mb−1 2O(b ) poly(n, m, L), where L is the input bit length.

Proof strategy.

      1. A minimum cap cover gives a lower bound K from the witness spine and a tree of height at most
         K + b − 1.
      2. Only the last b − 1 witness queries need to be guessed. Their caps contain at most b(b − 1) points,
         so guess their residual state and find the earlier cover exactly.
      3. Exact local subset tables verify every suffix exit. A feasible optimal tree supplies one of these guesses,
         proving exactness.


9.1.1      Supporting propositions and the algorithm
Let K be the minimum number of supplied caps covering P . For a target height D ∈ [K, K + b − 1],
let d0 = max{0, D − b + 1}. A suffix guess consists of at most D − d0 ordered witness queries and
the set R ⊆ P alive immediately before them.
    The witness path must eliminate every other hypothesis, while each positive exit contains only
a few hypotheses. Comparing these two requirements confines the optimum to a short interval.

Proposition 9.2 (The optimum lies in a short height interval). A minimum supplied-halfplane
cover is computable in polynomial time, and the optimal height lies in [K, K + b − 1].

   Early exits have enough remaining depth for any proper local code. The only delicate exits
occur near the end of the witness path; preserving their guessed residual set makes the earlier cover
independent.

Proposition 9.3 (Each suffix guess is exactly checkable). Guess R inside the suffix caps’ union. A
prefix cover of P \ R using caps disjoint from R, followed by the suffix, gives height at most D if its
cover size is at most d0 and every suffix first-hit group Aj satisfies j + h(Aj ) ≤ D − d0 , where j is
its first-hit position within the suffix and h is exact local identification height. These conditions are
decidable in polynomial time for fixed b.

   Exact checking is useful only if an optimum appears among the guesses. Cutting an optimal
witness path at the prescribed depth provides precisely the small suffix and residual set being
enumerated.

Proposition 9.4 (Enumeration is complete and polynomial). Every height-D tree provides an
                                                                   2
accepted suffix guess. There are O(mb−1 ) ordered suffixes and 2O(b ) candidate residual sets per
suffix. For b = 1, a minimum cover itself is exact.




                                                         86
 Algorithm 16: Exact maximum depth with a distinguished miss hypothesis and bounded
 caps
   Input: Distinguished hypothesis p; other hypotheses P ; supplied caps of size at most fixed b.
   Output: An exact minimum-height supplied-query tree.
 1 if P = ∅ then

 2     return the zero-query tree
 3 Compute the minimum supplied-halfplane cover size K of P
 4 for D = K, . . . , K + b − 1 do
 5     d0 ← max{0, D − b + 1}
 6     foreach ordered supplied-query suffix of length q ≤ D − d0 do
 7         foreach R contained in the union of its caps do
 8             if the suffix does not cover R then
 9                  continue
10            Find a minimum cover Q of P \ R using only caps disjoint from R
11            if |Q| > d0 or the cover is infeasible then
 12               continue
13            Compute the suffix’s first-hit groups Aj on R ∪ {p}
14            if some j + cW (ExactTree(Aj , W)) > D − d0 then
 15               continue
16            Execute Q followed by the suffix on the all-miss spine
17            Attach exact local supplied-query trees to all positive first-hit groups
18            return this tree


    The theorem’s height interval means the loop needs only the listed values of D. For each
value, the algorithm searches suffixes and residual sets; since a suffix has at most b − 1 queries and
each cap contains at most b points, its union contains at most b(b − 1) points. An empty suffix
therefore permits only R = ∅. The prefix step is a minimum supplied-halfplane cover, computed
by the polynomial algorithm cited in Theorem 9.1; when the instance has the special hull-interval
representation, its dedicated interval oracle performs the same step.

Proof of Theorem 9.1. For P = ∅, return the zero-query tree. Otherwise, Proposition 9.2 limits
the target heights. Proposition 9.3 proves that every accepted guess constructs a legal tree, and
Proposition 9.4 ensures acceptance at the optimum. Returning the first accepted height is exact;
the stated enumeration and polynomial cover and local optimizations give the running time.

9.1.2   Technical proofs of the height propositions
Proof of Proposition 9.2. Let K be the minimum number of supplied caps covering P . Minimum
planar halfplane cover is exactly solvable in polynomial time [14]. Separability ensures that the cover
exists. The path identifying p in any tree must cover P , so its height is at least K. Conversely,
query a minimum cover along the all-miss spine and identify each positive hit group locally. A hit
group has at most b members and therefore admits a proper identification tree of height at most
b − 1. This gives height at most K + b − 1. Only these b target heights need to be tested.


Proof of Proposition 9.3. For a target D ∈ [K, K + b − 1], put d0 = max{0, D − b + 1}. Guess an
ordered suffix of q ≤ D − d0 ≤ b − 1 supplied queries on the witness spine. Guess also the set R ⊆ P
remaining just before that suffix. Require that the suffix caps cover R; thus R is a subset of their



                                                    87
union and has size at most b(b − 1). For each guess, compute a minimum cap cover of P \ R using
only supplied caps disjoint from R, and require size at most d0 . An empty suffix permits R = ∅.
   The suffix must fit on both kinds of path: a positive exit needs its local code, and the all-
miss route must also terminate within the budget. Checking both prevents a hidden witness-path
overrun.
Claim 9.1 (Local suffix tests include the witness leaf). The exact checks verify every positive exit
and the full miss route.

Proof. Execute the guessed suffix on R ∪ {p}. For each nonempty first-hit group Aj at position j,
compute its exact local identification height h(Aj ) by subset Bellman DP on at most b hypotheses.
Require j + h(Aj ) ≤ D − d0 and q ≤ D − d0 . These tests verify the entire suffix tree, including
the witness leaf. The prefix cover takes at most d0 queries, preserves R on its all-miss route,
and its positive exits cost at most b − 1 additional queries. Thus its exits have height at most
d0 + b − 1 = D when d0 > 0; if d0 = 0, the prefix is empty. The equality here uses the positive
branch of d0 = max{0, D − b + 1}; no such equality is needed when the prefix has length zero. The
suffix starts no later than depth d0 , so an accepted guess constructs a tree of height at most D.




Proof of Proposition 9.4. For completeness, take an identifying tree of height at most D. If its
witness path ends before depth d0 , use an empty suffix and R = ∅; its witness queries cover P
within d0 . Otherwise take the remaining witness queries after depth d0 as the suffix, and the actual
nonwitness state there as R. The prefix caps are disjoint from R and cover P \ R. The suffix has at
most b − 1 queries and covers R; its positive subtrees satisfy the exact local-height tests. This guess
                                                               2
is accepted. There are O(mb−1 ) suffix descriptions and 2O(b ) candidate subsets per suffix, and all
remaining computations are polynomial for fixed b. For b = 1, a minimum cover itself is exact.

9.2   An EPTAS for weighted singleton and pair caps
Assume every cap has size one or two, all singleton caps for P are supplied, and the graph G
of two-point caps is a disjoint union of paths and cycles. The latter condition follows for planar
instances with all singleton caps: every nonwitness point is a convex-hull vertex, and a two-point
cap joins consecutive
           P          vertices. Let the positive raw weights be w0 for p and wv for v ∈ P , and put
Ω = w0 + v wv .
    For a matching M , replace each matched pair by a group of mass wu + wv and leave un-
matched vertices
           P       as singleton groups. Let J(M ) be the group multiset, k = NP− |M |, and
P (M ) =     uv∈M (wu + wv ). With group masses a1 ≥ · · · ≥ ak , write C(J) =          j jaj . The
matching cost is
                                 G(M ) = w0 k + C(J(M )) + P (M ).                              (28)

Theorem 9.5. For this weighted singleton/pair path-and-cycle class, expected depth has a deter-
                                            −3
ministic (1 + ε) approximation in time 2O(ε ) poly(n, L, log(1/ε)) for 0 < ε ≤ 1. The polynomial
exponent is independent of ε; this is an EPTAS.




                                                  88
Proof strategy.

   1. Delete one residue class of path and cycle edges. Averaging finds a near-optimal matching on
      bounded-size components.
   2. Enumerate components containing the heaviest vertices. Their complement has little mass, so only
      light components need rounding.
   3. Express the exact matching cost as a convex quadratic program on component configurations. Its
      fractional optimum lower-bounds the best integral invoice; the computed feasible solution has only
      an additive numerical optimization error.
   4. Independent configuration rounding adds only a controlled variance term. Conditional expectations
      make it deterministic.


9.2.1   Supporting propositions and the algorithm
Work with raw weights and put F ∗ = minM G(M ) = Ω OPTE . The graph, weights, group counts,
and count functions below describe the instance. The tuning controls are integers K ≥ 1, H ≥ 0
and an additive accuracy 0 < η ≤ 1, chosen only in the final calibration. Put B = 2K, the resulting
component-size bound. Take the H heaviest nonwitness vertices, or all of them if fewer than H
exist, and let Ωtail be the mass of the others.
    Before approximating matchings, we need to justify that they describe the full adaptive problem.
The witness spine supplies this link; the supplied singleton queries realize every resulting matching.

Proposition 9.6 (The matching cost formula is exact). Minimizing (28) is exactly the minimum
raw weighted depth, including arbitrary adaptive trees. Every matching is executable with supplied
singleton and pair queries.

    Two reductions make the remaining optimization manageable. Edge deletion bounds component
size at a small relative cost, while fixing heavy components leaves little mass exposed to rounding.

Proposition 9.7 (Residue deletion and heavy enumeration). Deleting edge numbers in one residue
modulo K leaves components of at most B vertices. Some residue has a surviving matching of cost
at most (1 + 1/K)F ∗ . Moreover, Ωtail ≤ 2F ∗ /(H + 1), and all matching choices on components
meeting a heavy vertex can be enumerated in at most 2BH trials.

   For a fixed heavy trial, enumerate the matching configurations c of each remaining, light com-
ponent i. Let kic be its group count, Pic its matched-pair mass, and zic (t) its number of groups of
mass at least t. Let Zf (t), kf , Pf be the P                                 P components. Fractional
                                              corresponding totals of the fixed
configuration weights satisfy xic ≥ 0 and c xic = 1. Set µ(t) = Zf (t) + ic xic zic (t) and minimize
                                             !
                                                                    1 ∞
                                  X                    X             Z
             Q(x) = w0 kf +           xic kic + Pf +      xic Pic +       (µ(t)2 + µ(t)) dt.      (29)
                                                                    2 0
                                 ic                    ic

    Group order couples choices made in different components. Counting groups above each mass
threshold captures this coupling in one quadratic objective, without guessing the final order.

Proposition 9.8 (An exact convex quadratic model). The displayed objective equals the matching
cost on integral configurations. Its minimum over the product of configuration simplices lower-
bounds the best integral invoice. It is a rational convex quadratic program with N 2O(B) variables
and O(N ) mass breakpoints. A feasible rational solution within raw additive error ηΩ is computable
with polynomial bit complexity and time polynomial in the model size, L, and log(1/η).

                                                  89
   Rounding combines the component choices into one matching. Only the light components fluc-
tuate, and their mass bounds the extra cost of turning the relaxation into a tree.

Proposition 9.9 (Deterministic rounding costs only light mass). From a feasible rational x, con-
ditional expectations produce a matching of cost at most Q(x) + (B/2)Ωtail in polynomial time in
the model size and bit length. If every trial uses a feasible rational x within ηΩ of its fractional
optimum, the cheapest tree over all residues and heavy trials has cost at most
                                                         
                                          1          B
                                      1+     +η+             F ∗.
                                          K        H +1


 Algorithm 17: Expected-depth EPTAS for weighted singleton/pair paths and cycles
   Input: Distinguished miss hypothesis, supplied singleton tests and pair graph G; positive raw
           weights; 0 < ε ≤ 1.
   Output: A deterministic (1 + ε) approximation.
 1 Use the controls K, H, η from Section 9.2.3; set B ← 2K
 2 if n = 1 then
 3     return the zero-query tree
 4 Find the H heaviest nonwitness vertices (or all of them if fewer exist)
 5 for r = 0, . . . , K − 1 do
 6     Delete edges numbered r modulo K on each path or cycle
 7     Enumerate each remaining component’s matching configurations
 8     foreach joint configuration on components meeting the heavy vertices do
 9         Fix these heavy configurations
10         Solve the light-component convex program (29) to raw additive accuracy ηΩ
11         foreach unfixed light component do
12              Fix a configuration minimizing the conditional expected invoice, using rational first and
                   second count moments
13          Form the matched-pair and singleton groups; sort them by decreasing mass
14          Query the supplied group tests on the miss spine, using one supplied singleton test to
             identify each pair hit
15          Retain the resulting tree and its exact expected cost

16   return the cheapest retained tree

    Deleting one edge residue bounds the component size, and enumerating the heavy components
leaves a light-instance relaxation. The conditional-expectation step then fixes each remaining com-
ponent while preserving the invoice bound, so every retained candidate is an actual matching and
hence yields a legal tree. Propositions 9.7–9.9 prove the component bound, heavy enumeration,
convex relaxation, and rounding guarantee. Proposition 9.6 then converts the rounded matching
into its exact tree cost. The fractional solution guides the rounding, while the returned tree comes
from an actual matching.

Proof of Theorem 9.5. For n = 1, return the exact zero-query tree. For n ≥ 2, choose K, H, η by
Section 9.2.3. Proposition 9.7 provides a good residue and an enumerated heavy part of its near-
optimal matching. Propositions 9.8 and 9.9 return cost at most (1 + ε)F ∗ . Proposition 9.6 turns
the matching into a legal supplied-query tree. Divide by Ω to obtain the stated expected-depth
ratio. The calibration gives BH = O(ε−3 ); the other stages have polynomial exponent independent
of ε.



                                                     90
9.2.2   Technical proofs of the EPTAS propositions
Proof of Proposition 9.6. Follow the witness in an arbitrary proper tree. Its first-hit groups are
disjoint singletons or supplied pairs, so the pair groups form a matching M and the number of
groups is k = N − |M |. A supplied pair query first hitting only one vertex can be replaced here
P that vertex’s supplied singleton test. The witness contributes w0 k, group positions contribute
by
   j jaj , and each pair exit needs exactly one further supplied singleton query, contributing its full
pair mass. This is the displayed invoice before sorting.
     For adjacent group masses a < b, putting b first reduces their contribution by [ja + (j + 1)b] −
[jb + (j + 1)a] = b − a > 0. Thus nonincreasing order minimizes the position term, giving C(J(M )).
Conversely, query any matching’s disjoint pairs and unmatched singletons in that order, and attach
one supplied singleton query at each pair exit. This attains w0 k + C(J(M )) + P (M ), proving
exactness for arbitrary trees.


Proof of Proposition 9.7. We first control the cost of simplifying the graph, then control the mass
left for rounding. For graph simplification, compare each residue with the same optimal matching
by splitting its deleted pairs.
Claim 9.2 (One residue loses at most a 1/K fraction). Splitting the removed matched edges leaves
a legal near-optimal matching.

Proof. Number edges consecutively on each path or cycle. For each common residue r ∈ {0, . . . , K −
1}, delete edges numbered r modulo K. A path component between consecutive deleted edges has
at most K vertices, as do its end components. On a cycle, all components except possibly the one
crossing the numbering cut have the same bound. The crossing component joins two end portions,
each with fewer than K edges, and has at most 2K = B vertices. If a residue deletes no edge of a
cycle, that cycle has fewer than K edges, so is already smaller than B.
    Take an optimal matching, with sortedP group masses a1 , . . . , ak . For a matched pair of weights
a ≥ b at position j, put Rj = w0 + l>j al . Its original weighted depth contribution is (j +
1)(a + b), including the local pair query. After replacing it by two consecutive singleton queries the
contribution is ja + (j + 1)b, a change of −a. Every later group and the witness are delayed by one
query, a change of Rj . Thus the net change is exactly Rj − a.
    For simultaneous splits, each earlier split delays all points of a later group by one regardless
of whether that group is also split. The local change −a is likewise independent of the group’s
shifted position. The net changes therefore add. Sorting the new singleton and pair groups can
only improve the temporary schedule. Since the Rj are positive,

                     X               k
                                     X                    k
                                                          X
                              Rj ≤         Rj = kw0 +           (l − 1)al ≤ kw0 + C(J) ≤ F ∗ .
                  j matched          j=1                  l=1

The first inequality adds the remaining nonnegative Rj terms. For the equality, group l contributes
to exactly l − 1 earlier tails. Replacing l − 1 by l gives the next inequality, and the last discards the
nonnegative pair-continuation term from the exact invoice of Proposition 9.6. Each matched edge
belongs to exactly one residue trial. Averaging the temporary schedules over the K residues gives
cost at most

on the summed tails. At least one trial meets this average bound, and sorting its groups produces
a surviving matching of no larger cost.


   A component can be small in vertex count yet carry a heavy vertex, raising the rounding cost.
Enumerating the heavy part removes this risk, while the rank lower bound controls the remaining
mass.
Claim 9.3 (Heavy components leave a low-mass rounding problem). The rank lower bound controls
all unfixed vertex mass.

Proof. Order individual nonwitness weights as w(1) ≥ · · · ≥ w(N ) . A first-hit group contains at
most two vertices, so at most 2j vertices can leave by spine position j. Consequently the sorted
list of first-hit positions is componentwise at least ⌈1/2⌉, . . . , ⌈N/2⌉. Pairing larger weights with
smaller positions minimizes their weighted sum by adjacent exchanges. Ignoring local continuation
and witness costs therefore gives

The first inequality drops the nonnegative contributions of the heaviest H vertices, and the second
applies the rank lower bound uniformly to every remaining index. Thus the tail mass is bounded
without assuming any lower bound on an individual weight. For each residue, enumerate all match-
ing configurations in components containing one of the H heaviest vertices. There are at most H
such components. A path or cycle on at most B vertices has at most B edges, so has at most 2B
edge subsets and hence at most that many matching configurations. The joint number of choices
is at most (2B )H = 2BH . This enumeration is independent of n in its exponent. The remaining
components’ total vertex mass is at most Ωtail . If H ≥ N , all components are fixed and there is no
rounding stage.

singleton and available-pair masses, at most N + |E(G)| = O(N ) rational values. Include zero

Proof of Proposition 9.9. We separate the rounding analysis from its deterministic implementation.
The fractional objective records the mean counts; independent rounding changes only their quadratic
contribution, which can be charged to light vertex mass.

    An expected-cost bound still needs an individual matching that attains it. Fixing one component
at a time preserves that bound and keeps all intermediate computations rational and explicit.
Claim 9.5 (Exact conditional expectations give a supplied tree). One component at a time can be
fixed without increasing the expected invoice.

Proof. Suppose some components have already been fixed. For the next component, the current
conditional expected invoice is the weighted average of the conditional expected invoices obtained
by fixing each configuration, with weights xic summing to one. At least one configuration of positive
probability therefore has value no larger than the current expectation. Choose a minimum such
value and repeat.
    Compute each value exactly from the fixed counts and the remaining independent first and
second count moments at the same O(n) breakpoints. The identity for E[Z(t)2 ] remains valid with
the fixed counts incorporated into Zf (t). All interval lengths, probabilities, moments, and linear
terms are rational, with polynomial bit length; the number of configurations and breakpoints is the
model size. At the end no randomness remains, so the actual matching cost is at most the initial
expectation. Sorting its final groups yields a supplied-query tree by Proposition 9.6.


    For the good residue, enumeration contains the heavy part of an optimum surviving matching.
The fractional optimum is no greater than that matching’s invoice. In a nontrivial instance every
hypothesis has depth at least one, so F ∗ ≥ Ω. For the good residue and heavy trial, the computed
feasible point thus satisfies
                               Prop. 9.8             Prop. 9.7
                        Q(x)      ≤        Q∗ + ηΩ      ≤        (1 + 1/K)F ∗ + ηF ∗ .

Claim 9.2 bounds the surviving comparison invoice, while F ∗ ≥ Ω converts the numerical error to
a relative one. Adding Claim 9.4, with the tail estimate of Claim 9.3, gives cost at most
                                                                           
                             ∗       ∗     B      ∗         1          B
                  (1 + 1/K)F + ηF +             F = 1+         +η+            F ∗.
                                         H +1               K        H +1

The algorithm takes the cheapest trial, so it meets this same bound.

9.2.3   Calibration of the EPTAS controls

Parameter calibration.
For 0 < ε ≤ 1, choose

                        K = ⌈3/ε⌉,         B = 2K,          H = ⌈3B/ε⌉,       η = ε/3.
                                                                                                   −3
Then 1/K + η + B/(H + 1) ≤ ε. Also B = O(ε−1 ) and H = O(ε−2 ), so heavy enumeration costs 2O(ε ) .
Configuration enumeration, convex optimization, and conditional expectations keep a polynomial exponent
independent of ε.



                                                       94
    The ceiling choices give K ≥ 3/ε and H + 1 ≥ 3B/ε, hence 1/K ≤ ε/3 and B/(H + 1) ≤ ε/3.
Together with η = ε/3 this proves the calibrated ratio. For 0 < ε ≤ 1, K = O(ε−1 ), so B = O(ε−1 )
and H = O(B/ε) = O(ε−2 ); consequently BH = O(ε−3 ) as used in the enumeration bound.
    All-singleton availability is essential both for the invoice’s converse and for splitting removed
pairs. The EPTAS includes the weighted pair-path hardness instances below, and its exponential
dependence on 1/ε is compatible with strong NP-completeness. This subclass therefore cannot
establish APX-hardness unless P = NP.


10      A PTAS for uniform shallow caps in convex position
The bounded-cap restriction also permits an approximation scheme for uniform expected depth
when the hypotheses are in convex position. The construction first clears short gaps in their cyclic
order. Every remaining query then acts inside one bounded-size block. A count-vector dynamic
program retains all choices of how to interleave queries from these blocks. Uniform weights make
blocks with the same local test family interchangeable.
Theorem 10.1. Suppose H = P ∪ {p} is in strictly convex position, p is a distinguished hypothesis,
and every supplied side missing p contains at most a fixed number b ≥ 1 of hypotheses. Under the
uniform prior, expected depth admits a polynomial-time approximation scheme. Every query in the
returned tree is supplied, and the result permits input families with no singleton caps.
    For 0 < ε ≤ 1, the returned cost is at most (1 + ε) OPTE . For the block length ℓ chosen in the
final calibration, put db,ℓ = 2(b+1)ℓ . One running-time bound is f (b, ℓ)(n + 1)db,ℓ poly(n, m, L), where
L is the input bit length and f depends only on b, ℓ. The polynomial exponent depends on b and ε;
the guarantee is a PTAS.

Proof strategy.

     1. Clear short gaps in the convex cyclic order using supplied caps containing marked points. A cap
        cannot reach two remaining blocks.
     2. Describe each small block by its supplied interval masks. Equal point weights make blocks with the
        same type and live mask interchangeable, so only their multiplicities need to be stored.
     3. Optimize all block interleavings by an exact count-vector DP, using exact supplied-query codes on
        positive branches.
     4. Charge the clearing prefix against the witness-spine rank lower bound. Large enough blocks make
        that charge at most ε times the optimum.


10.1     Supporting propositions and the algorithm
                                    P
Write N = |P | = n − 1, G(T ) = h∈H dT (h), and OPTG = minT G(T ) = n OPTE . We use a
positive integer block length ℓ ≥ max{2b, b(b−1)} and choose its value in the final calibration. When
N < ℓ, exact subset Bellman optimization solves the instance. Assume henceforth that N ≥ ℓ.
   The purpose of clearing is to make every surviving query local. Short gaps suffice because a
shallow cap cannot cross a fully cleared gap and still reach points on both sides.
Proposition 10.2 (Clearing gaps isolates small residual blocks). Partition the convex cyclic order
into pieces of length ℓ and mark the first b points of each piece, or the whole piece if shorter. The
number r of marked points satisfies
                                                          2bN
                                           r ≤ b⌈N/ℓ⌉ ≤       .                                       (30)
                                                           ℓ

                                                    95
A legal supplied-query prefix clears all marked points in at most r spine queries and has total raw
contribution
                            Gprefix ≤ r(N + 1) + b(b − 1)r ≤ 2r(N + 1).                        (31)
Every remaining cap acts inside one residual block of size at most ℓ.

    The global state records each block’s available splits and live positions. Uniform weights then
let us forget a block’s identity.

Proposition 10.3 (Block types retain all continuation information). A residual block’s type is its
size and its family FT of nonempty supplied interval masks. There are at most 2bℓ types and at
most db,ℓ pairs (T, R) of a type and a nonempty live mask. Under uniform weights, blocks with the
same (T, R) can be exchanged without changing the optimum continuation cost. Each mask retains
a realizing query for every physical block of its type.
                                                                     P
    Let cT,R count blocks in local state (T, R), and put W (c) = 1 + T,R cT,R |R|, including the dis-
tinguished hypothesis. For each type, use Algorithm 1 to compute the exact raw local identification
cost gT (A) for every subset A using its supplied masks; gT (A) = 0 for |A| ≤ 1. The positive branch
has no distinguished witness. Let eT,R be a unit count vector, with eT,∅ = 0.
    The algorithm must still choose which block to query next. The count vector preserves these
choices, while the local tables handle the positive exits that no longer interact with other blocks.

Proposition 10.4 (The residual count-vector recurrence is exact). The optimum residual raw cost
is
                F (0) = 0,
                                                                         
                F (c) = W (c) +    min        gT (A) + F c − eT,R + eT,R\A .               (32)
                                   cT,R >0, q∈FT
                                     A=R∩q̸=∅

The DP has at most (N +1)db,ℓ states, polynomial bit complexity, and a supplied-query reconstruction
realizing its value. The recurrence includes every adaptive interleaving of the blocks.

    Clearing buys exact residual optimization at an extra prefix cost. The witness path already
incurs substantial rank cost when caps are small, so this prefix can be charged against the full
optimum.

Proposition 10.5 (The clearing charge is small relative to the optimum). The exact residual
optimum is at most OPTG , and

                                                    96
 Algorithm 18: Uniform expected-depth PTAS for bounded convex-position caps
   Input: Uniform hypotheses P ∪ {p} in convex position; supplied caps missing p, each of size at
           most fixed b; 0 < ε ≤ 1.
   Output: A supplied-query tree of expected cost at most (1 + ε) OPTE .
 1 N ← |P |; use ℓ from Section 10.3
 2 if N < ℓ then
 3     return ExactTree(P ∪ {p}, E) with raw unit weights
 4 Partition the convex cyclic order of P into pieces of length ℓ; mark their first min{b, piece size}
    points as D
 5 U ← P ; start an empty miss-spine prefix
 6 while D ∩ U ̸= ∅ do
 7    Choose x ∈ D ∩ U and a supplied cap q containing x
 8    A←U ∩q
 9    Append q to the prefix; attach a legal local code for A of height at most |A| − 1 on its hit side
10    U ← U \ A; continue on the miss side
11 Intersect the unmarked pieces with U to obtain the residual blocks
12 Store each physical block’s map from its masks to realizing supplied queries; group identical mask
    families as types T and compute every local cost gT (A) by ExactTree
13 Compute the initial count vector c of the full residual block masks
14 Evaluate (32), memoizing minimizing transitions
15 Backtrack from c: select a physical block in the chosen state, query that block’s stored realizing
    line, and translate the local positive code using the same block’s query map
16 Attach the reconstructed continuation to the prefix’s miss leaf
17 return the resulting tree




Proof of Theorem 10.1. Choose ℓ by Section 10.3. If N < ℓ, exact subset optimization takes time
depending only on b, ε, apart from polynomial input processing. Otherwise, Proposition 10.2 con-
structs the legal prefix, and Propositions 10.3 and 10.4 give an exact supplied-query residual tree
with the stated time bound. Proposition 10.5 and the calibration yield Galg ≤ (1+ε) OPTG . Divide
by n to obtain the expected-depth ratio. No step introduces an unsupplied singleton test.

10.2    Technical proofs of the supporting propositions
Proof of Proposition 10.2. There are two separate obligations: show that the marked gaps isolate
the surviving blocks, and clear those gaps using only supplied queries. We begin with the geometric
separation property.
Claim 10.1 (Marked gaps prevent caps from crossing blocks). Every residual cap meets at most
one unmarked block.

Proof. Each cap is a cyclic interval in the convex order of P : a line crosses the boundary of its
convex polygon at most twice. Partition that order into consecutive pieces of length ℓ, with a
possibly shorter last piece. Mark the first b points of each piece, or the whole piece when its size is
at most b. Let D be the marked set and r = |D|. There are ⌈N/ℓ⌉ pieces and each contributes at
most b marked points. Since N/ℓ ≥ 1,

                                  r ≤ b⌈N/ℓ⌉ ≤ b(N/ℓ + 1) ≤ 2bN/ℓ,

which proves (30). The middle inequality uses ⌈x⌉ ≤ x + 1, and the last uses N/ℓ ≥ 1 to absorb
the additional piece. The unmarked portions are blocks of at most ℓ points. Between neighboring
nonempty blocks, including across the cyclic wraparound, there are at least b marked points. A

                                                    97
cyclic cap of size at most b cannot meet two such blocks, because it would also contain an entire
intervening gap and hence at least b + 2 points.


   Supplied caps containing the remaining marked points clear them legally; removing additional
unmarked points can only shrink the residual blocks.
Claim 10.2 (Supplied queries clear the marked set with controlled cost). No private or arbitrary
singleton query is required by the prefix.

Proof. While a marked point x remains on the miss spine, query any supplied cap containing x. Such
a cap exists because the supplied tests distinguish x from p. Identify its first-hit group by a proper
local supplied-query tree, and continue on the miss side. Separation gives a local height at most
|A| − 1 ≤ b − 1 for each hit group A: repeatedly query a line separating two remaining hypotheses.
Each spine query clears a new marked point, so at most r are used. Additional unmarked points
may leave through these exits. The residual portions of the blocks still have size at most ℓ, and
Claim 10.1 therefore ensures that every residual cap still lies in one block: clearing extra points
cannot create a new hit across a marked gap.
    Each spine node queries at most N + 1 live hypotheses, so at most r such nodes contribute
r(N + 1) to the raw sum of depths. Its disjoint positive exits contain at most br points in total,
each with at most b − 1 additional local queries. Therefore

                                   Gprefix ≤ r(N + 1) + br(b − 1).

Since b(b − 1) ≤ ℓ ≤ N , the second term is at most rN ≤ r(N + 1). This gives Gprefix ≤ 2r(N + 1),
as required.




Proof of Proposition 10.3. Relabel the points of each residual block by their positions. Its type T
is its size together with the family FT of all distinct nonempty supplied-query masks on that block.
No cap can wrap around a block’s ends, since it would contain an entire marked gap. Deleting
additional points preserves interval order. Thus every mask is an ordinary interval of at most b
positions. For each possible left endpoint there are at most b possible interval lengths. Padding to
ℓ positions therefore gives at most bℓ masks, hence at most 2bℓ subsets of masks that could form
a family. The union of a family’s masks is its full block, since every point is separated from p, so
the family also determines the block size. A local state (T, R) records a nonempty live mask R in a
block of type T . For each type there are at most 2ℓ − 1 nonempty live masks, so the total number
of local states is at most 2bℓ (2ℓ − 1) ≤ 2(b+1)ℓ = db,ℓ , using 2ℓ − 1 ≤ 2ℓ .
     Two blocks of the same type have identical available splits after relabelling positions. Store a
realizing supplied query separately for each physical block and mask, so every chosen split uses a
line valid on that block. Each realizing query misses all other residual blocks. Uniform costs depend
only on their live cardinalities, so exchanging blocks of the same (T, R) preserves every continuation
cost. The count vector therefore retains all needed information.


Proof of Proposition 10.4. Exactness requires both directions: every adaptive tree must obey the
recurrence, and each recurrence choice must have a legal supplied query realization. Locality supplies
the common reason for both. Any query that is constant on the current live hypotheses can be


                                                 98
deleted: every hypothesis continues to the same child, and removing the query shortens each of
their depths by one. Thus we may suppress such queries and analyze a nonconstant root query.
Claim 10.3 (Every nonconstant adaptive query gives one recurrence transition). A positive branch
is local; the miss branch changes only one block state.

Proof. By Proposition 10.2, a nonconstant query hits only one residual block. A query at state c
contributes one unit of depth for each live hypothesis, hence raw cost W (c). Its hit group lies in one
block with local state (T, R) and equals A = R ∩ q ̸= ∅. The miss branch retains p and all other
blocks, and changes this block to R \ A. The two branch costs are bounded below by gT (A) and
F (c − eT,R + eT,R\A ) respectively. Thus every adaptive tree has cost at least the recurrence value,
by induction on the number of live nonwitness points.
    Conversely, for a minimizing transition choose a physical block in state (T, R), which exists
because cT,R > 0, and its stored supplied query realizing q. The query misses every other residual
block and is proper because its hit group is nonempty and p is on the miss side. Attach the exact
local code of cost gT (A) and the inductively reconstructed miss continuation. Their total cost is
W (c) + gT (A) + F (c − eT,R + eT,R\A ), attaining the recurrence. Its live nonwitness count decreases
by |A| ≥ 1, so the induction and the DP are acyclic. The choice of block at every transition remains
unrestricted, covering all adaptive interleavings. When no live block remains, only p remains and
F (0) = 0.


    Once these transitions are exact, the remaining issue is their representation. Counting inter-
changeable block states gives the size bound, while backtracking retains the physical queries needed
for one output tree.
Claim 10.4 (The compressed state space has the stated bit complexity). Only counts, supplied
masks, and integral costs are needed.

Proof. There are at most db,ℓ count coordinates, each between zero and N , so the enclosing count
cube has at most (N + 1)db,ℓ states. For a coordinate with positive count, there are at most bℓ
supplied masks to try. Each local Bellman table concerns at most 2ℓ subsets; these bounds depend
only on b, ℓ. Constructing the type and physical-query maps requires polynomial input processing.
    Every reachable state contains at most n actual hypotheses. A proper identifying tree for it
has height at most n − 1, giving raw cost at most n(n − 1). All weights are one, so the DP costs
are integers with O(log n) bits on reachable states. Storing a minimizing transition and a physical
realizing query at each used state suffices for backtracking; no list of all adaptive trees is stored.




Proof of Proposition 10.5. Restrict an optimal full tree to the residual hypotheses, suppressing
constant-answer nodes. Lemma 2.12 shows that this can only decrease depths; Proposition 10.4
therefore makes the exact residual cost at most OPTG . For the lower bound, order the nonwitness
hypotheses by their first-hit position on the witness spine. The first j queries can remove at most
bj of them, so the ith hypothesis in this order has first-hit position at least ⌈i/b⌉ and leaf depth at
least that large. Ignoring the nonnegative witness and continuation costs yields
                                       N               N
                                       X               X             N (N + 1)
                             OPTG ≥          ⌈i/b⌉ ≥         i/b =             .
                                                                        2b
                                       i=1             i=1



                                                   99
The second inequality uses ⌈i/b⌉ ≥ i/b term by term, and the equality sums the first N integers.
Write Gres for the exact residual cost. The restriction argument gives Gres ≤ OPTG , while the
prefix estimate gives Galg ≤ 2r(N + 1) + Gres . Consequently

                                Galg − OPTG Prop. 10.2 2r(N + 1)
                                                ≤
                                   OPTG                  OPTG
                                                  2r(N + 1)         4br
                                            ≤                   =
                                               N (N + 1)/(2b)       N
                                            Eq. 30 4b 2bN    8b 2
                                              ≤            =      .
                                                   N ℓ        ℓ
The first inequality uses residual optimality, the second the rank lower bound, and the last the
marked-point count. Dividing by n proves the expected-depth guarantee.

10.3    Calibration of the block length

Parameter calibration.
For 0 < ε ≤ 1, choose                                      2 
                                                            8b
                                     ℓ = max 2b, b(b − 1),        .
                                                             ε
The first two bounds ensure gap separation and the prefix estimate; the last gives 8b2 /ℓ ≤ ε. For fixed b, ε,
ℓ and db,ℓ are constants, yielding the claimed PTAS time.

    The proof uses only the cyclic-interval property of the caps. In particular it also applies when
the nonwitness points alone are in convex position. For fixed b, a constant-factor hardness reduction
through uniform one-layer shallow-cap instances would contradict this PTAS unless P = NP. A
construction based on bounded-size caps must therefore prove interactions beyond this cyclic-interval
subclass to establish such a gap.


11     Randomized per-hypothesis depth and optimal mixing
The previous algorithms return one tree. For a fixed prior, expected depth averages that tree’s
depths over hypotheses; deterministic maximum depth protects the slowest hypothesis in that same
tree. We now consider a separate extension: preprocessing returns a small collection of legal trees
and probabilities for choosing among them. At the start of each complete identification run, sample
one tree and execute it to identify the hidden hypothesis. Each run follows that tree from start to
finish, and every sampled tree identifies every hypothesis correctly.
    The hidden hypothesis is fixed before this random choice. Different trees may favor different
hypotheses, so we ask that each hypothesis have small expected depth over the choice of tree. For
a distribution µ over legal supplied-query trees, define

                             R(µ) = max ET ∼µ dT (h),         R∗ = min R(µ).                              (34)
                                      h∈H                             µ

We call this the Las Vegas per-hypothesis depth objective: correctness is certain, while the query
count depends on the random tree. For each fixed hypothesis, the objective first averages over trees
and then takes the maximum across hypotheses. A hypothesis may have a long path in one sampled
tree and still meet the bound when other trees give it shorter paths.



                                                     100
    For fixed-prior expected cost and expected maximum depth, randomization leaves the optimum
unchanged: each mixture averages a single-tree objective. Explicitly, for a fixed prior π, linearity
gives
              X                      X                               
          min     πh Eµ dT (h) = min   πh dT (h),  min Eµ max dT (h) = min max dT (h).
          µ                      T                      µ       h              T   h
              h                        h

Each mixture cost is a convex combination of the corresponding single-tree costs, so it is at least
their minimum; choosing a minimizing tree with probability one attains equality in both cases. The
different order of expectation and maximum in (34) is what makes mixing useful here.

A strict planar separation. Take the three hypotheses ht = (t, t2 ), t = 0, 1, 2, and supply the
three lines
                          4y − 8t0 x + 4t20 − 1 = 0, t0 = 0, 1, 2.
Each line’s negative side contains only its own point: its expression there is −1, and its value at
another point is at least 3. Asking a hypothesis’s singleton line first identifies it in one query; the
other two hypotheses require a second query. Thus choosing the first line trades one hypothesis’s
depth against the others:

                        hypothesis isolated by the first line h0 h1 h2
                                         h0                   1  2  2
                                         h1                   2  1  2
                                         h2                   2  2  1
                        uniform mixture’s expected depth 5/3 5/3 5/3

These are all possible proper depth vectors. Every individual tree has maximum depth 2, but
sampling the favored hypothesis uniformly gives every fixed hypothesis expected depth 5/3. Every
tree has uniform-prior expected depth at least 5/3, so R∗ = 5/3 exactly. All coordinates and line
coefficients are integers, and every hypothesis lies strictly off the supplied lines. The gain comes
from distributing the short paths across runs while each run follows one complete path.

11.1    One algorithm and its supporting propositions
Let n = |H| ≥ 2 and L = n − 1. Contracting queries constant on their current state makes every
legal identification tree proper: at each internal node, its query separates the hypotheses reaching
that node into two nonempty sets. Every such tree has n leaves and depths at most L. Write T for
the finite family of these trees and
                                    X
                          cπ (T ) =    πh dT (h),    OPTE (π) = min cπ (T ).
                                                                    T ∈T
                                     h∈H

We use the full family T to define the comparison optimum, while the algorithm stores the trees
                                                P mass to some hypotheses; every tree in T still
it constructs. A comparison prior may assign zero
identifies all of H. Write ∆(H) = {π : πh ≥ 0,    h∈H πh = 1} for the probability simplex.
    The connection to the previous sections is constructive. An expected-depth algorithm can build
a tree favoring a chosen prior. By repeatedly emphasizing hypotheses that earlier trees treated
slowly, we use that algorithm to build a collection that protects all hypotheses. We represent the
available expected-depth algorithm by an oracle AE with a fixed rational guarantee α ≥ 1:

               cπ (AE (π)) ≤ α OPTE (π) for every strictly positive rational prior π.             (35)

                                                 101
The polynomial or quasipolynomial expected-depth construction supplies this oracle. A rational
learning rate 0 < η ≤ 1 and a round count K will be calibrated at the end for the desired tolerance.
    Initialize equal positive weights. In each round normalize them to a prior, construct its oracle
tree, and increase a hypothesis’s weight according to its depth in that tree. This directs later trees
toward hypotheses that earlier trees identify slowly. In the three-point example, after a tree with
depths (1, 2, 2) the last two hypotheses receive larger multipliers, encouraging later trees to give
them the short path. Any tree meeting the oracle’s weighted-cost guarantee yields the bound,
regardless of which hypothesis it favors. If η = p/q in lowest terms, use the integer update
                                                              
                                       wh ← wh qL + p dTt (h) .                                   (36)

Its common factor qL cancels on normalization. This is the usual multiplicative-weights update:
hypotheses that are deeper in the current tree receive larger weight and therefore receive more
emphasis in later oracle calls. The update and its game interpretation use the multiplicative-
weights framework of Freund and Schapire and of Arora, Hazan, and Kale [17, 16]. Here the losses
are hypothesis depths scaled by L, and each oracle tree becomes a column of the restricted mixture
LP. The next proposition derives the regret bound for this particular choice of losses.
    The weight updates decide which trees to construct. A second step decides how often to use
each constructed tree. After constructing T1 , . . . , TK , optimize their actual mixture by the restricted
master LP

Each inequality is the expected depth of one fixed hypothesis, and r is their common upper bound.
The LP has one variable for each constructed tree and represents the full family T implicitly. Retain
an optimal basic mixture, which will use at most n trees. For each identification run, sample one of
those trees from its exact rational probabilities and execute it. The proof first bounds the uniform
mixture of all K generated trees. Since that mixture is a feasible LP solution, optimization preserves
its bound and may remove most stored trees.

   To compare this construction with the best possible randomized strategy, we first identify its
benchmark. The hardest prior is the dual of the tree-mixture problem: even an ideal mixture
must pay the optimal expected cost of that prior. Since the dual benchmark is an expected-depth
optimum for some prior, an expected-depth approximation oracle can bound its value in every
round.
Proposition 11.1 (The comparison game and its entropy lower bound). The optimum in (34) also
covers adaptive zero-error randomized algorithms that terminate almost surely on every hypothesis.
It satisfies
                                 R∗ = max OPTE (π) ≥ log2 n.                                 (38)

The dual maximum ranges over the full simplex and may assign zero mass; Algorithm 19 supplies
strictly positive priors to the expected-depth oracle.
   The benchmark bounds the oracle’s cost on every round’s prior. The regret proposition turns
these changing weighted averages into a bound for each hypothesis: a repeatedly slow hypothesis

                                                   103
gains influence until the oracle’s weighted cost reflects its depth. The resulting average-depth bound
is for the uniform distribution over generated trees. Since the restricted master LP also considers
this mixture, its optimized maximum per-hypothesis cost is no greater.

let πt be the normalized weights before round t. The uniform mixture of the generated trees satisfies

    The uniform mixture in the regret bound may contain many trees. The master LP keeps its
guarantee while producing a concise strategy. The next proposition also ensures that the repeated
priors and the final random choice can be implemented exactly.
Proposition 11.3 (Exact rational construction and sparse optimal mixing). Every iterative prior
is strictly positive and rational, with encoding length polynomial in n, K and the encoding length
of η. The LP (37) is polynomial in size and has an optimal basic solution supported on at most
n trees. Its value is at most R(µunif ). Its probabilities have polynomial encoding length and admit
exact sampling with an expected polynomial number of fair random bits. Every sampled query run
is correct and uses at most n − 1 supplied tests.

Proof strategy.

   1. View hypotheses and legal trees as the two sides of a finite game. Its hardest prior lower-bounds
      every randomized strategy.
   2. Increase the weight of hypotheses with large current depths. The potential comparison bounds each
      hypothesis’s average depth by the oracle’s average weighted cost.
   3. Optimize the generated mixture by a small LP. The entropy lower bound makes the regret term a
      multiplicative tolerance; a basic solution leaves at most n trees to sample.
    Together, these statements transfer the oracle’s approximation coefficient to the new objective,
with a controllable tolerance. The extra work constructs and mixes trees; the query procedures
inside each tree remain those of the expected-depth oracle.

mixture of at most n legal trees. If the oracle is polynomial-time in its rational input, preprocessing
is polynomial in the input size and 1/ε. For a singleton, return its leaf with zero queries.

                                                    104
11.2     Proofs of the supporting propositions
11.2.1    The hardest prior is the dual comparison object
The game interpretation rests on two facts. First, fixing the random seed turns the policy into
a proper tree, and contracting constant queries can only shorten its paths. Averaging over seeds
therefore gives a tree mixture whose expected depth on each hypothesis is at most the algorithm’s.
Second, the mixture optimum equals the expected-depth cost of its hardest prior. We prove these
in order, then use the resulting lower bound to absorb the weight-update error.
Claim 11.1 (Random seeds reduce to a finite proper-tree mixture). Any zero-error randomized
algorithm terminating almost surely on each hypothesis induces a mixture over T whose expected
depth on each hypothesis is at most that of the algorithm.
Proof. Condition on a complete random seed. Since H is finite, outside a probability-zero union
of events the resulting deterministic policy terminates correctly on every hypothesis. The union of
its n finite paths is a finite identification tree. Delete unreachable branches and contract queries
constant on their current states. Every retained query splits a state properly, so the resulting tree
has n leaves, n − 1 internal nodes, and maximum depth at most L. The finite supplied query family
admits only finitely many such labeled trees. Pushing the seed distribution onto these trees gives
the required mixture. Conversely, sampling any such mixture gives a legal zero-error strategy.
    After fixing the seed, every strategy is a proper legal tree. The finite family of these trees
defines a mixture LP for the analysis; its dual assigns mass to hypotheses, giving precisely the prior
needed by the expected-depth oracle. The algorithm constructs a polynomial-size subset of this
finite family, then optimizes the mixture over those trees.
Claim 11.2 (Finite LP duality identifies the hardest prior). The finite-tree mixture optimum equals
maxπ∈∆(H) minT ∈T cπ (T ).

Proof strategy.
We derive the dual by assigning one multiplier to each hypothesis constraint and one to mixture normal-
ization. The Lagrangian gives the dual constraints directly; weak duality is then explicit, and finite LP
strong duality completes the equality.

11.2.2   A weight potential bounds every hypothesis’s depth
Consider a hypothesis that remains slow in many generated trees. Its weight must grow quickly. The
sum of all weights, however, grows according to the oracle’s cost on the current prior. Comparing
these two growth rates bounds every hypothesis’s average depth by the oracle’s average weighted
cost. Scaling depths by L lets the same calculation apply to every proper tree.

1 + η ≤ 2. Therefore η/ ln(1 + η) ≤ 1 + η and 1/ ln(1 + η) ≤ 2/η. Substituting separately into the
nonnegative mean-cost and error terms gives
                                                   1 X             2L ln n
                             R(µunif ) ≤ (1 + η)       cπt (Tt ) +         ,
                                                   K t              ηK

as claimed.

                                                     107
11.2.3   Integer updates and a basic LP solution give a finite strategy
We analyze the updates with logarithmic potentials and implement them using positive integer
weights; normalization gives the exact rational priors supplied to the oracle. A basic solution of
the master LP bounds the number of stored trees and the bit length of their probabilities. Exact
sampling then completes the construction and preserves the query guarantee.

Claim 11.6 (All oracle inputs have strictly positive rational weights). The updates (36) preserve
positivity and have polynomial encoding length.

Proof. The initial weights are one. Every update factor is a positive integer at most (p + q)L.
After K rounds, each weight therefore has O(K log((p + q)L) + 1) bits. Normalization adds at most
⌈log2 n⌉ bits to the denominator. All priors are strictly positive, so every call meets the oracle’s
hypothesis. The generated proper trees have O(nK) total nodes; their depth matrix consists of
integers at most L.

   All generated trees can therefore be stored in a polynomial-size depth matrix. The LP has
one column per generated tree and n hypothesis-depth constraints; these constraints determine the
worst expected depth. An optimal basic solution uses this structure to retain a small support.

Claim 11.7 (An optimal basic mixture uses at most n trees). The LP (37) has an optimal basic
solution with at most n positive tree probabilities and polynomial-bit rational coefficients.

Proof strategy.
Put the LP in standard form and use positivity of the objective variable to bound the number of tree
probabilities in a basic solution. Then bound the basis determinants to control the bit length of those
probabilities.

Proof. Its uniform mixture is feasible. Replacing every depth inequality by
                               X
                                   µt dTt (h) + sh − r = 0,  sh ≥ 0,
                                 t

gives standard form with n + 1 equalities and nonnegative variables µt , sh , r. Because n ≥ 2, every
proper tree has depth at least one on every hypothesis. Since the probabilities sum to one, each
depth inequality therefore forces r ≥ 1, so the variable r is positive in every feasible solution. A
basic solution has at most n + 1 positive variables, leaving at most n for tree probabilities. If some
equalities are dependent, the basis dimension decreases and the same support bound remains valid.
A finite optimal basic solution exists, and its integer basis matrix has entries of absolute value at
most L. For completeness, let b ≤ n + 1 be the basis size. The basis matrix√ B is nonsingular and
integer, so | det B| ≥ 1. Each of its columns has Euclidean norm at most b L. Hadamard’s bound
and Cramer’s rule therefore give
                                  √                         √                  det Bi
                  1 ≤ | det B| ≤ ( b L)b ,    | det Bi | ≤ ( b L)b ,    xi =          .
                                                                               det B
The bound on Bi also holds because the right-hand side has entries zero or one, and L ≥ 1. Each
numerator and denominator thus needs O(b(log b+log L)+1) bits. In particular the tree probabilities
have polynomial encoding length. Rational LP algorithms, followed by exact basis extraction on
the optimal face, find such an optimal basic solution in polynomial time [12]. The uniform mixture
is feasible, so the optimized value is at most R(µunif ).


                                                 108
    The sparse LP solution assigns probabilities to the retained trees. Each run samples one tree
and executes it from root to leaf. Its rational probabilities allow an exact draw using fair bits, so
the execution preserves the zero-error query guarantee.

Claim 11.8 (The compressed mixture is exactly sampleable). The optimized rational mixture gives
a legal zero-error execution with at most L queries and expected polynomial sampling cost.

Proof strategy.
Clear the rational probabilities to obtain integer interval lengths. Rejection sampling produces a uniform
integer in their common range, which selects a tree with exactly its LP probability and finite expected bit
cost.

Proof. By Claim 11.7, take a common denominator D for its at most n polynomial-bit probabilities;
                                                                                                Qs
               P is polynomial. Explicitly, if their denominators are b1 , . . . , bs , take D = i=1 bi ;
its encoding length
then log2 D = i log2 P bi is polynomial. Writing the probabilities as ai /D gives nonnegative integer
interval lengths with i ai = D. Draw a uniform integer from {0, . . . , D − 1} by fair bits and
rejection, then use the cumulative integer probability intervals. Rejection makes every accepted
integer equiprobable, so an interval of length ai has probability ai /D. If D = 1, select the sole
supported tree deterministically. Otherwise each attempt uses ⌈log2 D⌉ bits and is accepted with
probability greater than 1/2, so the expected number of attempts is less than two: with b = ⌈log2 D⌉,
each b-bit draw is uniform on {0, . . . , 2b − 1}, and D/2b > 1/2. Conditioning on a draw below D
makes it uniform on the desired range. The sampled tree identifies every hypothesis and has depth
at most L. This completes the compression proposition.

   The two optimization viewpoints also give a useful stopping certificate. The restricted master
LP supplies an upper bound on R∗ because its mixture is a feasible strategy, while

                                                  cπt (Tt )
                                            max             ≤ R∗
                                              t      α
supplies a lower bound by (35) and (38). Their ratio is an instance-specific certificate. Stop early
when it meets the desired factor; otherwise, the fixed-round guarantee still applies.

11.3    Calibration and the strongest resulting guarantees
The preceding proof separates the oracle’s approximation loss from the loss due to finitely many
weight-update rounds. Split the desired tolerance equally between these two terms; the following
choices make that split explicit.

    Using a stronger expected-depth oracle preserves the prior updates and mixture LP while im-
