and reducing the fraction can only decrease their magnitudes. A proper identifying tree has n − 1
internal nodes and is a polynomial certificate. Evaluate each supplied answer and sum the leaf
depths to verify its rational expected cost in polynomial time.

14.2.3   Calibration of the cloud spacing

Parameter calibration.
Choose Q = 4 maxi wi . Every cloud has width less than Q/4; the half-anchor boundaries of (51) therefore
miss all points and select exactly the stated clouds. Since Q ≤ 4n and N ≤ n, every abscissa is O(n2 ).
Squaring it bounds the ordinates by O(n4 ), and the formulas for both query types bound all integer
coefficients by O(n4 ).

Remark 14.9 (Limits of the reduction). The cloud identity adds the same nonnegative internal cost
∆ to every matching. Let Tw be the matching returned by the weighted EPTAS and let Tu be its
lifted tree on the uniform cloud instance. The exact cost identity gives

 c(Tu ) = c(Tw ) + ∆ ≤ (1 + ε) OPTweighted +∆ ≤ (1 + ε)(OPTweighted +∆) = (1 + ε) OPTuniform .

Thus the lift preserves the EPTAS as well as exact hardness. A constant-factor inapproximability
result would need a different construction.

                                                  140
15      Conclusion
The table collects the approximation factors proved for the two depth objectives and the two an-
swer models. Here γ is the calibrated count-potential coefficient from Section 8.3, and ρtwo is the
calibrated expected-depth factor from Section 6.3.

     Objective                               Polynomial time             Quasipolynomial time
     Maximum depth, binary answers           2 + γ < 4.464966            2+ε
     Maximum depth, on-line answers          4 + 2γ < 8.929932           4+ε
     Expected depth, binary answers and      ρtwo < 5.182976             2+ε
     arbitrary priors
     Expected depth, on-line answers and     2ρtwo < 10.365952           4+ε
     arbitrary priors

    The common-witness normalization is the structural step shared by both algorithms. In a flat
state, it turns each supplied test into an opposite cap and represents that cap by at most two
adjacent vertices of a convex hull. For each hypothesis, the positive hull answers form a circular
interval. This representation keeps the query family fixed and gives the supplied tests an interval
view.
    The two objectives use this shared structure in different ways. To obtain the maximum-depth
guarantee, we follow a common witness through an optimal tree. Restricting that tree to a pattern
class removes the queries on the witness prefix, so that prefix gives the class a deadline. The adjacent-
support lemma reduces these deadlines to singleton and pair blocks, which the scheduler orders.
Endpoint decoding then uses cached negative answers to preserve the remaining height credit. When
there are logarithmically many discovery slots, an exact Cartesian-tree dynamic program improves
the schedule; the global cap and priority fallback handle the other cases. Together these steps give
a factor below 4.464966.
    For expected depth, the algorithm first learns each full hull pattern and then recurses within its
class. Restricting an optimal tree to a class removes queries whose answers are already determined,
and that saved cost helps pay for the first positive hull answer. The answers also reveal information
about the pattern, which shortens the endpoint code. Exact integer-budget coverage over adjacent
hull blocks selects when each pattern is found, while the all-negative waiting charge accounts for the
zero-pattern route. The algorithm chooses its first two budgets, geometric shift, and endpoint mode
jointly. Signed first-cover corrections then account for each change in a class’s first-hit batch. These
restriction and information credits telescope over the recursive tree, so each stage cost is charged
once.
    The quasipolynomial algorithms store compatible discovery grades. This lets them handle all
deadlines together. Their internal bounds have leading coefficient arbitrarily close to two and an
additive count or entropy potential; exact logarithmic-depth lookahead absorbs that potential and
gives the final (2 + ε) ratio. The on-line-answer entries follow from the same factor-two simulation,
using the binary guarantee with parameter ε/2. The algorithms run in quasipolynomial time.
    For randomized per-hypothesis depth, the adversary chooses the hidden hypothesis before sam-
pling the random tree. Multiplicative weights then constructs trees for a sequence of positive ra-
tional priors. A restricted mixture LP combines these trees to minimize the largest per-hypothesis
expected depth. Proposition 11.1 identifies the hardest prior as the comparison benchmark, and
Theorem 11.4 shows that the weight updates preserve the expected-depth oracle’s approximation
factor up to an arbitrary tolerance. The LP compresses the resulting strategy to at most n trees.



                                                  141
Since this objective differs from deterministic maximum depth, the LP value and its comparison
priors provide a separate, instance-specific approximation certificate.
    The small-cap results also delimit possible hardness constructions. With a distinguished all-miss
hypothesis and fixed cap size, maximum depth is exactly solvable in polynomial time. For expected
depth, the weighted singleton/pair path-and-cycle subclass with all singleton tests has an EPTAS.
Consequently, APX-hardness for the current weighted pair-path reduction would imply P = NP.
A broader uniform subclass also has a PTAS: fixed-size caps in convex position with an all-miss
hypothesis, even when singleton tests are absent. Clearing short gaps permits exact optimization
of the remaining block interleavings, and the prefix cost stays a small fraction of the full optimum.
A constant-factor hardness construction must therefore use instances outside this uniform fixed-cap
class.
    The hardness reductions show that strong NP-completeness persists even when all hypotheses lie
on a parabola and every query is a supplied line. The expected-depth reduction applies to arbitrary
positive priors and to uniform priors: replacing each weight by a uniform cloud adds a fixed internal
identification cost to every matching. Several approximation questions remain: whether constant-
factor inapproximability holds, whether the polynomial factors can be lowered, and whether the
quasipolynomial factors can fall below two.


A     Fixed certificate for the two initial batches
The local proof uses a comparison distribution to show that some candidate schedule has enough
combined first-hit and mode-saving credit while preserving the all-negative waiting bound. This
distribution certifies existence; the algorithm searches candidate schedules and minimizes their
rational score. We list the complete fixed distribution here, making the calibration reproducible
directly from the stated data.
    This table specifies the proof distribution in Theorem 6.5. The distribution is independent of
the input. The shift has density 1/(u ln β) on [1, β). An entry (q, r; L) selects that initial-budget
variant and one first-batch saving minorant. Here r = 0 means budgets q, k2 , k3 , . . ., and r > 0
means q, r, k2 , k3 , . . .. All entries use

                                Aq = Mq − δwq,      Cq = c∗ Mq − d∗ δwq.

A mixture θ(q, r; L) + (1 − θ)(q ′ , r′ ; L′ ) is conditional on that row’s shift range. It compares two
actual variants; it never adds two savings from the same schedule.
    Read the certificate in two stages: the small table supplies the conditional mixing weights, and
the row tables select which variants to compare on each shift range. A row with one option assigns
it all conditional mass. The twelve rational mixing weights are listed as integer numerators with

                          ∆µ = 3d∗ P1 ,
                                                             
                       ∆D0 (k) = P1 31{3<k} − 1{1<k} − 31{3<k} = −P1 1{k>1} .

                                                                    144
In particular, its rank-one contribution is P1 (c∗ − 2), its contributions at ranks two and three are
P1 (c∗ + 2), and every later contribution is 2P1 . Rows with two options use the same calculation for
each option and multiply the results by its conditional weight before adding. For example, row 2
contributes P2 [θ1 (c∗ − 2) + (1 − θ1 )(1 − 3)] to a1 and P2 [3d∗ θ1 + 4(1 − θ1 )] to µ. Each execution
chooses one valid alternative. Consequently, the expected row contribution is the weighted average
of the credit from each alternative.
